Measure theory via Locales
Georg Lehner

Abstract
. We present an approach to measure theory using the theory of locales. This includes concrete constructions of measure algebras associated to Radon measures, such as the Lebesgue measure on ℝ n \displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n} , via Grothendieck topologies constructed from valuations, that circumvent the classical approach via σ \displaystyle\sigma -algebras. As an application we obtain a functorial construction of the induced measure μ ∗ \displaystyle\mu_{*} on the locale of sublocales 𝔖 ​ 𝔩 ​ ( X ) \displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(X) of a Hausdorff space X \displaystyle X equipped with a Radon measure μ \displaystyle\mu , which in particular shows that μ ∗ \displaystyle\mu_{*} is invariant under measure-preserving homeomorphisms. We furthermore give a construction of the measurable locale associated to a smooth manifold, functorial in submersions, as well as comparison results to classical measure theory.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 28A60; Secondary: 28C15, 18F10, 18F70

Robert Hooke,
Micrographia
, 1665, Public domain, via Wikimedia Commons

1.
Introduction

A common mode of thought in mathematical circles is that measure and probability theory belong to the domain of analysis, and that categorical or structuralist approaches to the subject miss the point. Consider, for example, the MathOverflow question titled “Is there an introduction to probability theory from a structuralist/categorical perspective?”
[
htta
]
, to which the second most upvoted answer, with 133 votes at the time of this writing, is:

In the spirit of this answer to a different question, I’ll offer a contrarian answer.
How to understand probability theory from a structuralist perspective:

Don’t

To put it less provocatively, what I really mean is that probabilists don’t think about
probability theory that way, which is why they don’t write their introductory books
that way. The reason probabilists don’t think that way is that probability theory is
not about probability spaces. Probability theory is about families of random variables. (Mark Meckes
[
htt
]
)

While we think that the sentiment expressed in this answer is well-intentioned - separating formalism from what truly matters is indeed crucial - we also think that the general willingness to accept the traditional
\displaystyle\sigma
-algebra-based approach, with all its paradoxes and exceptions, as a necessary evil has hindered the development of potential connections between measure theory and areas not closely related to analysis. It has also led to the quiet acceptance of paradoxes such as those due to Vitali and Banach–Tarski. Let us be more specific about what a “good” theory of measure should be capable of:

(1)

The central formal objects representing the many examples used in mathematical practice should be directly constructible from geometric or combinatorial data, with constructions explicit enough to permit direct arguments.

(2)

The notion of almost-everywhere equivalence should be built into the formalism so that handling it becomes automatic. Objects that agree classically up to almost-everywhere equivalence should be intrinsically identical.

(3)

The central formal objects should organise into a category with good formal properties.

(4)

Results in the classical literature on measure theory should admit a mostly direct translation into this new framework.

Let us remark why we would require points (2) and (3). These are an attempt to address the sentence “Probability theory is about families of random variables.” given in the above quote by Meckes. First of all, we want to regard random variables as the same if they are the same up to almost everywhere equivalence. Second, the choice of domain of a random variable should not matter in practice: any precomposition by an isomorphism should be regarded as equivalent. A categorical approach can handle this issue, since it is an intrinsic feature of category theory that using categorical concepts allows one to treat “isomorphic situations as equivalent”.

It should be said that the attitude towards a structural approach to measure and probability theory has shifted in recent years. We would like to highlight work of authors such as Simpson
[
Sim12
]
, Vickers
[
Vic08
]
,
[
Vic11
]
, Henry
[
Hen17
]
, Jackson
[
Jac06
]
, Pavlov
[
Pav22
]
, Tao & Jamneshan
[
JT23
]
and Jamneshan
[
Jam14
]
, among others, which for the most part emphasize sheaf-theoretic and point-free perspectives to measure theory, by using locale and topos-theory as a foundation.

In this paper we will address points (1) to (4) by adopting a locale theoretic approach to measure theory, building on the techniques presented by the aforementioned authors. Locales serve as a replacement for topological spaces. The standard references for locale theory are
[
Joh82
]
,
[
PP12
]
and
[
Vic89
]
. A
locale

\displaystyle L
is specified by its
frame
of abstract open sets
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(L)=F
, a poset
\displaystyle(F,\leq)
that generalizes the lattice of open subsets of a topological space. This poset is required to satisfy:

•

Closure under arbitrary suprema, written as
\displaystyle\bigvee_{i\in i}U_{i}
for a collection
\displaystyle U_{i}\in F,i\in I.
This includes the existence of a minimal element
\displaystyle 0
(the empty supremum).

•

Closure under finite infima, written as
\displaystyle U_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge U_{n}
. This includes the existence of a maximal element
\displaystyle 1
(the empty infimum).

•

The distributivity relation

V\wedge\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}=\bigvee_{i\in I}V\wedge U_{i}

needs to hold.

A frame homomorphism
\displaystyle f^{*}:F\rightarrow F^{\prime}
is a function preserving the order, arbitrary suprema and finite infima. A
sublocale

\displaystyle S\hookrightarrow L
of a frame is given by the image of a surjective frame homomorphism
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(L)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(S)
.

For comparison, if
\displaystyle X
is a topological space, then
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X)
forms a frame, and every continuous map
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
induces a frame homomorphism
\displaystyle f^{-1}:\mathcal{O}(Y)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(X)
. However not every frame arises this way. Similarly, every subspace
\displaystyle S\subset X
of a topological space determines a sublocale of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X)
, but not every sublocale of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X)
corresponds to an actual subspace.

A
valuation
is a function
\displaystyle\mu:D\rightarrow[0,\infty]
on a lower bounded distributive lattive
\displaystyle D
, such that

•

\displaystyle\mu(U)\leq\mu(V)
whenever
\displaystyle U\leq V\in D
.

•

\displaystyle\mu(0)=0
.

•

\displaystyle\mu(U)+\mu(V)=\mu(U\vee V)+\mu(U\wedge V)
for all
\displaystyle U,V\in D
.

If
\displaystyle D=\mathcal{O}(L)
for a locale
\displaystyle L
, then we call a valuation
\displaystyle\mu:\mathcal{O}(L)\rightarrow[0,\infty]
a
measure
on
\displaystyle L
if it furthermore satisfies

\mu(\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i})=\sup_{i\in I}\mu(U_{i}).

for every directed system of opens
\displaystyle U_{i},i\in I
. Let us give two examples of measure in this locale theoretic sense:

•

In the case of a Radon measure
\displaystyle\mu
on a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
, such as for example the Lebesgue measure on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the restriction of
\displaystyle\mu
to the set of opens of
\displaystyle X
will give a measure in the locale-theoretic sense.

•

If
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{L},\mu)
is a
localizable
measure space (See Section
12.1
), then the quotient
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}/\mathcal{N}
, where
\displaystyle\mathcal{N}
is the ideal of null sets, is an example of a frame. Then
\displaystyle\mu
descends to a (locale-theoretic) measure on this frame. The corresponding locale
\displaystyle L(\mathcal{L}/\mathcal{N})
is practically never obtained via an actual topological space.

The most striking result in the setting of localic measure theory may be the construction of a translation and rotation invariant measure
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}
on the coframe of all sublocales of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}
proved independently by Leroy
[
Ler13
]
and Simpson
[
Sim12
]
, which “resolves” the well-known Banach-Tarski paradox. Any subset
\displaystyle A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
induces a corresponding sublocale and therefore has an associated value
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}(A)
which can rightfully be called the
Lebesgue measure
of
\displaystyle A
. The reason there is no contradiction to the Vitali or Banach-Tarski paradox is that the intersections of the individual sets appearing in a paradoxical decomposition may have no points, but are nonetheless non-trivial sublocales. Among other things, we will generalize this result to the context of arbitrary Radon measures over Hausdorff spaces
\displaystyle X
in this article.

The salient feature that separates the theory of locales from that of topological spaces is that one is able to use Grothendieck topologies for the construction and description of locales. This works much like the use of presentations of groups via generators and relations. The theory of Grothendieck topologies is well-established in the broader context of sheaf theory. For the sake of having a coherent account in the setting of locales, we collect the main techniques in Section
3
. A Grothendieck topology consists of the datum of a poset
\displaystyle(P,\leq)
, to be thought of as “local pieces”, which is analogous to the set of generators of a group, together with a collection
\displaystyle\tau
of coverings
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p~|~i\in I\}
, which contain the information of when one local piece
\displaystyle p
is covered by smaller local pieces
\displaystyle p_{i}
, which are analogous to the set of relations for a group presentation. There is a locale
\displaystyle L=L(P,\tau)
generated from this datum, for which
\displaystyle P
functions much like a basis in traditional point-set topology. Abstract open sets of
\displaystyle L
, continuous functions into
\displaystyle L
, as well as measures on
\displaystyle L
are all determined from constructions purely on
\displaystyle P
, much like how a presentation of a group
\displaystyle G
means that arbitrary group elements can be representated by words, and group homomorphisms out of
\displaystyle G
are determined by how they act on generators.
1
1

1

See Definition
3.2
, Theorem
3.20
and Theorem
4.12
.
There is a recognition principle that allows one to determine when a given locale
\displaystyle L
arises from a particular Grothendieck topology, called the Basis Theorem (Theorem
3.12
). All this means that even if one only cares about an ordinary topological space
\displaystyle X
, it can still be very useful to find a suitable presentation via a Grothendieck topology.

The central example of a Grothendieck topology will be the
\displaystyle\mu
-
inner topology
for the case of a finite valuation
\displaystyle\mu
on a lower bounded distributive lattice
\displaystyle(D,\leq)
. We call this datum a
valuation site
. The coverings in this case consist of two different cases. (See Definition
4.20
.)

•

Finite unions cover:
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p~|~i\in I\}
is a cover whenever

\bigvee_{i\in I}p_{i}=p

for
\displaystyle I
finite.

•

\displaystyle\mu
-approximations cover:
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p~|~i\in I\}
is a cover whenever
\displaystyle p_{i},i\in I,
is directed and

\mu(p)=\sup_{i\in I}\mu(p_{i}).

The corresponding locale
\displaystyle L(D,\mu)=(D,\mu)^{inn}
comes equipped with a
locally finite
and
faithful
measure
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
(See Definition
4.5
), which extends
\displaystyle\mu
, and can be thought of as a
\displaystyle\mu
-generic point. The main technical result about this construction is summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem 1.1

(See Theorem
4.38
)
.

There exists an adjunction

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ValSite}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{MeasFrm}}.}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{inn}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{fin}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

This adjunction is idempotent, inducing an equivalence of categories

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ValSite}_{\sigma,\mathrm{faithf}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{MeasFrm}_{\mathrm{l.f.~faithf}}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{inn}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{fin}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

where

•

\displaystyle{\mathrm{ValSite}_{\sigma,\mathrm{faithf}}}
is the full subcategory of the category
\displaystyle\mathrm{ValSite}
of valuation sites, given by bounded
\displaystyle\sigma
-complete and faithful valuation sites, and

•

\displaystyle{\mathrm{MeasFrm}_{\mathrm{l.f.~faithf}}}
is the full subcategory of the category
\displaystyle{\mathrm{MeasFrm}}
of frames equipped with measures, given by faithful and locally finite measures.

One of the uses of the
\displaystyle\mu
-inner topology will be a concrete description of measure algebras. (Adressing point (1).) Suppose
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
is the structure of a Radon measure
\displaystyle\mu
on a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
. The restriction of
\displaystyle\mu
to the set
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
of compact subsets is a finite valuation.

Theorem 1.2

(See Theorem
12.11
)
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
be a Radon measure on a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
. Then we have a measure-preserving isomorphism

L(\mathcal{K}(X),\mu)\cong L(\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{N}).

where
\displaystyle L(\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{N})
is the locale associated to the
measure algebra

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{N}
of
\displaystyle\mu
, obtained as the complete Boolean algebra of measurable sets modulo null sets.

The notion of Radon measure includes practically all relevant examples of measure spaces for applications in analysis, geometry and probability theory. For these applications, the measure algebra together with its associated measure contains arguably all of the important information needed for measure theory. (Most notably
\displaystyle L^{1}
and
\displaystyle L^{\infty}
, and more generally
\displaystyle L^{p}
, can be constructed directly from it.) The associated sheaf topos has also been suggested as a natural place for a type of conditional set theory, within which all constructions and arguments are automatically measurable and invariant under almost everywhere equivalence
[
Jam14
]
. The relevance of Theorem
12.11
is that for these applications, the entire information of the Boolean algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}
is superfluous. What is relevant is the datum of
\displaystyle\mu
defined on the set of compact sets
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
. Constructions, such as sheaves, geometric morphisms or continuous functions, are entirely determined from geometric constructions on
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
that agree with the
\displaystyle\mu
-inner topology (in other words, are insensitive to approximating from below via compacts). While the entire
\displaystyle\sigma
-algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}
is often a fairly wild and inexplicit mathematical object, when dealing with practical applications this wildness is irrelevant. Philosophically speaking, this should not be too surprising, as for the construction of a Radon measure one typically starts with giving a
content

\displaystyle\lambda:\mathcal{K}(X)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
and then uses an extension principle, such as variants of Carathéodory’s extension theorem.

The locale
\displaystyle L(\mathcal{K}(X),\mu)\cong L(\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{N})
has a Boolean algebra of open sets and comes equipped with a locally finite and faithful measure. We call locales with these two properties
measurable locales
. Given a measurable locale
\displaystyle L
, define
\displaystyle L^{\infty}(L)
to be the set of bounded, continuous, complex-valued functions on
\displaystyle L
. There exists a Gelfand-type duality due to Pavlov.

Theorem 1.3

(
[
Pav22
]
)
.

The functor
\displaystyle L^{\infty}
induces an equivalence

L^{\infty}:\mathrm{MblLoc}^{op}\simeq\mathrm{CVNA},

where
\displaystyle\mathrm{MblLoc}
is the category of measurable locales, and
\displaystyle\mathrm{CVNA}
is the category of commutative von Neumann algebras and normal
\displaystyle*
-morphisms. The inverse functor sends a commutative von Neumann algebra to its locale of projections.

This theorem means that all statements about measurable locales have corresponding functional-analytic counterparts, and vice versa. In particular, a theory of integration for measurable locales is already worked out. Therefore it is worthwhile to give a concrete condition for when a valuation site
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
produces a measurable locale
\displaystyle L(D,\mu)
. We call this condition
almost Boolean
, see Definition
6.9
for details.

Theorem 1.4

(See Theorem
6.18
)
.

If
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
is almost Boolean, then
\displaystyle L(D,\mu)
is a measurable locale.

The main example is given by a
regular content

\displaystyle\lambda:\mathcal{K}(X)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
on a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
(See Section
8.3
).

Theorem 1.5

(See Theorem
8.17
)
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff topological space and
\displaystyle\lambda:\mathcal{K}(X)\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
a regular content. Then
\displaystyle\lambda
is an almost Boolean valuation on
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
.

This motivates the development of a theory of measure based purely on the notion of an almost Boolean valuation on the set
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
of compact subsets of a Hausdorff space.

Definition 1.6

(See
8.1
)
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff space. A
Radon valuation
on
\displaystyle X
is a finite and almost Boolean valuation
\displaystyle\mu:\mathcal{K}(X)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
on the set of compact subsets of
\displaystyle X
. Denote by
\displaystyle X^{\mu}
the associated inner locale together with its locally finite and faithful measure
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
. It comes with a natural continuous map
\displaystyle X^{\mu}\rightarrow X
.

As discussed before, classical Radon measures give examples of Radon valuations. The existence of a Radon valuation has many pleasant consequences, which are discussed in Section
8
. First all, it equips
\displaystyle X
with a measure
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
via pushforward along
\displaystyle X^{\mu}\rightarrow X
. Furthermore, recall the notion of a sublocale of a locale
\displaystyle L
. The set of sublocales
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(L)
naturally forms a co-frame, or equivalently
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(L)^{op}
is a frame, whose corresponding locale we denote by
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(L)
, called the
locale of sublocales
of
\displaystyle L
, sometimes also referred to as the
dissolution locale
of
\displaystyle L
. This locale has a natural continuous map
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(L)\rightarrow L
, whose inverse image part sends an open
\displaystyle U
to the closed complement of
\displaystyle U
viewed as a sublocale, and which comes with a universal property regarding a specific class of maps
\displaystyle M\rightarrow L
. (See Theorem
5.5
.) This universal property holds in particular for the map
\displaystyle X^{\mu}\rightarrow X
and produces a unique lift,

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{Sl}(X)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\mu}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{can}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}

The map
\displaystyle X^{\mu}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
equips the locale of sublocales with a measure, also denoted as
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
, via pushforward, or equivalently the co-frame
\displaystyle(\mathrm{Sl}(X),\leq)
with a
co-measure
. Due to the universality of this construction it is straightforward to verify functoriality of this assignment.

Theorem 1.7

(See Theorem
8.9
)
.

There exists a lift of the functor
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}:\mathrm{RadHausSpc}_{glob}\rightarrow\mathrm{Loc}
to a functor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{Sl}:\mathrm{RadHausSpc}_{glob}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{MeasLoc}_{glob}\\
(X,\mu)&\mapsto&(\mathfrak{Sl}(X),\mu_{*})\end{array}

where
\displaystyle\mathrm{MeasLoc}_{glob}
is the category of locales equipped with measures and globally defined measure-preserving maps between them.

Corollary 1.8

(See Corollary
8.11
)
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff space equipped with a Radon valuation
\displaystyle\mu
. Then there exists a measure
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
on the locale of sublocales
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
of
\displaystyle X
, which is invariant under measure-preserving homeomorphisms of
\displaystyle X
, and the natural continuous map
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(X)\rightarrow X
is measure-preserving.

What is fascinating is that this in particular assigns to each subset of
\displaystyle X
a consistent measure, which is invariant under measure-reserving homeomorphisms, via pushforward along the natural map
\displaystyle X^{disc}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
, which in case the Radon valuation
\displaystyle\mu
is a regular content agrees with the classical induced Radon measure on measurable sets, see Corollary
8.22
. The formula of the measure one obtains for an arbitrary subset
\displaystyle S\subset X
is given by

\mu_{*}(S)=\sup\{\mu(K)~|~K\subset S\text{ compact}\}.

The pushforward however does not need to send a partition of
\displaystyle X
into disjoint subsets to a partition of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
, therefore no contradiction to Vitali’s Paradox or the Banach-Tarski Paradox is obtained. Rather, the locale of sublocales “knows” when one has chosen a bad decomposition, since there are
more
sublocales than subsets to detect these failures. This result is similar to the results obtained by Leroy
[
Ler13
]
and Simpson
[
Sim12
]
, although the main conceptual difference is that we construct a measure on
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(X)^{op}
rather than
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(X)
, which results in much better formal properties.

Remark 1.9
.

This result is maybe the strongest indicator that from the point-of-view of topology the notion of a subspace of a topological space
\displaystyle X
described by a
set
of points is a concept that should be treated with caution and in general replaced by the notion of a sublocale. While it is fine to define a single particular subspace of a topological space via its subset of points, it is not a good idea to consider the totality of all such subspaces in a geometric or topological context. The notion of subspace really only works well when one restricts to finite unions and intersections of closed and open sets (and occasionally slightly more if one adds additional assumptions on the space
\displaystyle X
). The collection of all subspaces of a space should of course also be viewed
topologically
- In this sense the locale
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
is the natural answer to how one should organize all “topological sub-objects of
\displaystyle X
” into one space. However,
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
is often not spatial, therefore one is forced to accept locale theory as the ambient setting in which to do topology. (Even if one is interested only in concrete topological spaces.)

Remark 1.10
.

Compared with classical measure theory, the approach may seem backwards. Classically, for a measure space
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{L},\mu)
one first produces the
\displaystyle\sigma
-algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{P}(X)
, and then obtains the measure algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}/\mathcal{N}
after the fact. In our approach, we construct the measure algebra
first
, and obtain the fact that we can measure all sublocales as a consequence.

Remark 1.11
.

The locale of sublocales
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(L)
has a basis given by locally closed subsets of
\displaystyle X
, i.e. intersections of open and closed subsets. It therefore plays a role analogous to the constructible topology on a scheme. Continuous functions on the constructible topology have been used as a replacement for the notion of measurable function in the context of
motivic integration theory
as developed originally by Kontsevich, see e.g.
[
Bli05
]
. Similarly, in the context of classical measure theory, we can think of continuous functions defined on
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(L)
as a useful and better behaved replacement of the notion of measurable function on
\displaystyle X
. A working theory of Lebesgue integration of functions defined on the locale of sublocales
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(L)
is for example developed by Bernardes in
[
Ber25
]
.

Let us present some more results under the assumption that we have a regular content
\displaystyle\lambda
on a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
.

Theorem 1.12

(See Theorem
8.20
)
.

Let
\displaystyle\lambda
be a regular content on a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
. Then the induced map
\displaystyle\tilde{p}:X^{\lambda}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
identifies
\displaystyle X^{\lambda}
with a sublocale of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
.

A variant of this theorem has already been proven by Leroy
[
Ler13
, Théoremè 2]
. The results are not entirely equivalent, but agree when
\displaystyle X
is also assumed locally compact.

We give a universal property for the map
\displaystyle X^{\lambda}\rightarrow X
as the terminal measurable locale equipped with a locally finite and faithful measure and a measure-preserving map to
\displaystyle X
. This characterization was originally suggested by Simon Henry in the mathoverflow post
[
httb
]
.

Theorem 1.13

(See Theorem
8.24
)
.

Let
\displaystyle\lambda
be a regular content on a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
and assume that the induced measure
\displaystyle\lambda_{*}
on
\displaystyle X
is locally finite.
2
2

2

This condition is automatically satisfied if
\displaystyle X
is locally compact, see Lemma
9.3
.
Suppose
\displaystyle f:(Y,\mu)\rightarrow(X,\lambda_{*})
is a measure-preserving continuous map, with
\displaystyle Y
being Boolean, and
\displaystyle\mu
a locally finite and faithful measure on
\displaystyle Y
. Then there exists a unique measure-preserving map
\displaystyle\tilde{f}:(Y,\mu)\rightarrow(X^{\lambda},\lambda_{*})
making the triangle

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\lambda}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

commute.

The following is a characterization of the set of points of
\displaystyle X^{\lambda}
. As a consequence one sees that
\displaystyle X^{\lambda}
is rarely ever spatial.

Proposition 1.14

(See Propositon
8.25
)
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff space,
\displaystyle\lambda
a regular content and
\displaystyle X^{\lambda}
the associated Boolean inner measure locale. Then the natural map
\displaystyle p_{\lambda}:X^{\lambda}\rightarrow X
induces an injection

\mathrm{pts}(X^{\lambda})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{pts}(X)

which identifies the left-hand side with the set of point
\displaystyle x
of
\displaystyle X
such that
\displaystyle\lambda(\{x\})>0
.

We call a continuous map
\displaystyle f:X^{\mu}\rightarrow Y
into an arbitrary locale
\displaystyle Y
a
\displaystyle Y
-valued
Random variable
. This terminology fits with the classical definition of a Random variable up to almost everywhere equivalence.

Proposition 1.15

(See Proposition
8.27
and Corollary
8.33
)
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff space and
\displaystyle\mu
a Radon valuation. Assume that
\displaystyle\mu_{*}(U)>0
for all non-empty open sets
\displaystyle U\subset X
, or equivalently that for all non-empty open sets
\displaystyle U
there exists
\displaystyle K\subset U
compact with
\displaystyle\mu(K)>0
. Then the map

p_{\mu}:X^{\mu}\rightarrow X

is dense. In particular, for any Hausdorff locale
\displaystyle Y
it induces an injection

\mathrm{Map}(X,Y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}(X^{\mu},Y)

of continuous maps into random variables.

The special case of
\displaystyle Y=\mathbb{C}
produces an inclusion
\displaystyle C_{b}(X;\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
of bounded continuous complex-valued functions on
\displaystyle X
into
\displaystyle L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
.

We also prove a representation theorem for measurable locales in Section
9.1
.

Theorem 1.16

(See Theorem
9.10
)
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a measurable locale. Then there exists a locally compact Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
together with a regular content
\displaystyle\lambda
such that
\displaystyle L\cong X^{\lambda}
.

Following this, we give a short discussion on how one would construct the classical Lebesgue measure on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}
as well as more generally the Haar measure on a locally compact Hausdorff group directly in Section
10
. This is meant to illustrate how results in the literature on classical measure theory can be imported into the localic setup in an efficient manner.

As a last application, we provide a construction of the
Lebesgue locale

\displaystyle M_{Leb}
of a smooth manifold
\displaystyle M
, which is the localic counterpart to the
\displaystyle C^{*}
-algebra
\displaystyle L^{\infty}(M)
(which can be constructed without reference to one particular measure). This construction has some pleasant functoriality and provides a basic object on which to set up integration theory on smooth manifolds.

Theorem 1.17

(See Theorem
11.12
)
.

There exists a functor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(-)_{Leb}:\mathrm{Man}_{\mathrm{subm}}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{MblLoc}\\
M&\mapsto&M_{Leb}\end{array}

where
\displaystyle\mathrm{Man}_{\mathrm{subm}}
is the category of large smooth manifolds and smooth submersions as maps between them.

Remark 1.18
.

Given the framework provided in this article, one can give a simple logical definition of a probability space, which covers virtually all practical applications:

Definition 1.19
.

A
probability system
consists of the datum of a complete Boolean algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
of
events
together with a
faithful measure

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}
such that
\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(1)=1
, in other words an assignment
\displaystyle\mathbb{P}:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow[0,1]
, such that

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbb{P}(E)=0\text{ iff }E=0\\
\mathbb{P}(1)=1\\
\mathbb{P}(E_{1})+\mathbb{P}(E_{2})=\mathbb{P}(E_{1}\vee E_{2})+\mathbb{P}(E_{1}\wedge E_{2})\\
\text{Whenever }\{E_{i}\leq E~|~i\in I\}\text{ is a directed system: }\mathbb{P}(E)=\sup_{i\in I}\mathbb{P}(E_{i})\text{ iff }E=\bigvee_{i\in I}E_{i}\\
\end{array}

The practical meaning of this definition is that once a class of “basic events” that generates
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
under suprema and whose probabilities are known has been identified, working within the algebra of events boils down entirely to the estimation of probabilities. This set of basic events can in praxis always be modelled via an almost Boolean valuation site. Viewing
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
as presenting a locale, a random variable with values in some locale
\displaystyle L
is just a continuous function
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\rightarrow L
. It equips
\displaystyle L
with a probability measure via pushforward (In the case of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}
-valued random variables: The
distribution
of the random variable). The definition of an ergodic action of a group
\displaystyle G
is also straightforward. An action of a group
\displaystyle G
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
is
ergodic
, if the quotient
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{G}
(taken in the category of locales) is a point. The corresponding topos of sheaves on
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
is the topos of conditional set theory over
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
.

Remark 1.20
.

There exist many close analogies between the study of algebraic
\displaystyle K
-theory and measure theory, as already elaborated upon to some extent in the article
[
Leh25
]
. One of the conceptual advantages of the localic definition of a measure is that it highlights these similarities much more closely than the classical definition of a measure on a
\displaystyle\sigma
-algebra does. To demonstrate, compare the following three definitions.

•

A measure on a locale
\displaystyle L
is a functor
\displaystyle\mu:\mathcal{O}(L)\rightarrow[0,\infty]
such that

–

\displaystyle\mu(0)=0
,

–

\displaystyle\mu(U)+\mu(V)=\mu(U\vee V)+\mu(U\cap V)
, and

–

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in I}\mu(U_{i})=\mu(\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i})
for every directed set
\displaystyle U_{i},i\in I
.

•

Let
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
be a cocomplete 1- or
\displaystyle\infty
-category. A
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
-valued
cosheaf
on a locale
\displaystyle L
is a functor
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{O}(L)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
such that

–

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(0)\cong 0
,

–

for every
\displaystyle U,V
the square

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}(U\wedge V)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}(V)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}(U)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}(U\vee V)}}

is a pushout, and

–

\displaystyle\mathrm{colim}_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i})=\mathcal{F}(\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i})
for every directed set
\displaystyle U_{i},i\in I
.

•

A
finitary localizing invariant
with values in a cocomplete stable
\displaystyle\infty
-category
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
is a functor
\displaystyle F:\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
such that
3
3

3

See e.g.
[
HLS23
]
.

–

\displaystyle F(0)\cong 0
,

–

for every Verdier square

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}}

the square

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(A)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(B)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(C)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(D)}}

is a pushout, and

–

\displaystyle\mathrm{colim}_{i\in I}F(A_{i})\cong F(\mathrm{colim}_{i\in I}A_{i})
for every filtered diagram
\displaystyle A_{\bullet}:I\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{perf}}
.

We remark that algebraic
\displaystyle K
-theory and topological Hochschild Homology are examples of finitary localizing invariants.

The formal similarity should be clear: All three definitions are instances of combining the inclusion-exclusion principle with the principle of exhaustion from below. Other examples can be found as well throughout mathematics, e.g. the notion of a homology theory to name another one.

Remark 1.21
.

The use of Hausdorff topological spaces may seem odd from the point of view of developing “point-free” versions of measure theory. Ultimately, this was a concession to keep in line with classical literature, as the theory of regular contents and Radon measures on Hausdorff spaces is already quite well developed, and regular contents are used to construct basically all relevant examples of measure spaces in mathematical practice. Using our present approach, all of these examples can be imported into the locale theoretic context in a fairly pain-free manner.

An approach that is less compromising with respect to classical literature, but more in line with the locale theoretic spirit is taken by Leroy
[
Ler13
]
, who instead studies locally finite measures on
regular
locales. Regularity is a separation condition that is related to Hausdorffness, and works as a reasonable replacement. It seems likely to us that the theory presented in this article could be phrased in this language as well, by reinterpreting the poset of compact subsets
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
of a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
as (the opposite of) the poset of Scott open filters of a regular locale
\displaystyle L
. We have ommitted this approach, as it might alienate readers that are not as familiar with the workings of locale theory, and the conceptual shift present in this article is quite substantial as it is already.

In the case of locally compact Hausdorff spaces both approaches essentially become equivalent. The notion of a locally compact Hausdorff space can be developed entirely within locale theory using locally compact and completely regular locales. Any locally compact locale is automatically spatial (in the presence of the axiom of choice) and the two categories are equivalent, see e.g.
[
PP12
, Section 5.3 and 5.4]
. Locally finite measures on a locally compact Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
are in bijection with regular contents on
\displaystyle X
(See Theorem
9.4
), therefore our theory simply adds a different angle to the theory presented in Leroy’s work when working with locally compact Hausdorff spaces.

Remark 1.22
.

The present article is not complete. For example, the theory of integration, one of the main motivations of measure theory, is absent. Along with this there is no discussion of product measures, or of measures on function spaces. There is also no discussion of group actions on measure locales and ergodic theory. At least in principle, one should be able to readily import these topics from classical literature into the locale theoretic setup. This is something to be addressed in future work.

Remark 1.23
.

Since there is a community of people interested in constructive approaches to measure theory (this interest is visible for example in the work of Vickers
[
Vic08
]
, Coquand-Spitters
[
Spi09
]
, Spitters
[
Spi05
]
among others) we should mention that this paper has been written assuming classical foundations. The use of choice as well as proof by contradiction is present, most notably in the comparison of the three different definitions of a measurable locale (Theorem
6.5
), as well as (unsurprisingly) when comparing the classical measure theory via
\displaystyle\sigma
-algebras to the approach via the
\displaystyle\mu
-inner Grothendieck topology in Section
12
. Nonetheless, we believe the usage of Grothendieck topologies to describe Radon measures can be a useful starting tool for constructive measure theory; something to be worked out by someone more experienced in the matter of constructive mathematics than the author of this article.

1.1.
Acknowledgements

The author thanks Dmitri Pavlov and Simon Henry for helpful feedback on mathoverflow to the question
[
httb
]
that inspired this article, as well as David Kern, Christian Espíndola and Asgar Jamneshan for pointers and explanations. I also want to thank Holger Reich and Chris Huggle for enduring my many ramblings on the topic of this paper during our lunch breaks.

2.
Locale theory

We will use the language of frames and locales throughout this paper. For general resources, see
[
Joh82
]
,
[
PP12
]
and
[
Vic89
]
. Suppose
\displaystyle(P,\leq)
is a poset and
\displaystyle p,q\in P
. We call an element
\displaystyle p\wedge q
a
meet
if the condition

r\leq p\wedge q\text{ iff }r\leq p\text{ and }r\leq q

holds for all
\displaystyle r\in P
. Meets, if they exist, are unique. Of course, one can think of the poset
\displaystyle(P,\leq)
as a special kind of category, a viewpoint that we will often abuse in this paper. Using this perspective, meets are just products.

The
join

\displaystyle p\vee q
is defined dually as the meet of
\displaystyle p
and
\displaystyle q
in the opposite poset
\displaystyle P^{op}
. A
distributive lattice

\displaystyle(D,\leq)
is a poset that admits joins, written as
\displaystyle U\vee V
and meets, written
\displaystyle U\wedge V
, and the relation

U\wedge(V\vee W)=(U\wedge V)\vee(U\wedge W)

holds, for any
\displaystyle U,V,W\in D
. A distributive lattice is called
lower bounded
if it has a bottom element
\displaystyle 0
, and furthermore
bounded
if it also has a top element
\displaystyle 1
.

We refer to monotone maps
\displaystyle f:P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
as
functors
. A functor
\displaystyle f:D\rightarrow D^{\prime}
between distributive lattices is called a
lattice homomorphism
if
\displaystyle f
preserves meets and joins. We denote the categories of lower bounded, respectively bounded, distributive lattices by

\mathrm{DLatt}_{lb}~\text{ and }~\mathrm{DLatt}_{bd},

where morphisms are lattice homomorphisms that preserve the bottom element, respectively the bottom and top element.

More generally, for any poset
\displaystyle P
one can define
infima
, respectively
suprema
, of families of elements
\displaystyle p_{i}\in P,i\in I
analogously to how one defines meets and joins. We will denote these as
\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i\in I}p_{i}
for the infimum, and
\displaystyle\bigvee_{i\in I}p_{i}
for the supremum. Note that top elements and meets are special cases of infima, whereas bottom elements and joins are special cases of suprema. Thinking in terms of categories, infima are just limits, and suprema are just colimits. We call a poset
\displaystyle(P,\leq)
a
complete lattice
if it is closed under arbitrary suprema, which is the case iff it is closed under arbitrary infima.

A
frame
is a poset
\displaystyle(F,\leq)
which admits arbitrary suprema and finite infima and satisfies the infinite distributivity law

U\wedge\bigvee_{i\in I}V_{i}=\bigvee_{i\in I}U\wedge V_{i}

for all
\displaystyle U\in F
and families
\displaystyle V_{i}\in F,i\in I
. We note that in particular any frame is a complete distributive lattice. An important example of a frame is given by the poset
\displaystyle(\mathcal{O}(X),\subset)
of open sets of a topological space
\displaystyle X
. We highlight the following, slightly nonstandard, definition.

Definition 2.1
.

A functor
\displaystyle f^{*}:F\rightarrow F^{\prime}
between frames is called a
partial frame homomorphism
, if
\displaystyle f^{*}
preserves arbitrary suprema and (binary) meets. The element
\displaystyle f^{*}(1)\in F^{\prime}
is called the
domain
of
\displaystyle f^{*}
. A partial frame homomorphism
\displaystyle f^{*}
is called
globally defined
, or simply a
frame homomorphism
, if
\displaystyle f^{*}(1)=1
.

This defines the category
\displaystyle\mathrm{Frm}_{\mathrm{part}}
of frames and partial frame homomorphisms, as well as the category
\displaystyle\mathrm{Frm}
of frames and frame homomorphisms. As an example, a partially defined continuous map
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
with open support
\displaystyle U
gives a partial frame homomorphism
\displaystyle f^{-1}:\mathcal{O}(Y)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(X)
, which is globally defined iff
\displaystyle U=X
. This reversal of direction motivates one to define the category of locales as the opposite category
\displaystyle\mathrm{Loc}=\mathrm{Frm}^{op}
. This way assigning to a topological space
\displaystyle X
its frame
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X)
produces a
covariant
functor
\displaystyle\mathrm{Top}\rightarrow\mathrm{Loc}
, which is an equivalence when restricted to the full subcategories of sober spaces and spatial locales
[
PP12
, II.4.6]
. By abuse of notation, for a general locale
\displaystyle L
we use the notation
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(L)
for the corresponding frame of
\displaystyle L
and refer to elements
\displaystyle U\in\mathcal{O}(L)
as opens of
\displaystyle L
. Conversely, for a given frame
\displaystyle F
we write
\displaystyle L(F)
for the corresponding locale. We refer to arrows
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow L^{\prime}
of locales as
continuous maps
or also
geometric morphisms
, which are of course just dually defined as frame homomorphisms
\displaystyle f^{*}:\mathcal{O}(L^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(L)
. Similarly, we define the category
\displaystyle\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{part}}=\mathrm{Frm}_{\mathrm{part}}^{op}
of locales and partial continuous maps.

Soberness of a topological space is a rather mild separation condition. It is implied by Hausdorffness, and implies
\displaystyle T_{0}
, but is independent of
\displaystyle T_{1}
. We will not have any use for topological spaces which are not sober in this paper. On the other hand, the use of non-spatial locales is absolutely crucial for our purposes.

We note that any partial frame homomorphism
\displaystyle f^{*}:F\rightarrow F^{\prime}
has an associated right adjoint
\displaystyle f_{*}:F^{\prime}\rightarrow F
, defined via the characterization of adjoints, i.e. for all
\displaystyle U\in F,V\in F^{\prime}
we have

f^{*}(U)\leq V\text{ iff }U\leq f_{*}(V).

The existence of
\displaystyle f_{*}
follows either abstractly from the adjoint functor theorem, or can be seen concretely via the formula

f_{*}(V)=\bigvee_{U\text{ s.t. }f^{*}(U)\leq V}U.

Remark 2.2
.

A perhaps unusual feature to readers versed in classical point-set topology or also locale theory is that the category
\displaystyle\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{part}}
is a
pointed category
, with the role of the zero object being taken by the locale of the empty set
\displaystyle\emptyset
. If
\displaystyle L,L^{\prime}
are locales, then the zero map
\displaystyle 0:L\rightarrow L^{\prime}
is given by the functor
\displaystyle 0^{*}:\mathcal{O}(L^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(L),U\mapsto 0
.

We call a globally defined map
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
a
quotient map
if
\displaystyle f^{*}
is injective. Quotient maps are epimorphisms in the category of locales
[
PP12
,
\nopp
IV.1]
. Similarly, we call
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
an
embedding
if
\displaystyle f_{*}
is injective. Embeddings are equivalently
regular
monomorphisms in the category of locales. Subsets
\displaystyle S\subset\mathcal{O}(M)
that give rise to embeddings are called
sublocales
. More on this in the upcoming Section
2.2
.

Despite the fact that locale theory is often called point-free topology, every locale
\displaystyle L
comes with a set of points, see e.g.
[
PP12
,
\nopp
II.3]
. Let
\displaystyle\mathrm{pt}
be the locale corresponding to the topological space given by a single point. We define
\displaystyle\mathrm{pts}(L)=\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Loc}}(\mathrm{pt},L)
. It can be checked that a point of a locale is equivalently described by a
completely prime filter

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{O}(L)
, that is a subset
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{O}(L)
such that

•

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
is proper:
\displaystyle 0\not\in\mathcal{F}
.

•

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
is upward closed: If
\displaystyle U\in\mathcal{F},U\leq V
then also
\displaystyle V\in\mathcal{F}
.

•

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
is closed under binary meets: If
\displaystyle U,V\in\mathcal{F}
then also
\displaystyle U\wedge V\in\mathcal{F}
.

•

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
is completely prime: If
\displaystyle\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
then there exists
\displaystyle i\in I
such that
\displaystyle U_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
.

2.1.
Heyting implication

Now let
\displaystyle L
be a locale, with associated frame
\displaystyle F
. A somewhat under-appreciated feature in classical point-set topology is that in any frame, there is a notion of
Heyting implication

\displaystyle U\rightarrow V
between opens
\displaystyle U,V
, defined by the adjunction
4
4

4

This is of course just a special case of the definition of the internal hom in a cartesian closed category.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\wedge-}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\rightarrow-}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Concretely
\displaystyle U\rightarrow V
can be computed as

U\rightarrow V=\bigvee_{W\text{ s.t. }W\wedge U\leq V}W

The following are standard properties of Heyting implication.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
\displaystyle F
be a frame and
\displaystyle U,V,W\in F
. The following statements hold.

(1)

\displaystyle(U\rightarrow V)\wedge U\leq V

(2)

\displaystyle 1\rightarrow U=U

(3)

\displaystyle U\rightarrow V=1
iff
\displaystyle U\leq V
.

(4)

\displaystyle U\rightarrow(V\rightarrow W)=(U\wedge V)\rightarrow W
.

(5)

\displaystyle V\leq(U\rightarrow V)
.

(6)

\displaystyle U\leq(U\rightarrow V)\rightarrow V
.

(7)

\displaystyle(((U\rightarrow V)\rightarrow V)\rightarrow V)=U\rightarrow V
.

(8)

The assignment
\displaystyle-\rightarrow V:F^{op}\rightarrow F
sends suprema to infima.

Proof.

(1)

We note that this is simply the counit of the adjunction, in this context also called evaluation. We compute it from the formula

(U\rightarrow V)\wedge U=\left(\bigvee_{W\text{ s.t. }W\wedge U\leq V}W\right)\wedge U=\bigvee_{W\text{ s.t. }W\wedge U\leq V}W\wedge U\leq V.

(2)

We have
\displaystyle U\leq(1\rightarrow U)
by using the adjunction on the equality
\displaystyle 1\wedge U=U
. The converse corresponds to the counit of the adjunction
\displaystyle(1\rightarrow V)\wedge 1\leq V
.

(3)

Note that
\displaystyle(U\rightarrow V)\leq 1
is always true. The converse is by the adjunction

1\leq(U\rightarrow V)\text{ iff }U=1\wedge U\leq V.

(4)

Let
\displaystyle Z\in F
be open. Then

Z\leq U\rightarrow(V\rightarrow W)\text{ iff }Z\wedge U\leq(V\rightarrow W)\text{ iff }Z\wedge U\wedge V\leq W\text{ iff }Z\leq(U\wedge V)\rightarrow W.

The statement follows by the Yoneda lemma.

(5)

This follows by applying the adjunction to
\displaystyle U\wedge V\leq V
.

(6)

This follows by applying the adjunction to the counit

U\wedge(U\rightarrow V)\leq V.

(7)

Applying (5) we immediately get

U\rightarrow V\leq(((U\rightarrow V)\rightarrow V)\rightarrow V)

as a special case. However, we can also apply the contravariant functor
\displaystyle(-)\rightarrow V
to (5) to obtain

\displaystyle(((U\rightarrow V)\rightarrow V)\rightarrow V)\leq U\rightarrow V
.

(8)

One verifies that
\displaystyle(-)\rightarrow V
is always right adjoint to itself (viewed as a functor
\displaystyle F\rightarrow F^{op}
).

∎

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
be a partial continuous map. Let
\displaystyle U
be an open of
\displaystyle L
and
\displaystyle V,Z
opens of
\displaystyle M
. Then:

(1)

\displaystyle f_{*}(f^{*}(V)\rightarrow U)=V\rightarrow f_{*}(U)
, and

(2)

\displaystyle f^{*}(V\rightarrow Z)\leq f^{*}(V)\rightarrow f^{*}(Z)
.

Proof.

Consider the square of commuting left adjoints:

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(L)}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(L)}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(M)}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(M)}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\wedge f^{*}(V)}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\wedge V}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

The first equation is obtained by passing to right adjoints, whereas the second is the mate of this square. (Alternatively verified directly by applying
\displaystyle f^{*}
to (1) in Proposition
2.3
and adjoining over).
∎

2.2.
Sublocales

Given a locale
\displaystyle L
with corresponding frame
\displaystyle F
and a subset
\displaystyle F^{\prime}\subset F
, one may wonder when
\displaystyle F^{\prime}
itself defines a locale. If this happens, and the inclusion becomes the right adjoint of a frame homomorphism, we call the associated locale
\displaystyle L^{\prime}
a sublocale of
\displaystyle L
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\displaystyle i:L^{\prime}\rightarrow L
be a continuous map of locales. We call
\displaystyle i

an embedding
, if
\displaystyle i_{*}:\mathcal{O}(L^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(L)
is injective.

Lemma 2.6

(
[
PP12
]
, III.1 & 2,
[
Joh82
]
II.2)
.

Let
\displaystyle i:L^{\prime}\rightarrow L
be a continuous map of locales. The following are equivalent:

•

\displaystyle i
is an embedding.

•

The left adjoint
\displaystyle i^{*}:\mathcal{O}(L)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(L^{\prime})
is surjective.

•

\displaystyle i
is an extremal monomorphism in the category of locales.

•

\displaystyle i
is a regular monomorphism in the category of locales.

•

\displaystyle i^{*}i_{*}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{O}(L^{\prime})}
.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a locale with frame
\displaystyle F
. A
sublocale
of
\displaystyle L
is defined as a subset
\displaystyle S\subset F
such that
\displaystyle S
is a frame in its induced order and the inclusion
\displaystyle i_{*}:S\hookrightarrow F
is the right adjoint to a frame homomorphism
\displaystyle i^{*}:F\rightarrow S
.

We note that any sublocale of
\displaystyle L
gives an embedding
\displaystyle i:L(S)\rightarrow L
and any embedding
\displaystyle f:L^{\prime}\rightarrow L
identifies
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(L^{\prime})
with a sublocale of
\displaystyle L
.

Example 2.8

(
[
PP12
]
, III.6)
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a locale and
\displaystyle U
an open of
\displaystyle L
. We get the induced
open sublocale
associated to
\displaystyle U
by analyzing the adjunction

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}(L)_{/U}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(L)}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{!}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

The three functors involved are given as:

•

\displaystyle i_{!}(V)=V
for any
\displaystyle V\leq U
. This functor is a partial frame homomorphism, with
\displaystyle\mathrm{dom}(i_{!})=U
.

•

\displaystyle i^{*}(W)=W\wedge U
. This functor is a frame homomorphism.

•

\displaystyle i_{*}(V)=U\rightarrow V
.

It may be a bit unusual to a reader new to locale theory that the natural identification of the open sublocale associated to an open
\displaystyle U
is via the functor
\displaystyle i_{*}=U\rightarrow-
, and not via the more easily understood forget functor
\displaystyle i_{!}
. We will often identify the open
\displaystyle U
with the sublocale
\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}(L)_{/U}}
in notation.

Remark 2.9
.

The above example justifies our naming of the notion of partial continuous maps. Let
\displaystyle f^{*}:F\rightarrow F^{\prime}
be a partial frame homomorphism. Then
\displaystyle f^{*}
factors as

f^{*}:F\xrightarrow{f^{\#}}F^{\prime}_{/f^{*}(1)}\subset F^{\prime}.

Writing
\displaystyle\mathrm{dom}(f)=f^{*}(1)
, this corresponds to a geometric factorization

L(F^{\prime})\supset\mathrm{dom}(f)\xrightarrow{f}L(F).

One could therefore identify the category
\displaystyle\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{part}}
with a certain subcategory of the category
\displaystyle\mathrm{Span}(\mathrm{Loc})
of locales and morphisms given by spans, as is common in the setting of
\displaystyle 6
-functor formalisms. We won’t investigate this perspective any further in this paper.

Example 2.10
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a locale and
\displaystyle U
an open of
\displaystyle L
. We get the induced
closed sublocale
associated to
\displaystyle U^{c}
from the adjunction

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}(L)_{U/}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(L)}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

The two functors involved are given as:

•

\displaystyle i^{*}(W)=W\vee U
. This functor is a frame homomorphism.

•

\displaystyle i_{*}(V)=V
for
\displaystyle V
such that
\displaystyle U\leq V
.

Remark 2.11
.

Viewing
\displaystyle i_{!}:\mathcal{O}(U)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(L)
as a partial frame homomorphism, or dually as a partial map of locales
\displaystyle L\rightarrow U
(the partial map that is the identity map on the open domain
\displaystyle U
), we see that the composite

U^{c}\hookrightarrow L\rightarrow U

is the zero map. This sequence, which is in fact a fiber sequence, corresponds to the classical open-closed decomposition of
\displaystyle L
.

Example 2.12
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a topological space, and
\displaystyle i:S\subset X
a subset, equipped with the subspace topology. Then the frame homomorphism
\displaystyle i^{-1}=S\cap-:\Omega(X)\rightarrow\Omega(S)
gives an embedding between associated locales. However, not every sublocale of the associated locale to
\displaystyle X
arises this way, even if
\displaystyle X
is sober. In other words, sublocales of spatial locales need not be spatial.

Sublocales can in fact also be characterized via the Heyting implication.

Lemma 2.13

(
[
PP12
]
, III.2.2)
.

Let
\displaystyle S\subset F
be a subset of a frame
\displaystyle F
. Then
\displaystyle S
determines a sublocale iff

(1)

\displaystyle S\subset F
is closed under infima.

(2)

\displaystyle S\subset F
is closed under Heyting implication, i.e. if
\displaystyle U\in S
and
\displaystyle V\in F
then
\displaystyle V\rightarrow U\in S
.

We finish this section by discussing the universal property of a sublocale, which is a very special case of the notion of a Bousfield localization.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a locale with associated frame
\displaystyle F
, and
\displaystyle i_{*}:S\hookrightarrow F
be a sublocale. Let
\displaystyle P
be a poset and
\displaystyle f:F\rightarrow P
be a functor. Then there exists a functor
\displaystyle g:S\rightarrow P
such that

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

commutes iff
\displaystyle f=fi_{*}i^{*}
, in which case
\displaystyle g=fi_{*}
. Furthermore, given this situation:

•

If
\displaystyle P
has arbitrary suprema, and
\displaystyle f
preserves them, then so does
\displaystyle g
.

•

If
\displaystyle P
has a top element, and
\displaystyle f(1)=1
, then so does
\displaystyle g
.

•

If
\displaystyle P
has binary meets, and
\displaystyle f
preserves them, then the same holds for
\displaystyle g
.

Remark 2.15
.

In particular, a (partial) continuous map
\displaystyle f:M\rightarrow L
factors through an embedding
\displaystyle i:L^{\prime}\rightarrow L
iff
\displaystyle f=fi_{*}i^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle g:S\rightarrow P
be such that
\displaystyle gi^{*}=f
. Then
\displaystyle g=gi^{*}i_{*}=fi_{*}
, and
\displaystyle fi_{*}i^{*}=gi^{*}=f
. Conversely, assume
\displaystyle f=fi_{*}i^{*}
. Setting
\displaystyle g=fi_{*}
we simply verify that
\displaystyle gi^{*}=fi_{*}i^{*}=f
.

Now assume that
\displaystyle P
has arbitrary suprema and
\displaystyle f
preserves them. Then

g(\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i})=g(i^{*}(\bigvee_{i\in I}i_{*}U_{i}))=f(\bigvee_{i\in I}i_{*}U_{i})=\bigvee_{i\in I}fi_{*}(U_{i})=\bigvee_{i\in I}g(U_{i}).

Lastly, the statements about binary meets and top elements follow immediately since
\displaystyle g=fi_{*}
is then a composite of functors that preserves them.
∎

We obtain a simple universal property for closed sublocales.

Corollary 2.16
.

Let
\displaystyle L,M
be locales,
\displaystyle V
an open of
\displaystyle M
, and
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
a partial continuous map. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\displaystyle f
factors through the closed embedding
\displaystyle V^{c}\hookrightarrow M
.

(2)

\displaystyle f^{*}(V)=0
.

(3)

\displaystyle V\leq f_{*}(0)
.

More generally, assume
\displaystyle U
is an open of
\displaystyle L
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1
′
)

\displaystyle f
induces a (unique) map
\displaystyle f|:U^{c}\hookrightarrow V^{c}
.

(2
′
)

\displaystyle f^{*}(V)\leq U
.

(3
′
)

\displaystyle V\leq f_{*}(U)
.

If this is the case, then
\displaystyle f|^{*}=f^{*}(-)\vee U:\mathcal{O}(M)_{V/}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(L)_{U/}
.

Proof.

Clearly, the first statement is a special case of the second, by setting
\displaystyle U=0
. (Note that
\displaystyle 0\leq f^{*}(V)
is always satisfied.) The equivalence of (2) and (3) follows immediately from the adjunction:

V\leq f_{*}(U)\text{ iff }f^{*}(V)\leq U.

Now consider the composite
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(M)\xrightarrow{f^{*}}\mathcal{O}(L)\xrightarrow{-\vee U}\mathcal{O}(L)_{U/}
. This functor descends along the functor
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(M)\xrightarrow{-\vee V}\mathcal{O}(M)_{V/}
iff

\displaystyle f^{*}(-\vee V)\vee U=f^{*}(-)\vee f^{*}(V)\vee U=f^{*}(-)\vee U.

By evaluating at
\displaystyle 0
and using that
\displaystyle f^{*}(0)=0
, we see that this is the case iff
\displaystyle f^{*}(V)\vee U=U
, which is again equivalent to the statement
\displaystyle f^{*}(V)\leq U
.
∎

Let
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
be a continuous map of locales. Since in any category, regular monomorphisms are closed under pullback along another map, we can define for any sublocale
\displaystyle S\hookrightarrow M
a pre-image sublocale
\displaystyle f_{-1}[S]\hookrightarrow L
via the pullback

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{-1}[S]}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M.}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in the category of locales. This pullback always exists, as the category of locales is closed under limits
[
PP12
, Chapter IV.4]
, and is given as the largest sublocale of
\displaystyle L
contained in
\displaystyle f^{-1}(S)\subset\mathcal{O}(L)

[
PP12
, III 4.2]
. Taking pre-images behaves well with respect to open and closed inclusions.

Lemma 2.17

(
[
Joh82
]
, II.2.8)
.

Let
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
be a continuous map of locales and
\displaystyle U
an open of
\displaystyle M
. Then
\displaystyle f_{-1}[U]
is the open sublocale given by
\displaystyle f^{*}(U)
, and
\displaystyle f_{-1}[U^{c}]
is the closed sublocale given by
\displaystyle f^{*}(U)^{c}
.

2.3.
The locale of sublocales

In classical point-set topology, for a topological space
\displaystyle X
, the natural continuous map
\displaystyle X^{disc}\rightarrow X
corresponds to the inclusion
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X)\subset\mathcal{P}(X)
. This allows one to talk about subspaces of a space, determined by subsets. The locale-theoretic analogue of this is given by replacing the powerset
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(X)
for a space
\displaystyle X
by the set
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(L)
of sublocales of a locale
\displaystyle L
.

Theorem 2.18

(
[
PP12
]
, Chapter III, 3.2.1 and 6.4.1.)
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a locale. The poset
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(L)
of sublocales, ordered by inclusion, is a co-frame, i.e.
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(L)^{op}
is a frame. Furthermore, there is a natural injective frame homomorphism

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\nabla:\mathcal{O}(L)&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Sl}(L)^{op}\\
U&\mapsto&U^{c}\end{array}

Remark 2.19
.

The reader may wonder why the natural morphism
\displaystyle\nabla
sends an open
\displaystyle U
to its closed complement, and not to
\displaystyle U
itself viewed as a sublocale. In general for any frame
\displaystyle F
, the co-frame
\displaystyle F^{op}
can be thought of as representing closed subsets. Since
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(L)
is a co-frame, we may think of it as representing
closed
subsets of a (not-necessarily discrete!) locale analogous to
\displaystyle X^{disc}
. This reversal of direction is of course a formality, but can be confusing in practice.

Infima and suprema in
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(L)
have concrete descriptions, see
[
PP12
, Ch. III 3.2]
. Let
\displaystyle S_{i}\hookrightarrow L,i\in I,
be a collection of sublocales of
\displaystyle L
.

•

The infimum
\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i\in I}S_{i}
is given by the intersection
\displaystyle\bigcap_{i\in I}S_{i}
.

•

For the supremum we have the description

\bigvee_{i\in I}S_{i}=\{\bigwedge A|A\subset\bigcup_{i\in I}S_{i}\}.

Moreover, the closed and open sublocales play a special role. Let
\displaystyle i:S\hookrightarrow L
be a sublocale. Then

S=\bigwedge\{U^{c}\vee V~|~i_{*}i^{*}(U)=i_{*}i^{*}(V)\}.

In other words, joins of open and closed sublocales generate the entire co-frame of sublocales under infima.

Definition 2.20
.

Write
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(L)
for the locale corresponding to the frame
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(L)^{op}
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{can}:\mathfrak{Sl}(L)\rightarrow L
for the natural quotient map given by
\displaystyle\nabla
. We call
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(L)
the
locale of sublocales
of
\displaystyle L
, or sometimes also the
dissolution locale
. (In reference to
[
Isb91
]
, where it was originally defined.)

Theorem 2.21

(
[
PP12
]
Ch. IV, Corollary 6.3.2.)
.

The assignment
\displaystyle L\mapsto\mathfrak{Sl}(L)
is a functor

\mathfrak{Sl}:\mathrm{Loc}\rightarrow\mathrm{Loc}

and
\displaystyle\mathrm{can}:\mathfrak{Sl}\rightarrow\mathrm{id}
is a natural transformation, that is valuewise a quotient map. A map of locales
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
is sent to the frame homomorphism
\displaystyle f_{-1}[-]:\mathrm{Sl}(M)^{op}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sl}(L)^{op}
.

Remark 2.22
.

The map
\displaystyle\mathrm{can}:\mathfrak{Sl}(L)\rightarrow L
comes with a universal property which will be expanded upon later in Theorem
5.5
. We just mention here that this universal property in particular implies that for
\displaystyle X
a topological space, we get a unique lift against
\displaystyle\mathrm{can}
as such

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{Sl}(X)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{disc}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{can}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\varphi}

This can be interpreted either as a supremum-preserving functor
\displaystyle\varphi_{\#}:\mathcal{P}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{Sl}(X)
, which associates to each subspace of
\displaystyle X
(viewed as a closed subspace of
\displaystyle X^{disc}
) its corresponding sublocale, as defined in Example
2.12
, or dually as an infimum-preserving functor
\displaystyle\varphi_{*}:\mathcal{P}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{Sl}(X)^{op}
, sending each subspace of
\displaystyle X
(viewed as an open subspace of
\displaystyle X^{disc}
) to the open complement of its corresponding sublocale. We note that
\displaystyle\varphi_{\#}
(respectively
\displaystyle\varphi_{*}
) will not preserve infima (respectively suprema) in general. This should be viewed as a feature and not a bug: As an example, the subspaces
\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}
and
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}
have non-trivial (even dense) intersection when viewed as sublocales of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}
. This is the reason why Banach-Tarski-like paradoxical decompositions fail to be decompositions in the locale theoretic setting.

2.4.
Open maps

The notion of an open map of locales generalizes that of an open map of topological spaces and will be useful later on.

Definition 2.23
.

A map of locales
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
is called
open
, if
\displaystyle f^{*}:\mathcal{O}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(L)
has a further left adjoint
\displaystyle f_{!}
(called
direct image
of
\displaystyle f
) and the adjunction
\displaystyle f_{!}\dashv f^{*}
satisfies the
Frobenius identity

f_{!}(U\wedge f^{*}(V))=f_{!}(U)\wedge V

for all
\displaystyle U
open in
\displaystyle L
and
\displaystyle V
open in
\displaystyle M
.

Open maps
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
can be equivalently characterized by the property that the direct image of an open sublocale of
\displaystyle L
under
\displaystyle f
is again an open sublocale, see
[
PP12
,
\nopp
III 7.2]
. Important will be the following equivalent characterization.

Proposition 2.24
.

A map of locales
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
is open iff
\displaystyle f^{*}
preserves arbitrary infima and implications.

Proof.

By the adjoint functor theorem,
\displaystyle f_{!}
exists iff
\displaystyle f^{*}
preserves infima. Let us prove that
\displaystyle f^{*}
preserving implications is equivalent to the Frobenius identity. Assume that the Frobenius identity holds. Let
\displaystyle U
open in
\displaystyle L
, and
\displaystyle V,W
open in
\displaystyle M
. We have the chain of equivalent statements:

\begin{array}[]{rl}U\leq f^{*}(V\rightarrow W)&\text{iff}\\
f_{!}(U)\leq V\rightarrow W&\text{iff}\\
f_{!}(U)\wedge V\leq W&\text{iff, by Frobenius}\\
f_{!}(U\wedge f^{*}(V))\leq W&\text{iff}\\
U\wedge f^{*}(V)\leq f^{*}(W)&\text{iff}\\
U\leq f^{*}(V)\rightarrow f^{*}(W).\end{array}

Applying the Yoneda Lemma to the variable
\displaystyle U
, we obtain that
\displaystyle f^{*}
preserves implications. The reader is invited to verify that applying the Yoneda Lemma to
\displaystyle W
instead gives the converse.
∎

Lemma 2.25
.

Let
\displaystyle L,M
be locales,
\displaystyle U
an open of
\displaystyle L
,
\displaystyle V
an open of
\displaystyle M
, and
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
an open map. Then
\displaystyle f
induces an open map
\displaystyle f|:U^{c}\hookrightarrow V^{c}
iff
\displaystyle U=f^{*}(V)
; or equivalently both
\displaystyle f_{!}(U)\leq V
and
\displaystyle f^{*}(V)\leq U
hold.

Proof.

Assume
\displaystyle f
is open, and
\displaystyle f
induces a map
\displaystyle f|:U^{c}\hookrightarrow V^{c}
, which by Lemma
2.16
happens iff
\displaystyle f^{*}(V)\leq U
. Since infima in over-posets are computed underlying, we see that

f^{*}(-)\vee U:\mathcal{O}(M)_{V/}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(L)_{U/}

has a left adjoint. It can be identified directly with

f_{!}(-)\vee V:\mathcal{O}(L)_{U/}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(M)_{V/}.

For the Frobenius identity, assume
\displaystyle U\leq W
, and
\displaystyle V\leq Z
. We need the two terms

\begin{array}[]{rcl}f_{!}(W\wedge(f^{*}(Z)\vee U))\vee V&=&f_{!}((W\wedge f^{*}(Z))\vee U)\vee V\\
=f_{!}(W\wedge f^{*}(Z))\vee f_{!}(U)\vee V&=&(f_{!}(W)\wedge Z)\vee f_{!}(U)\vee V\end{array}

and

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(f_{!}(W)\vee V)\wedge Z&=&(f_{!}(W)\wedge Z)\vee V\end{array}

to agree. But, when evaluating at
\displaystyle W=0
we see that this is the case iff
\displaystyle f_{!}(U)\vee V=V
, i.e.
\displaystyle f_{!}(U)\leq V
.
Furthermore, using the adjunction this is equivalent to
\displaystyle U\leq f^{*}(V)
.
∎

Let us package the statement from Lemma
2.25
slightly differently.

Definition 2.26
.

We call a pair
\displaystyle(L,U)
, where
\displaystyle L
is a locale and
\displaystyle U
is an open of
\displaystyle L
, a
marked
locale. If
\displaystyle(L,U),(M,V)
are two marked locales, then a continuous map
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
will be called
compatible
, if
\displaystyle f^{*}(V)=U
. We denote by
\displaystyle\mathrm{MarkLoc}_{\mathrm{open}}
the category of marked locales and
open
compatible maps.

Theorem 2.27
.

There exists an adjunction

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{MarkLoc}_{\mathrm{open}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{open}}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{c}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-,0)}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

with the left adjoint
\displaystyle L\mapsto(L,0)
obtained by equipping a locale with the marking given by the bottom element
\displaystyle 0
, and the right adjoint being given by
\displaystyle(L,U)\mapsto U^{c}\hookrightarrow L
. Furthermore, the left adjoint is fully faithful.

Proof.

Fully faithfulness of the left adjoint
\displaystyle(-,0)
is automatic, since any frame homomorphism preserves
\displaystyle 0
. The statement that the given functors are adjoint is just a reformulation of Lemma
2.25
.
∎

3.
Sites and Grothendieck topologies

3.1.
Sites on posets

Locales can be thought of as
\displaystyle 0
-topoi, and just like topoi arise naturally from Grothendieck topologies. In this section we will discuss the notion of Grothendieck pretopologies and sites, and generalities around them, in the context of posets. This material is fairly standard and will be familiar to people coming from topos theory, see e.g.
[
Joh82
, Chapter 2]
; however, we have included many details for potential readers from other areas, who might be unfamiliar with it. This section will also set up consistent notation for what is to follow. We do not claim any originality in this section. The basic gist is that in order to define a locale, it often suffices to give a description of only some basic building blocks, maybe also thought of as “local pieces”, together with a description of when one such piece is covered from smaller sub-pieces. This is similar to the notion of a basis in topology, but much more flexible since we do not require the existence of a set of points to start with.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\displaystyle(P,\leq)
be a poset with binary meets. A
Grothendieck pretopology

\displaystyle\tau
on
\displaystyle P
is for each
\displaystyle p\in P
a collection of subsets of
\displaystyle P_{/p}
called
coverings
. We use the notation
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p~|~i\in I\}
for such a subset of
\displaystyle P_{/p}
. Moreover, the collection of all coverings needs to satisfy the conditions:

•

Identities:
For all
\displaystyle p\in P
, the set
\displaystyle\{p\leq p\}
is a covering.

•

Stability under base change:
If
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p~|~i\in I\}
is a covering and
\displaystyle q\leq p
, then
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\wedge q\leq q~|~i\in I\}
is a covering.

•

Locality:
If
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p~|~i\in I\}
is a covering and
\displaystyle\{p_{ij}\leq p_{i}~|~j\in J_{i}\}
is a covering for each
\displaystyle i\in I
, then
\displaystyle\{p_{ij}\leq p~|~i\in I,j\in J_{i}\}
is a covering.

We call
\displaystyle(P,\leq)
together with a Grothendieck pretopology
\displaystyle\tau
a
locally cartesian 0-site.

From now on we will write
\displaystyle(P,\tau)
for a poset
\displaystyle P
together with its Grothendieck topology
\displaystyle\tau
. Let us understand what the information of
\displaystyle\tau
actually is. First of all, we think of the elements
\displaystyle p\in P
as
elementary propositions
. We have a notion of implication given by the order
\displaystyle p\leq q
. The requirement that
\displaystyle P
is closed under meets means that we are able to form logical “and” of elementary propositions. The Grothendieck topology controls what we mean by “or”. To make sense of this, let us define what a general proposition is. Denote by
\displaystyle\mathbf{2}=(\{0,1\},\leq)
the poset of Boolean truth values. We think of
\displaystyle 0
as false and
\displaystyle 1
as true.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\displaystyle(P,\tau)
be a locally cartesian
\displaystyle 0
-site. A
proposition
, also called
propositional sheaf
, is a functor
\displaystyle F:P^{op}\rightarrow\mathbf{2}
such that for any cover
\displaystyle\{U_{i}\}
of
\displaystyle U
we have
5
5

5

Note that the “only if” direction in the definition of a propositional sheaf is automatic since
\displaystyle F
is a functor, so
\displaystyle F(U)\leq F(U_{i})
for all
\displaystyle i\in I
.

F(U)=1\text{ iff }F(U_{i})=1\text{ for all }i\in I.

The set of propositional sheaves forms a sub-poset of
\displaystyle\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2})
, which we denote by
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})
.

Any functor
\displaystyle F:P^{op}\rightarrow\mathbf{2}
is determined by the set of objects that are mapped to
\displaystyle 1
. In this sense we can give an equivalent definition.

Definition 3.3
.

A
\displaystyle\tau
-ideal is a subset
\displaystyle U\subset P
such that:

•

\displaystyle U
is downward closed.

•

Whenever
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\}
is a cover of
\displaystyle p
, and for all
\displaystyle i\in I
we have
\displaystyle p_{i}\in U
, then also
\displaystyle p\in U
.

Write
\displaystyle\tau\text{-}\mathrm{Idl}(P)
for the set of
\displaystyle\tau
-ideals.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\displaystyle(P,\tau)
be a locally cartesian
\displaystyle 0
-site. There is a bijection

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})&\cong&\tau\text{-}\mathrm{Idl}(P)\\
F&\mapsto&F^{-1}(1).\end{array}

The inverse sends a
\displaystyle\tau
-ideal
\displaystyle U
to the functor
\displaystyle F_{U}:P^{op}\rightarrow\mathbf{2}
uniquely determined by the condition
\displaystyle F_{U}(p)=1
iff
\displaystyle p\in U
.

The following is a standard fact in locale theory.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\displaystyle(P,\tau)
be a locally cartesian
\displaystyle 0
-site. The poset of propositional sheaves
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})
is a frame, and the inclusion

\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2})

has a left adjoint
sheafification
functor, which is a frame homomorphism; in other words
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})
defines a sublocale of
\displaystyle L(\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2}))
. Identifying the right-hand side with the set of downward closed sets of
\displaystyle P
, the sheafification of a downward closed set
\displaystyle V\subset P
can be computed as the
\displaystyle\tau
-ideal

V^{sh}=\{p\in P~|~\exists\text{ covering }\{p_{i}\leq p~|~i\in I\}\text{ s.t. }p_{i}\in V\text{ for all }i\in I\}.

It is straightforward to verify that
\displaystyle V\rightarrow V^{sh}
is left adjoint to the inclusion of propositional sheaves into presheaves. For the argument that it also preserves finite meets, see
[
Leh25
, Lemma 2.4]
. We will denote the corresponding locale by
\displaystyle L(P,\tau)
.

Example 3.6
.

Let
\displaystyle D
be a lower bounded distributive lattice. A central and canonical example is given by the
finite join
pretopology
\displaystyle fin
on
\displaystyle D
, which is defined by taking the coverings

\displaystyle\{d_{i}\leq d~|~i\in I\}

whenever
\displaystyle I
is finite and
\displaystyle\bigvee_{i\in I}d_{i}=d
. In this case, a
\displaystyle fin
-ideal is a set
\displaystyle U\subset D
such that

•

\displaystyle 0\in U
,

•

\displaystyle U
is downward closed, and

•

whenever
\displaystyle d_{1},d_{2}\in U
, then also
\displaystyle d_{1}\vee d_{2}\in U
.

In other words, what is normally called an ideal of a lattice. We have canonical isomorphisms

\mathcal{O}(L(D,fin))=\mathrm{Idl}(D)\cong\mathrm{Fun}^{lex}(D^{op},\mathbf{2})\cong\mathrm{Ind}(D),

where
\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}(D)
is the filtered colimit completion of
\displaystyle D
, and
\displaystyle\mathrm{Fun}^{lex}(D^{op},\mathbf{2})\subset\mathrm{Fun}(D^{op},\mathbf{2})
refers to the set of finite limit (i.e. finite meet) preserving functors. The locale
\displaystyle L(D,fin)
is a
locally coherent
locale, automatically spatial and locally compact, with its set of compact open subsets given by
\displaystyle D
. For more information, see
[
Leh25
, Section 3]
.

Example 3.7
.

Let
\displaystyle F
be a frame. We can equip it with the
canonical pretopology
given by
\displaystyle\{U_{i}\leq U~|~i\in I\}
whenever
\displaystyle\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}=U
.

For any poset
\displaystyle P
, there is the Yoneda embedding

\begin{array}[]{rcl}y:P&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2})\\
p&\mapsto&\mathrm{Hom}_{P}(-,p)\end{array}

Interpreted as downward closed sets, this corresponds to the assignment
\displaystyle p\mapsto p\hskip-2.15277pt\downarrow~=\{q\in P~|~q\leq p\}
. We can compose this with sheafification to get a functor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}[-]:P&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})\\
p&\mapsto&[p]=(y_{p})^{sh}\end{array}

This functor preserves any finite meets that exist in
\displaystyle P
. We call propositional sheaves of the form
\displaystyle[p]

elementary propositions
. We remark that for a general Grothendieck pretopology, the representable functor
\displaystyle y_{p}
need not be a sheaf, and sheafification is usually necessary. Moreover, whilst the Yoneda functor
\displaystyle y:P\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2})
is always
fully faithful
(by which we mean injective), the same does not need to hold anymore for
\displaystyle[-]
.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\displaystyle(P,\tau)
be a locally cartesian
\displaystyle 0
-site. The Grothendieck pretopology
\displaystyle\tau
is called
subcanonical
if the representable functors
\displaystyle y_{p}
are propositional sheaves for all
\displaystyle p\in P
.

In case of a subcanonical Grothendieck pretopology, the functor
\displaystyle[-]:P\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})
