is fully faithful. As an example, the Grothendieck topology
\displaystyle(D,fin)
discussed in Example
3.6
is subcanonical.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\displaystyle(P,\tau)
be a locally cartesian
\displaystyle 0
-site. Then
\displaystyle\tau
is subcanonical iff
\displaystyle[-]:P\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})
is fully faithful.

Proof.

The Yoneda embedding is fully faithful, so if
\displaystyle\tau
is subcanonical, then
\displaystyle[-]=y
is fully faithful. Conversely, assume
\displaystyle[-]
is fully faithful. We then have for
\displaystyle p,q\in P
a natural equality

\mathrm{Hom}_{P}(p,q)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Sh}}([p],[q])=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{PSh}}(y_{p},[q])=[q](p).

In other words, the functors
\displaystyle y_{p}
and
\displaystyle[p]
agree.
∎

It is tautological that for any downward closed set
\displaystyle V\subset P
we have
\displaystyle V=\bigcup_{p\in V}p\hskip-2.15277pt\downarrow
. Applying the colimit preserving sheafification functor, we obtain the following.

Lemma 3.10

(Co-Yoneda Lemma for propositional sheaves)
.

Let
\displaystyle(P,\tau)
be a locally cartesian
\displaystyle 0
-site and let
\displaystyle U
be a propositional sheaf. Then

U=\bigvee_{p\in U}[p]

in
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})
.

This means that the frame
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})
is generated under suprema (= disjunctions) by elementary propositions. This lemma should not be surprising to readers familiar with topos theory, as it is the analog to the statement that any sheaf is given as a colimit of (sheafifications of) representables.

We have seen that (locally cartesian)
\displaystyle 0
-sites present locales. We are now asking the converse question: If we’re already given a locale
\displaystyle L
, how can we determine a (hopefully simpler)
\displaystyle 0
-site which determines
\displaystyle L
?

Definition 3.11
.

Let
\displaystyle F
be a frame. We call a subset
\displaystyle P\subset F
a
basis
for
\displaystyle F
if
\displaystyle P
is closed under binary meets and for any open
\displaystyle U\in F
there exist
\displaystyle p_{i},i\in I
such that
\displaystyle U=\bigvee_{i\in I}p_{i}
.

It is not hard to check that if
\displaystyle P
is a basis, the canonical pretopology of
\displaystyle F
restricts to a pretopology
\displaystyle\tau
on
\displaystyle P
. In the special case when
\displaystyle F
is given as
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X)
for a topological space
\displaystyle X
, the definition of a basis reduces to the classical definition of a basis. Given a basis
\displaystyle P\subset F
and
\displaystyle U\in F
denote by
\displaystyle h_{U}:P^{op}\rightarrow\mathbf{2}
the propositional sheaf corresponding to the
\displaystyle\tau
-ideal
\displaystyle\{p\in P~|~p\leq U\}
. The following theorem is saying that knowledge of the basis
\displaystyle P
together with its topology determines the locale.

Theorem 3.12

(Basis theorem)
.

Let
\displaystyle F
be a frame and suppose
\displaystyle P\subset F
is a basis. Then there is an equivalence

\begin{array}[]{rcl}F&\cong&\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})\\
U&\mapsto&h_{U}\end{array}.

Variations of this theorem in the context of topoi are well known, where it is also referred to as the Covering Lemma or “Lemme de comparaison”
[
AGV71
, III Théorème 4.1]
. For a proof in the context of posets as is stated here, see
[
Leh25
, Proposition 2.5.]
.

Example 3.13
.

The locale of lower reals
\displaystyle\overrightarrow{[0,+\infty)}
is defined via the following site.

•

Elementary propositions are given by the poset of positive rationals,
\displaystyle(\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0},\leq)
, to be thought of as representing the set
\displaystyle[0,a)
for
\displaystyle a\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
. The symbol
\displaystyle[0,0)
is interpreted as the empty set, or equivalently, the initial object.

•

A rational number
\displaystyle q
is covered by
\displaystyle q_{i}
iff
\displaystyle\sup_{i\in I}q_{i}=q
.

The opens of the locale
\displaystyle\overrightarrow{[0,+\infty)}
can be identified with the set
\displaystyle[0,+\infty]
, as for a real number
\displaystyle a
, we have the corresponding downward-closed set
\displaystyle[0,a]\subset\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
given as

[0,a]=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}~|~q\leq a\}.

The locale
\displaystyle\overrightarrow{[0,+\infty)}
plays a central role in analysis, as maps into it correspond to lower semicontinuous functions. It is locally compact, but not Hausdorff. Sheaves on this locale with value in an
\displaystyle\infty
-category appear naturally as well, e.g. in
[
Efi25
]
the
\displaystyle\infty
-category of such sheaves with values in an
\displaystyle\infty
-category
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}
is referred to as
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}_{\geq 0}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{C})
, and it is shown that
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}_{\geq 0}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{Sp})
is an
\displaystyle\omega_{1}
-compact generator of the
\displaystyle\infty
-category of dualizable stable
\displaystyle\infty
-categories. (See
[
Efi25
, Theorem D.1.]
)

Example 3.14
.

Consider the topological space of real numbers
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}
. It is an easy fact to check that the set of rational, open intervals
\displaystyle(a,b)
for
\displaystyle a<b\in\mathbb{Q}
together with the empty set forms a basis for the topology of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}
. Applying Theorem
3.12
we thus get the presentation of the locale of reals as:

•

The subposet
\displaystyle\mathrm{RatInt}\subset\mathbb{Q}^{op}\times\mathbb{Q}\cup\{\emptyset\}
, spanned by
\displaystyle(a,b)
such that
\displaystyle a<b
together with
\displaystyle\emptyset
, where
\displaystyle\emptyset
is a formally added bottom element.

•

Coverings are given by three cases:

–

\displaystyle(a,b)
is covered by
\displaystyle(a,d),(c,b)
whenever
\displaystyle a<c<d<b
.

–

\displaystyle(a,b)
is covered by
\displaystyle\{(c,d)~|~a<c<d<b\}.

–

The empty set
\displaystyle\emptyset
is covered by the empty covering.

This is for example found in
[
PP12
]
, Chapter XIV, or Johnstone
[
Joh82
]
. Restricting to dyadic rationals or other dense subsets of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}
also works. We leave it to the reader to give the analogous description of a site for
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Example 3.15
.

Let
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
be a
\displaystyle\sigma
-algebra on a set
\displaystyle X
, that is, a sub-Boolean algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{P}(X)
closed under countable unions. Then the poset
\displaystyle(\mathcal{F},\subset)
can be equipped with the
countable cover
pretopology, by declaring

\{U_{i}\subset U~|~i\in I\}

to be a covering if
\displaystyle I
is a
countable
set and
\displaystyle\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}=U
. Denote the resulting locale by
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}
. The locale
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}
plays a central role in the thesis by Jackson
[
Jac06
]
, for example Jackson shows that measures (in the classical sense) on
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
correspond uniquely to (localic) measures on the locale
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}
(see
[
Jac06
, Section 2.1, Theorem 2]
), a result that we obtain later as a special case of Theorem
4.12
.

3.2.
Flat functors and morphisms of sites

Suppose we are given a locally cartesian
\displaystyle 0
-site
\displaystyle(P,\tau)
and a locale
\displaystyle M
. A continuous map
\displaystyle f:M\rightarrow L(P,\tau)

is by definition defined via its inverse image functor

f^{*}:\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})\rightarrow\Omega(M),

which is a frame homomorphism. Since
\displaystyle f^{*}
is colimit preserving, it is in turn completely determined by its composition with the Yoneda functor
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}=f^{*}\circ[-]:P\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(M),
since any propositional sheaf is a supremum of elementary propositions, as seen in Lemma
3.10
. In the following we are concerned with the converse question: When does a functor
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:P\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(M)
define a continuous map
\displaystyle f:M\rightarrow L(P,\tau)
?

Definition 3.16
.

Let
\displaystyle(P,\tau)
be a locally cartesian
\displaystyle 0
-site and
\displaystyle M
a locale. A functor
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:P\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(M)
is called (partial)
\displaystyle\tau
-flat, if it is obtained as
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}=f^{*}\circ[-]
for a (partial) continuous map

\displaystyle f:M\rightarrow L(P,\tau).

We first analyse the situation of extending to the functor
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}
to the category of propositional presheaves
\displaystyle\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2})
. Suppose
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
is a poset with arbitrary suprema and
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:P\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
any functor. We can construct the left Kan extension
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{!}:\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
as the left adjoint to the functor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbf{f}^{*}:\mathcal{E}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2})\\
e&\mapsto&\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{f}(-),e)\end{array}

or equivalently via the formula

\mathbf{f}_{!}(U)=\bigvee_{p\in U}\mathbf{f}(p).

Theorem 3.17

(Universal property of pre-sheaves)
.

Let
\displaystyle P
be a poset, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
a complete lattice. Then the Yoneda functor
\displaystyle y:P\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2})
induces a bijection

\mathrm{Fun}^{L}(\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2}),\mathcal{E})\cong\mathrm{Fun}(P,\mathcal{E}),

with inverse given by
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}\mapsto\mathbf{f}_{!}
. Here,
\displaystyle\mathrm{Fun}^{L}
refers to functors preserving arbitrary suprema. Moreover, suppose
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:P\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is given. Then:

•

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{!}(1)=1
iff
\displaystyle\bigvee_{p\in P}\mathbf{f}(p)=1
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
.

•

Suppose
\displaystyle P
has (binary) meets,
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
is a frame and
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}
preserves meets. Then
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{!}
also preserves meets.

Proof.

It is clear that restriction along Yoneda and left Kan extension are inverse to each other, thus giving the claimed bijection.

The claim that
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{!}(1)=1
iff
\displaystyle\bigvee_{p\in P}f(p)=1
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
is also clear as in
\displaystyle\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2})
we have
\displaystyle 1=\bigvee_{p\in P}p
.

Finally, assume
\displaystyle P
has meets,
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
is a frame and
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}
preserves meets. Let
\displaystyle U,V\in\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2})
. Using that
\displaystyle\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2})
is a frame and that
\displaystyle y
preserves any existing meets, we have

U\wedge V=\bigvee_{p\in U,q\in V}y_{p\wedge q}.

Applying the supremum preserving functor
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{!}
we obtain

\mathbf{f}_{!}(U\wedge V)=\bigvee_{p\in U,q\in V}\mathbf{f}(p\wedge q)=\bigvee_{p\in U,q\in V}\mathbf{f}(p)\wedge\mathbf{f}(q)=\bigvee_{p\in U}\mathbf{f}(p)\wedge\bigvee_{q\in V}\mathbf{f}(q)=\mathbf{f}_{!}(U)\wedge\mathbf{f}_{!}(V)

where we used that meets distribute over suprema in
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
and that
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}
preserves finite meets.
∎

We now discuss how to incorporate Grothendieck pretopologies. Let us introduce with some terminology.

Definition 3.18
.

Let
\displaystyle(P,\tau)
and
\displaystyle(P^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
be two locally cartesian
\displaystyle 0
-sites. Let
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
be a functor. We say that:

•

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}

preserves coverings
if whenever
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p~|~i\in I\}
is a covering in
\displaystyle P
, then
\displaystyle\{\mathbf{f}(p_{i})\leq\mathbf{f}(p)~|~i\in I\}
is a covering in
\displaystyle P^{\prime}
.

•

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}
is a
partial morphism of sites
if it preserves coverings and binary meets.

•

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}
has
global support
if every element of
\displaystyle P^{\prime}
can be covered by elements of the form
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}(p)
for
\displaystyle p\in P
.

•

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}
is a
morphism of sites
if it is a partial morphism of sites with global support.

It is easy to verify that (partial) morphisms of sites compose, hence we obtain the categories
\displaystyle\mathrm{Site}_{\mathrm{part}}
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{Site}
of locally cartesian
\displaystyle 0
-sites with (partial) morphisms of sites.

For the following, note that the inclusion
\displaystyle i_{*}:\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})\subset\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2})
determines a sublocale.

Theorem 3.19

(Universal property of sheaves)
.

Let
\displaystyle(P,\tau)
be a locally cartesian
\displaystyle 0
-site and
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
a complete lattice. Then the elementary proposition functor
\displaystyle[-]:P\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})
induces a bijection

\mathrm{Fun}^{L}(\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2}),\mathcal{E})\cong\mathrm{Fun}^{cov}(P,\tau;\mathcal{E}),

with inverse
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}\mapsto\mathbf{f}_{\#}=\mathbf{f}_{!}i_{*}
, where

\displaystyle\mathrm{Fun}^{cov}(P,\tau;\mathcal{E})
is the sub-poset of
covering-preserving
functors, that is those
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:P\rightarrow\mathrm{E}
such that for any
\displaystyle\tau
-covering
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p~|~i\in I\}
we have

\mathbf{f}(p)=\bigvee_{i\in I}\mathbf{f}(p_{i})\text{ in }\mathcal{E}.

Moreover, suppose such a covering-preserving functor
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:P\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is given. Then:

•

The right adjoint to
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{\#}
is given by

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbf{f}^{\#}:\mathcal{E}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})\\
e&\mapsto&\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{f}(-),e).\end{array}

•

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{\#}(1)=1
iff
\displaystyle\bigvee_{p\in P}\mathbf{f}(p)=1
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
.

•

Suppose
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
is a frame and
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}
preserves meets. Then
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{\#}
also preserves meets.

In particular, if
\displaystyle F
is a frame, then
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:P\rightarrow F
is (partial)
\displaystyle\tau
-flat iff
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}
is a (partial) morphism of sites, where we equip
\displaystyle F
with the canonical topology.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:P\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
be a functor. By Theorem
3.17
we have a unique supremum-preserving extension
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{!}:\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
. Let
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p~|~i\in I\}
be a
\displaystyle\tau
-covering. Then
\displaystyle[p]=\bigvee_{i\in I}[p_{i}]
in
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})
, so if
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{!}
descends to propositional sheaves, we necessarily have that
\displaystyle f
is covering preserving. For the converse, since
\displaystyle i_{*}:\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})\subset\mathrm{Fun}(P^{op},\mathbf{2})
is a sublocale, by Proposition
2.14
the functor
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{!}
descends to
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})
iff for all downward closed sets
\displaystyle V
we have
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{!}(V)=\mathbf{f}_{!}(V^{sh})
. We have
\displaystyle V^{sh}=\{p\in P~|~\exists\text{ covering }\{p_{i}\leq p~|~i\in I\}\text{ s.t. }p_{i}\in V\text{ for all }i\in I\}

hence
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{!}(V)=\mathbf{f}_{!}(V^{sh})
iff

\bigvee_{p\in V}\mathbf{f}(p)=\bigvee_{q:~\exists\text{ covering with }p_{i}\in V}\mathbf{f}(q).

Note that we always have
\displaystyle\leq
for the above line. Now, assume that
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}
preserves coverings, then any
\displaystyle f(q)
appearing in the right hand side we have
\displaystyle f(q)\leq\bigvee f(p_{i})
with
\displaystyle p_{i}
in
\displaystyle V
. Therefore the reverse inequality also holds.

Again by Proposition
2.14
in the case
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{!}
descends we necessarily have
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{\#}=\mathbf{f}_{!}i_{*}
, so the claims about preservation of the top element or meets reduce to the analogous statements made in Theorem
3.17
.
∎

Corollary 3.20
.

Let
\displaystyle(P,\tau)
be a locally cartesian
\displaystyle 0
-site and
\displaystyle M
a locale. There is a bijection

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{part}}(M,L(P,\tau))&\cong&\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathrm{Site}}^{\mathrm{part}}((P,\tau),(\mathcal{O}(M),can))\\
f&\mapsto&f^{*}\circ[-]\end{array}

where the left-hand side refers to partial continuous maps between locales. Under this bijection, the domain of a partial map
\displaystyle f:M\rightarrow L(P,\tau)
is computed as
\displaystyle\mathrm{dom}(f)=\bigvee_{p\in P}f^{*}([p])
. This bijection restricts furthermore to a bijection

\mathrm{Map}(M,L(P,\tau))\cong\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathrm{Site}}((P,\tau),(\mathcal{O}(M),can)).

If we think of our site as given by elementary propositions, this just says that a continuous map is determined by an assignment
\displaystyle f^{*}
which preserves (external) implication, “and” and “or”.

Example 3.21
.

In particular, if
\displaystyle L=\mathrm{pt}
, we obtain a characterization of a point
\displaystyle x
of
\displaystyle L(P,\tau)
. It is a collection
\displaystyle x\subset P
, for which we use the formal symbol
“
\displaystyle x\in p
” whenever
\displaystyle p\in x
, such that:

•

There exists at least one
\displaystyle p\in P
such that “
\displaystyle x\in p
”.

•

If
\displaystyle p\leq q
, we have “
\displaystyle x\in p
” implies “
\displaystyle x\in q
”.

•

If “
\displaystyle x\in p
” and “
\displaystyle x\in q
”, then “
\displaystyle x\in p\wedge q
”.

•

If
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I}
cover
\displaystyle p
, and “
\displaystyle x\in p
”, then there exists an
\displaystyle i\in I
such that “
\displaystyle x\in p_{i}
”.

Example 3.22
.

It is clear that the Yoneda functor

[-]:P\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})

is
\displaystyle\tau
-flat, corresponding to the identity on
\displaystyle L(P,\tau)
.

We now come to the most flexible situation. Suppose
\displaystyle(P,\tau)
and
\displaystyle(P^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
are two locally cartesian
\displaystyle 0
-sites. When does a functor
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
induce a frame homomorphism on propositional sheaves?

Theorem 3.23
.

Let
\displaystyle(P,\tau)
and
\displaystyle(P^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
be two locally cartesian
\displaystyle 0
-sites. Then the assignments

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathrm{Site}}^{\mathrm{part}}((P,\tau),(P^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{part}}_{\mathrm{Frm}}(\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2}),\mathrm{Sh}(P^{\prime},\tau^{\prime};\mathbf{2}))\\
\mathbf{f}&\mapsto&([\mathbf{f}])_{\#}\end{array}

and

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathrm{Site}}((P,\tau),(P^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Frm}}(\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2}),\mathrm{Sh}(P^{\prime},\tau^{\prime};\mathbf{2}))\\
\mathbf{f}&\mapsto&([\mathbf{f}])_{\#}\end{array}

are well-defined. Moreover, for a given (partial) morphism of sites
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
, the right adjoint to

\displaystyle([\mathbf{f}])_{\#}:\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(P^{\prime},\tau^{\prime};\mathbf{2})
is given by

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbf{f}^{\#}:\mathrm{Sh}(P^{\prime},\tau^{\prime};\mathbf{2})&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})\\
F&\mapsto&F\circ\mathbf{f}.\end{array}

In particular, the above proposition states that we have a functor

\mathrm{Sh}(-;\mathbf{2}):\mathrm{Site}_{\mathrm{part}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Frm}_{\mathrm{part}},

and similarly

\mathrm{Sh}(-;\mathbf{2}):\mathrm{Site}\rightarrow\mathrm{Frm}.

Proof.

The claims about well-definedness follow directly from Corollary
3.20
, since for a (partial) morphism of sites
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}
, the composite
\displaystyle[\mathbf{f}]=[-]\circ\mathbf{f}:P\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(P^{\prime},\tau^{\prime};\mathbf{2})
is again a morphism of sites, where we equip
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(P^{\prime},\tau^{\prime};\mathbf{2})
with the canonical topology. The statement about the right adjoint of
\displaystyle([\mathbf{f}])_{\#}
follows directly from the description of the right adjoint provided by Theorem
3.19
together with the Yoneda lemma.
∎

Consider the assignment
\displaystyle F\mapsto(F,can)
where
\displaystyle F
is a frame and
\displaystyle can
is the canonical topology. Since a (partial) frame homomorphism
\displaystyle f^{*}:F\rightarrow F^{\prime}
is the same as a (partial) morphism of sites with respect to their canonical topologies we obtain a fully faithful functor

can:\mathrm{Frm}_{\mathrm{part}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Site}_{\mathrm{part}}

which restricts to a fully faithful functor

can:\mathrm{Frm}\rightarrow\mathrm{Site}.

The following is an immediate consequence of Corollary
3.20
.

Corollary 3.24
.

There are adjunctions

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Site}_{\mathrm{part}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Frm}_{\mathrm{part}}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sh}(-;\mathbf{2})}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces can}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

and

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Site}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Frm}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sh}(-;\mathbf{2})}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces can}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

with fully faithful right adjoints.

Philosophically speaking, this means that the notion of a (propositional) sheaf is in a suitable sense completely determined by the notions of frame and coverage.

4.
Valuations on lattices and frames

Whereas measure theory and classical point-set topology may have many similarities, their theories are more or less parallel to each other - both
\displaystyle\sigma
-algebras as well as topologies are defined as different types of (infinitary) algebraic substructures of
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(X)
for a set
\displaystyle X
. When changing from point-set topology to locale theory however, measure theory integrates extremely well. We begin with the general definition of a valuation.

4.1.
Valuations

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\displaystyle D
be a lower bounded distributive lattice with bottom element
\displaystyle 0
. We define the following terms:

•

A
valuation
on
\displaystyle D
is a functor
\displaystyle\mu:D\rightarrow([0,+\infty],\leq)
such that
\displaystyle\mu(0)=0
and

\mu(p)+\mu(q)=\mu(p\vee q)+\mu(p\wedge q)

for all
\displaystyle p,q
in
\displaystyle D
.

•

A valuation is called
finite
if
\displaystyle\mu(p)<+\infty
for all
\displaystyle p
in
\displaystyle D
.

Let
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:D^{\prime}\rightarrow D
be a homomorphism of lower bounded distributive lattices, and suppose
\displaystyle\mu
is a valuation on
\displaystyle D
.

•

The
pullback

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}^{\#}\mu
of
\displaystyle\mu
along
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}
is defined as
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}^{\#}\mu(d)=\mu(\mathbf{f}(d))
, and gives a valuation on
\displaystyle D^{\prime}
.

•

Suppose
\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}
is a valuation on
\displaystyle D^{\prime}
. Then
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}
is called
valuation-preserving
if
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}^{\#}\mu=\mu^{\prime}
.

Denote the set of finite valuations on
\displaystyle D
by
\displaystyle\mathrm{Val}(D)
. A pair
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
where
\displaystyle D
is a lower bounded distributive lattice and
\displaystyle\mu
a
finite
valuation on
\displaystyle D
is called a
valuation site
. Valuation sites together with valuation-preserving homomorphisms form a category
\displaystyle\mathrm{ValSite}
.

Now fix a lower bounded distributive lattice
\displaystyle D
with a finite valuation
\displaystyle\mu
. We will use the following two simple lemmata.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\displaystyle D
be a lower bounded distributive lattice and
\displaystyle\mu
a valuation on
\displaystyle D
. Suppose
\displaystyle p,q,r\in D
such that
\displaystyle q\leq p
. Then

\mu(p\wedge r)-\mu(q\wedge r)\leq\mu(p)-\mu(q).

Proof.

We compute

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mu(p\wedge r)-\mu(q\wedge r)&=&\mu(p)+\mu(r)-\mu(p\vee r)-\mu(q)-\mu(r)+\mu(q\vee r)\\
&=&\mu(p)-\mu(q)-(\mu(p\vee r)-\mu(q\vee r))\\
&\leq&\mu(p)-\mu(q)\end{array}

since
\displaystyle p\geq q
and therefore
\displaystyle p\vee r\geq q\vee r
.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\displaystyle p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}\in D
s.t.
\displaystyle q_{1}\leq p_{1}
and
\displaystyle q_{2}\leq p_{2}
. Then

\mu(p_{1}\vee p_{2})-\mu(q_{1}\vee q_{2})\leq\mu(p_{1})-\mu(q_{1})+\mu(p_{2})-\mu(q_{2}).

Proof.

We compute

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mu(p_{1}\vee p_{2})-\mu(q_{1}\vee q_{2})&=&\mu(p_{1})+\mu(p_{2})-\mu(q_{1})-\mu(q_{2})-(\mu(p_{1}\wedge p_{2})-\mu(q_{1}\wedge q_{2}))\\
&\leq&\mu(p_{1})+\mu(p_{2})-\mu(q_{1})-\mu(q_{2}),\end{array}

since
\displaystyle p_{1}\wedge p_{2}\geq q_{1}\wedge q_{2}
.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\displaystyle D
be a lower bounded distributive lattice and
\displaystyle\mu
a valuation. Then the subset
\displaystyle(D,\mu)^{fin}
of
\displaystyle D
consisting of those
\displaystyle p
such that
\displaystyle\mu(p)<\infty
is an ideal of
\displaystyle D
, and in particular itself a lower bounded distributive lattice. The valuation
\displaystyle\mu
restricts to a finite valuation on
\displaystyle D
.

Proof.

We only need to check two things:

•

If
\displaystyle p\leq q
, and
\displaystyle\mu(q)<\infty
, then
\displaystyle\mu(p)\leq\mu(q)<\infty
.

•

If
\displaystyle p_{1},p_{2}
such that
\displaystyle\mu(p_{i})<\infty
, then

\mu(p_{1}\vee p_{2})=\mu(p_{1})+\mu(p_{2})-\mu(p_{1}\wedge p_{2})<\infty.

The statement that
\displaystyle\mu
restricts to a finite valuation is obvious.
∎

4.2.
Measure locales

Definition 4.5
.

Let
\displaystyle F
be a frame.

•

A valuation
\displaystyle\mu
on
\displaystyle F
is called
continuous
if

\mu(\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i})=\sup_{i\in I}\mu(U_{i})

for all directed sets
\displaystyle\{U_{i}\}
.

•

A continuous valuation is called a
measure
.

•

An open
\displaystyle K\in F
is said to have
finite measure
if
\displaystyle\mu(K)<\infty
.

•

A valuation
\displaystyle\mu
on
\displaystyle F
is called
locally finite
if there exists a covering of
\displaystyle 1
with opens of finite measure.

•

A valuation is called
faithful
if the functors
\displaystyle\mu(-\wedge K)
are jointly conservative for
\displaystyle K
of finite measure. This means concretely that whenever
\displaystyle U\leq V
are opens, if
\displaystyle\mu(U\wedge K)=\mu(V\wedge K)
holds for all
\displaystyle K
with finite measure, then
\displaystyle U=V
.

Denote the set of measures on
\displaystyle F
by
\displaystyle\mathrm{Meas}(F)
. We call a pair
\displaystyle(F,\mu)
a
measure frame
and a valuation preserving partial frame homomorphism
measure-preserving
. A measure frame
\displaystyle(F,\mu)
will be called
faithful
if
\displaystyle\mu
is locally finite and faithful. Measure frames together with measure-preserving
partial
frame homomorphisms form a category
\displaystyle\mathrm{MeasFrm}
. The opposite category will be referred to as
\displaystyle\mathrm{MeasLoc}
, with objects
measure locales
and morphisms given by partial, measure-preserving, continuous maps.

Remark 4.6
.

We will sometimes use the terminology
chunk
to refer to an open of finite measure of a measure locale, a terminology that would be consistent with
[
Seg51
]
. In case the measure locale
\displaystyle L
is a
probability locale
, i.e.
\displaystyle L
is Boolean and
\displaystyle\mu(1)=1
, it is also sensible to refer to opens as
events
.

Remark 4.7
.

We remark that a measure on a locale
\displaystyle L
can be thought of as a decategorification of the concept of a cosheaf on
\displaystyle L
. The intuition for the definition of a locally finite measure
\displaystyle\mu
is that while an arbitrary open
\displaystyle U
can still have infinite measure, if this does happen, there is a
reason
coming from statements about finite measure below
\displaystyle U
. To be more concrete, let
\displaystyle U
be an open. If
\displaystyle\mu
is a locally finite and continuous valuation, then

\mu(U)=\sup_{K\leq U,\mu(K)<\infty}\mu(K)

is obtained as a supremum of finite values. The intuition for the notion of a
faithful
measure frame comes from the requirement that in practice, one often wants to disregard objects of measure zero.

Some simple observations.

•

A valuation
\displaystyle\mu
on a frame (or more generally bounded distributive lattice) is finite iff
\displaystyle\mu(1)<\infty
.

•

If
\displaystyle\mu
is a faithful valuation, then
\displaystyle\mu(U)=0
implies
\displaystyle U=0
.

•

If
\displaystyle\mu
is a faithful and finite valuation, then
\displaystyle\mu(U)=\mu(1)
implies
\displaystyle U=1
.

Let
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow L^{\prime}
be a partial continuous map of locales and
\displaystyle\mu
a valuation on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(L)
. Define the
pushforward

\displaystyle f_{*}\mu:\mathcal{O}(L^{\prime})\rightarrow[0,\infty]
via

\displaystyle f_{*}\mu(U)=\mu(f^{*}(U)).

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow L^{\prime}
be a partial continuous map of locales and
\displaystyle\mu
a valuation on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(L)
.

•

If
\displaystyle\mu
is a measure, then
\displaystyle f_{*}\mu
is a measure.

•

If
\displaystyle f
is
weakly proper
, by which we mean that
\displaystyle f_{*}
preserves directed suprema, and
\displaystyle\mu
is locally finite, then
\displaystyle f_{*}\mu
is also locally finite.

We note that the requirement for
\displaystyle f
to be weakly proper in the second statement cannot be dropped in general. As a counterexample consider the projection
\displaystyle\pi_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with the domain equipped with the Lebesgue measure.

Proof.

Suppose
\displaystyle\mu
is continuous, and
\displaystyle U=\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}
a directed supremum of opens in
\displaystyle L^{\prime}
. Then

f_{*}\mu(\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i})=\mu(f^{*}\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i})=\mu(\bigvee_{i\in I}f^{*}(U_{i}))=\sup_{i\in I}f_{*}\mu(U_{i}),

in other words
\displaystyle f_{*}\mu
is continuous.

Now assume
\displaystyle f_{*}
preserves directed suprema and
\displaystyle\mu
is locally finite. We know
\displaystyle f^{*}(1)=\bigvee_{U\leq f^{*}(1)~:~\mu(U)<\infty}U
since
\displaystyle\mu
is locally finite. Adjoining over, and using that
\displaystyle f_{*}
preserves directed suprema we have

1\leq\bigvee_{U\leq f^{*}(1)~:~\mu(U)<\infty}f_{*}(U).

But
\displaystyle f_{*}\mu(f_{*}(U))=\mu(f^{*}f_{*}(U))\leq\mu(U)<\infty
, therefore we have obtained
\displaystyle 1
as a supremum of finite measure elements.
∎

Example 4.9
.

Consider a locale
\displaystyle L
and open
\displaystyle U
. If
\displaystyle\mu
is a measure on
\displaystyle L
, then the pushforward along the partial map
\displaystyle j:L\rightarrow U
equips
\displaystyle U
with the
restriction

\displaystyle j_{*}\mu
of
\displaystyle\mu
. If
\displaystyle\mu
is locally finite or faithful, then so is the restriction. The resulting measure-preserving partial frame homomorphism is one of the reasons to consider the category of measure locales and
partial
measure-preserving maps, rather than the more restrictive notion of globally defined measure-preserving maps.

Conversely, one can consider the inclusion of the closed complement
\displaystyle i:U^{c}\hookrightarrow L
. If
\displaystyle\nu
is a measure on
\displaystyle U^{c}
, then
\displaystyle i_{*}\nu
is the measure on
\displaystyle L
given by
extension by zero
. If
\displaystyle\nu
is locally finite, then so is
\displaystyle i_{*}\nu
, but faithfulness is not inherited.

Example 4.10
.

If
\displaystyle x:\mathrm{pt}\rightarrow L
is a point of a locale
\displaystyle L
, we can equip
\displaystyle L
with the
Dirac measure

\displaystyle\delta_{x}
centered at
\displaystyle x
, defined as the pushforward
\displaystyle\delta_{x}=x_{*}\delta
, where
\displaystyle\delta
is the unique measure on the point
\displaystyle\mathrm{pt}
with value
\displaystyle\delta(\mathrm{pt})=1
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\displaystyle f:(L,\mu)\rightarrow(L^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be a global continuous measure-preserving map. If
\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}
is locally finite, then so is
\displaystyle\mu
.

Proof.

Pull back the cover
\displaystyle 1=\bigvee_{U^{\prime}~:~\mu^{\prime}(U^{\prime})<\infty}U^{\prime}
of
\displaystyle L^{\prime}
using
\displaystyle f^{*}
to get a cover of
\displaystyle f^{*}(1)=1
in
\displaystyle L
by opens of finite measure.
∎

Measures on a frame can always be constructed from local data using a basis. Suppose
\displaystyle D\subset F
is a basis that is closed under finite joins. Then any valuation
\displaystyle\mu
on
\displaystyle F
restricts to a valuation
\displaystyle\mu|_{D}:D\rightarrow[0,\infty]
on
\displaystyle D
, and since any open in
\displaystyle F
is obtained as a directed supremum of basic opens in
\displaystyle D
, by applying continuity of
\displaystyle\mu
, we see that
\displaystyle\mu
is completely determined by its restriction. Let us moreover call
\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle D
-finite
if
\displaystyle\mu|_{D}
is a finite valuation when restricted to
\displaystyle D
. We note that the existence of such a basis
\displaystyle D
for which
\displaystyle\mu
is
\displaystyle D
-finite implies that
\displaystyle\mu
is locally finite.

Conversely, suppose
\displaystyle\nu
is a functor
\displaystyle D\rightarrow[0,\infty)
. When does
\displaystyle\nu
extend to a measure on
\displaystyle F
? Note that if
\displaystyle\nu
arose as the restriction of a continuous valuation, we would have the condition that

\nu(p)=\sup_{i\in I}\nu(p_{i})

whenever
\displaystyle p=\bigvee_{i\in I}p_{i}
is a directed supremum in
\displaystyle F
with
\displaystyle p,p_{i}\in D
. But this is by definition just saying that
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p~|~i\in I\}
is a directed covering in the induced Grothendieck pretopology
\displaystyle\tau
on
\displaystyle D
. Call such valuations on
\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\tau
-compatible.

Given a
\displaystyle\tau
-compatible valuation
\displaystyle\nu
, one might try the definition

\nu_{*}(U)=\sup_{d\in D,d\leq U}\nu(d)

for
\displaystyle U\in F
. The next theorem argues that this indeed produces a measure.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
\displaystyle F
be a frame and
\displaystyle D\subset F
a basis closed under finite joins (including the bottom element). Then there is a bijection

(-)|_{D}:\mathrm{Meas}(F)\cong\mathrm{Val}(D,\tau)

where the set
\displaystyle\mathrm{Val}(D,\tau)
is the set of valuations on
\displaystyle D
that are compatible with the induced Grothendieck pretopology
\displaystyle\tau
. The inverse is given by
\displaystyle\nu\mapsto\nu_{*}
. The bijection restricts to a bijection

(-)|_{D}:\mathrm{Meas}(F)^{D\mathrm{-fin}}\cong\mathrm{Val}_{\mathrm{fin}}(D,\tau)

where:

•

The set
\displaystyle\mathrm{Meas}(F)^{D\mathrm{-fin}}
is the set of
\displaystyle D
-finite measures on
\displaystyle F
.

•

The set
\displaystyle\mathrm{Val}_{\mathrm{fin}}(D,\tau)
is the set of finite valuations on
\displaystyle D
that are compatible with the induced Grothendieck pretopology
\displaystyle\tau
.

Proof.

It is straightforward to see that restriction
\displaystyle(-)|_{D}
is well-defined and that the inverse map is given by
\displaystyle\nu\mapsto\nu_{*}
. The main difficulty lies in verifying that
\displaystyle\nu_{*}
is actually a measure for a given
\displaystyle\tau
-compatible valuation
\displaystyle\nu
. We note that it is also clear that
\displaystyle\nu_{*}(0)=\nu(0)=0
and that
\displaystyle\nu_{*}(U)\leq\nu_{*}(V)
whenever
\displaystyle U\leq V
in
\displaystyle F
. We need to show that
\displaystyle\nu_{*}
is continuous and that it satisfies the modularity condition.

Continuous: Suppose
\displaystyle U=\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}
is a directed supremum in
\displaystyle F
. We always have

\sup_{i\in I}\nu_{*}(U_{i})=\sup_{i\in I}\sup_{q\leq U_{i},q\in D}\nu(q)\leq\sup_{p\leq U,p\in D}\nu(p)=\nu_{*}(U).

To see the reverse inequality, note that for a given
\displaystyle p\leq U
with
\displaystyle p\in D
we have

p=\bigvee_{i\in I}p\wedge U_{i}=\bigvee_{i\in I}\bigvee_{q\leq U_{i},q\in D}p\wedge q.

The latter is a directed supremum with elements in
\displaystyle D
. Since
\displaystyle\nu
is compatible with
\displaystyle\tau
, this means that

\nu(p)=\sup_{i\in I}\sup_{q\leq U_{i},q\in D}\nu(p\wedge q)

Passing to suprema over
\displaystyle p
we see the reverse inequality.

Modularity: Let
\displaystyle U,V
in
\displaystyle F
be given. Then
\displaystyle U=\bigvee_{p\leq U,p\in D}p
and
\displaystyle V=\bigvee_{q\leq V,q\in D}q
are directed suprema. Combining them we get the directed suprema

U\vee V=\bigvee_{p\leq U,q\leq V,p,q\in D}p\vee q\text{ and }U\wedge V=\bigvee_{p\leq U,q\leq V,p,q\in D}p\wedge q.

We verify that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\nu_{*}(U)+\nu_{*}(V)&=&\sup_{p\leq U,q\leq V,p,q\in D}(\nu(p)+\nu(q))\\
&=&\sup_{p\leq U,q\leq V,p,q\in D}(\nu(p\vee q)+\nu(p\wedge q))\\
&\leq&\sup_{p\leq U,q\leq V,p,q\in D}\nu(p\vee q)+\sup_{p^{\prime}\leq U,q^{\prime}\leq V,p^{\prime},q^{\prime}\in D}\nu(p^{\prime}\wedge q^{\prime})\\
&=&\nu_{*}(U\vee V)+\nu_{*}(U\wedge V)\end{array}

where we have used that
\displaystyle\nu_{*}
is continuous. To see the reverse inequality, choose
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
, and
\displaystyle p,p^{\prime}\leq U
,
\displaystyle q,q^{\prime}\leq V
with
\displaystyle p,p^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}\in D
such that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\nu(p\vee q)+\epsilon&\geq&\nu_{*}(U\vee V)\\
\nu(p^{\prime}\wedge q^{\prime})+\epsilon&\geq&\nu_{*}(U\wedge V).\end{array}

Define
\displaystyle p^{\prime\prime}=p\vee p^{\prime}
and
\displaystyle q^{\prime\prime}=q\vee q^{\prime}
. Then
\displaystyle p^{\prime\prime}\leq U,p^{\prime\prime}\in D
and
\displaystyle q^{\prime\prime}\leq V,q^{\prime\prime}\in D
. Adding both sides we get

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\nu_{*}(U\vee V)+\nu_{*}(U\wedge V)&\leq&\nu(p\vee q)+\nu(p^{\prime}\wedge q^{\prime})+2\epsilon&\leq&\nu(p^{\prime\prime}\vee q^{\prime\prime})+\nu(p^{\prime\prime}\wedge q^{\prime\prime})+2\epsilon\\
&=&\nu(p^{\prime\prime})+\nu(q^{\prime\prime})+2\epsilon&\leq&\nu_{*}(U)+\nu_{*}(V)+2\epsilon.\end{array}

Since
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
was arbitrary, this completes the proof. (We remark that the proof remains valid even if
\displaystyle\nu_{*}(U\vee V)
or
\displaystyle\nu_{*}(U\wedge V)
have infinite value.)
∎

Example 4.13
.

Let
\displaystyle D
be lower bounded distributive lattice, equipped with the Grothendieck pretopology
\displaystyle fin
. In this situation, all finite valuations
\displaystyle\mu:D\rightarrow[0,\infty)
extend uniquely to locally finite measures
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
on the locally coherent locale
\displaystyle L(D,fin)
, which are finite on compact opens.

Example 4.14
.

An interesting sub-example of the previous example is obtained by letting
\displaystyle X
be a geometry, such as Euclidean space
\displaystyle E^{n}
, the sphere
\displaystyle S^{n}
or hyperbolic space
\displaystyle H^{n}
, of dimension
\displaystyle n
, and considering the associated lower bounded distributive lattice
\displaystyle D(X)
given by
\displaystyle n
-dimensional polytopes. (Compare with
[
Leh25
, Section 7.1]
.) The corresponding locally coherent space
\displaystyle L(D(X),fin)={{X_{\mathrm{poly}}}}
is central for the study of scissors-congruences. The assignment of
\displaystyle n
-dimensional volume
\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{n}:D(X)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
is a finite valuation, hence induces a measure
\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{n}
on
\displaystyle{{X_{\mathrm{poly}}}}
.

Example 4.15
.

Let
\displaystyle G
be a (discrete) group. Then the group
\displaystyle G
is amenable iff there exists a finitely additive
\displaystyle G
-invariant probability measure
\displaystyle\lambda
on the powerset
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(G)
. Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(G)
is a Boolean algebra,
\displaystyle\lambda
is equivalently a valuation on
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(G)
with
\displaystyle\lambda(G)=1
. In this case
\displaystyle L(\mathcal{P}(G),fin)=\beta(G)
can be identified with the Stone-Čech compactification of
\displaystyle G
. We get the result that
\displaystyle G
is amenable iff there exists a
\displaystyle G
-invariant probability measure on
\displaystyle\beta(G)
.

Remark 4.16
.

The requirement that a basis
\displaystyle P\subset F
is closed under finite joins could also be dropped. Since
\displaystyle\mu
is a valuation, i.e.
\displaystyle\mu(0)=0
and
\displaystyle\mu
satisfies modularity, the value of
\displaystyle\mu
on a finite join of base elements is completely determined, hence
\displaystyle\mu
is completely determined by its value on
\displaystyle P
as a functor
\displaystyle\mu|_{P}:P\rightarrow[0,\infty]
. The conditions required for such a functor to then induce a continuous valuation on
\displaystyle F
are slightly more complicated to state. We refrain from doing so since the usage of this more general case is not required for the rest of this paper. The reader is encouraged to work out the necessary conditions themselves in case the need arises.

A measure can be built from local information in the same way a continuous function can. This is summarized in the statement that the set of measures, varied over opens of a locale, forms itself a sheaf. We verify this in steps.

Corollary 4.17
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a locale and
\displaystyle U_{i},i\in I
a directed system of opens of
\displaystyle L
. Suppose
\displaystyle\mu_{i}:\mathcal{O}(U_{i})\rightarrow[0,+\infty]
is a collection of compatible measures, i.e. whenever
\displaystyle U_{i}\subset U_{j}
, then
\displaystyle\mu_{j}|_{U_{i}}=\mu_{i}
. Then there exists a unique measure
\displaystyle\mu:\mathcal{O}(U)\rightarrow[0,+\infty]
such that
\displaystyle\mu|_{U_{i}}=\mu_{i}
.
Furthermore:

•

\displaystyle\mu
is locally finite iff
\displaystyle\mu_{i}
is locally finite for all
\displaystyle i\in I
.

•

\displaystyle\mu
is faithful iff
\displaystyle\mu_{i}
is faithful for all
\displaystyle i\in I
.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle D\subset\mathcal{O}(U)
be the set of
\displaystyle V\leq U
such that
\displaystyle V\leq U_{i}
for some
\displaystyle i\in I
. This set forms an ideal, as
\displaystyle I
is directed, and since
\displaystyle U=\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}
it is a basis for
\displaystyle U
. Define
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}:D\rightarrow[0,\infty]
via
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}(V)=\mu_{i}(V)
for
\displaystyle V\leq U_{i}
. This definition is independent of choice of
\displaystyle i\in I
, since the measures
\displaystyle\mu_{i}
are assumed compatible. It is also clear that
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}
is a
\displaystyle\tau
-compatible valuation on
\displaystyle D
. Define
\displaystyle\mu
as the unique extension, provided by Theorem
4.12
.

For the statement about local finiteness, we can use the same argument by replacing
\displaystyle D
by the set of
\displaystyle V
such that
\displaystyle V\leq U_{i}
for some
\displaystyle i\in I
as well as
\displaystyle\mu(V)<\infty
.

Finally, if
\displaystyle\mu
is faithful, then clearly its restrictions are faithful. Conversely, assume
\displaystyle\mu_{i}
is faithful. Let
\displaystyle V\leq W\leq U
, and assume for all
\displaystyle K\leq U
with
\displaystyle\mu(K)<\infty
we have that
\displaystyle\mu(V\wedge K)=\mu(W\wedge K)
. Then in particular for all
\displaystyle i\in I
and
\displaystyle K\leq U_{i}
with
\displaystyle\mu(K)<\infty
, we have

\mu_{i}(V\wedge K)=\mu_{i}(W\wedge K)

and hence
\displaystyle V\wedge U_{i}=W\wedge U_{i}
for all
\displaystyle i\in I
. But then

V=\bigvee_{i\in I}V\wedge U_{i}=\bigvee_{i\in I}W\wedge U_{i}=W.

∎

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a locale and
\displaystyle U,V
opens of
\displaystyle L
. Assume
\displaystyle\mu_{U}:\mathcal{O}(U)\rightarrow[0,\infty]
and
\displaystyle\mu_{V}:\mathcal{O}(V)\rightarrow[0,\infty]
are two measures on
\displaystyle U
, respectively
\displaystyle V
, such that
\displaystyle\mu_{U}|_{U\wedge V}=\mu_{V}|_{U\wedge V}
. Then there exists a unique measure
\displaystyle\mu:\mathcal{O}(U\vee V)\rightarrow[0,\infty]
on
\displaystyle U\vee V
such that
\displaystyle\mu|_{U}=\mu_{U}
and
\displaystyle\mu|_{V}
. Furthermore:

•

\displaystyle\mu
is locally finite iff
\displaystyle\mu_{U}
and
\displaystyle\mu_{V}
are locally finite.

•

\displaystyle\mu
is faithful iff
\displaystyle\mu_{U}
and
\displaystyle\mu_{V}
are faithful.

Proof.

Define
\displaystyle\mu:\mathcal{O}(U\vee V)\rightarrow[0,\infty]
as

\mu(Z)=\mu_{U}(Z\wedge U)+\mu_{V}(Z\wedge V)-\mu_{U}(Z\wedge U\wedge V)

if
\displaystyle\mu_{U}(Z\wedge U\wedge V)<\infty
, and otherwise as
\displaystyle\mu(Z)=\infty
for
\displaystyle Z\leq U\vee V
.

We verify that
\displaystyle\mu
is a measure:

•

\displaystyle\mu(0)=0
is clear.

•

Monotone:
Assume
\displaystyle Z_{1}\leq Z_{2}\leq U\vee V
and w.l.o.g. that
\displaystyle\mu_{U}(Z_{i}\wedge U\wedge V)<\infty
for
\displaystyle i=1,2
(otherwise we are already done). Then

\mu_{U}(Z_{1}\wedge U)-\mu_{U}(Z_{1}\wedge U\wedge V)\leq\mu_{U}(Z_{2}\wedge U)-\mu_{U}(Z_{2}\wedge U\wedge V)

by Lemma
4.2
, and clearly also
\displaystyle\mu_{V}(Z_{1}\wedge V)\leq\mu_{V}(Z_{2}\wedge V)
. Adding both inequalities we get

\displaystyle\mu(Z_{1})\leq\mu(Z_{2})
.

•

Modularity:
Let
\displaystyle Z_{1},Z_{2}\leq U\vee V
and again w.l.o.g. that
\displaystyle\mu_{U}(Z_{i}\wedge U\wedge V)<\infty
for
\displaystyle i=1,2
. Then

\begin{array}[]{rl}&\mu(Z_{1})+\mu(Z_{2})\\
=&\mu_{U}(Z_{1}\wedge U)+\mu_{V}(Z_{1}\wedge V)-\mu_{U}(Z_{1}\wedge U\wedge V)\\
&+\mu_{U}(Z_{2}\wedge U)+\mu_{V}(Z_{2}\wedge V)-\mu_{U}(Z_{2}\wedge U\wedge V)\\
=&\mu_{U}((Z_{1}\vee Z_{2})\wedge U)+\mu_{V}((Z_{1}\vee Z_{2})\wedge V)-\mu_{U}((Z_{1}\vee Z_{2})\wedge U\wedge V)\\
&+\mu_{U}((Z_{1}\wedge Z_{2})\wedge U)+\mu_{V}((Z_{1}\wedge Z_{2})\wedge V)-\mu_{U}((Z_{1}\wedge Z_{2})\wedge U\wedge V)\\
=&\mu(Z_{1}\vee Z_{2})+\mu(Z_{1}\wedge Z_{2}).\end{array}

•

Continuity:
Let
\displaystyle Z_{i},i\in I
be a directed system such that
\displaystyle Z_{i}\leq U\vee V
for all
\displaystyle i\in I
. Again, w.l.o.g. we may assume that
\displaystyle\mu_{U}(Z_{i}\wedge U\wedge V)<\infty
for
\displaystyle i\in I
. Then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mu(\bigvee_{i\in I}Z_{i})&=&\mu_{U}(\bigvee_{i\in I}Z_{i}\wedge U)+\mu_{V}(\bigvee_{i\in I}Z_{i}\wedge V)-\mu_{U}(\bigvee_{i\in I}Z_{i}\wedge U\wedge V)\\
&=&\lim_{i\in I}\mu_{U}(Z_{i}\wedge U)+\lim_{i\in I}\mu_{V}(Z_{i}\wedge V)-\lim_{i\in I}\mu_{U}(Z_{i}\wedge U\wedge V)\\
&=&\lim_{i\in I}\mu(Z_{i})=\sup_{i\in I}\mu(Z_{i})\end{array}

since
\displaystyle\mu(Z_{i})
is a monotone net.

•

Local finiteness:
Now assume that
\displaystyle\mu_{U}
and
\displaystyle\mu_{V}
are locally finite. Combining coverings of
\displaystyle U
and
\displaystyle V
with elements of finite measure individually produces a covering of
\displaystyle U\vee V
, hence
\displaystyle\mu
is locally finite. The converse statement that local finiteness of
\displaystyle\mu
implies that same for
\displaystyle\mu_{U}
and
\displaystyle\mu_{V}
is clear.

•

Faithfulness:
Lastly, assume
\displaystyle\mu_{U}
and
\displaystyle\mu_{V}
are faithful. Let
\displaystyle W_{1}\leq W_{2}\leq U\vee V
be two opens and assume that

\mu(W_{1}\wedge K)=\mu(W_{2}\wedge K)

for all
\displaystyle K
with
\displaystyle\mu(K)<\infty
. Then by Lemma
4.2
we also have

\mu_{U}(W_{1}\wedge U\wedge K)=\mu(W_{1}\wedge U\wedge K)=\mu(W_{2}\wedge U\wedge K)=\mu_{U}(W_{2}\wedge U\wedge K)

hence
\displaystyle W_{1}\wedge U=W_{2}\wedge U
, and similarly with
\displaystyle V
. But then

W_{1}=(W_{1}\wedge U)\vee(W_{1}\wedge V)=(W_{2}\wedge U)\vee(W_{2}\wedge V)=W_{2}.

and hence
\displaystyle\mu
is faithful. The converse statement that faithfulness of
\displaystyle\mu
implies faithfulness of
\displaystyle\mu_{U}
and
\displaystyle\mu_{V}
is clear as well.

∎

The following theorem is a repackaging of the previous statements. Note that we have a functor

\mathrm{Meas}:\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{part}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Set}

which sends a locale
\displaystyle L
to its set of measures, and a map
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
to its action via pushforward of measures, by Proposition
4.8
. We can furthermore precompose it with the fully faithful inclusion

(\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{open~emb}})^{op}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{part}}

where
\displaystyle\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{open~emb}}
is the category of locales and open embeddings as morphisms, that sends an open embedding
\displaystyle i:U\rightarrow L
to the partial frame homomorphism
\displaystyle i_{!}
, to obtain a functor

\displaystyle\mathrm{Meas}:(\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{open~emb}})^{op}\rightarrow\mathrm{Set}.

Theorem 4.19
.

The functor

\mathrm{Meas}:(\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{open~emb}})^{op}\rightarrow\mathrm{Set}

is a sheaf with respect to the open cover topology, where
\displaystyle\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{open~emb}}
is the category of locales and open embeddings as morphisms. In particular, for every locale
\displaystyle L
, the functor

\mathrm{Meas}:\mathcal{O}(L)^{op}\rightarrow\mathrm{Set}

is a sheaf on
\displaystyle L
. The analogous statements are true with
\displaystyle\mathrm{Meas}
replaced by:

•

\displaystyle\mathrm{Meas}_{\mathrm{loc.fin}}
, the set of locally finite measures, and

•

\displaystyle\mathrm{Meas}_{\mathrm{loc.fin.,faithf}}
, the set of locally finite and faithful measures.

We remind the reader that a sheaf
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
on a locale
\displaystyle L
is a functor
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(L)^{op}\rightarrow\mathrm{Set}
such that the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(0)=\mathrm{pt}
.

(2)

For all opens
\displaystyle U,V
the square

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{F}(U\vee V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{F}(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{F}(U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{F}(U\wedge V)}

is a pullback.

(3)

For all directed systems
\displaystyle U_{i},i\in I
with
\displaystyle U=\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}
the natural map

\mathcal{F}(U)\rightarrow\lim_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i})

is a bijection.

In the case of the functor
\displaystyle\mathrm{Meas}
, Condition (2) is taken care of by Proposition
4.18
. Condition (3) is given by Corollary
4.17
. Condition (1) translates to the statement that the empty set
\displaystyle\emptyset
has exactly one measure on it, which is trivially true.

4.3.
Grothendieck topologies from valuations

In the following section, we will turn Theorem
4.12
on its head. Instead of starting with a Grothendieck pretopology, and looking at compatible valuations, we
start with a valuation
and use it to induce a Grothendieck pretopology.

Definition 4.20
.

Let
\displaystyle D
be a lower bounded distributive lattice and let
\displaystyle\mu
be a finite valuation. The
\displaystyle\mu
-
inner
Grothendieck pretopology
\displaystyle inn
on
\displaystyle D
generated via two cases:

•

Finite unions cover:
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p~|~i\in I\}
is a cover whenever

\bigvee_{i\in I}p_{i}=p

for
\displaystyle I
finite.

•

\displaystyle\mu
-approximations cover:
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p~|~i\in I\}
is a cover whenever
\displaystyle p_{i},i\in I,
is directed and

\mu(p)=\sup_{i\in I}\mu(p_{i}).

Denote the resulting locale by
\displaystyle L(D,\mu)
, called the
inner measure locale
of
\displaystyle D
and the corresponding frame of propositional sheaves by
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu;\mathbf{2})
. If
\displaystyle U
is an open in
\displaystyle L(D,\mu)
, equivalently described as an
\displaystyle inn
-ideal, we define

\mu_{*}(U)=\sup_{p\in U}\mu(p).

We call
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
the
inner measure
.

We note that both types of covering conditions can be combined into the statement that
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p\}_{i\in I}
is a cover iff for all
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
there exists a finite subset
\displaystyle F\subset I
such that

\mu(p)-\mu(\bigvee_{i\in F}p_{i})<\epsilon.

A specific case worth pointing out is that of the inclusion of a single element
\displaystyle\{q\leq p\}
, which is a
\displaystyle\mu
-approximation if
\displaystyle\mu(q)=\mu(p)
.

Proposition 4.21
.

The coverings described in the above definition do in fact define a Grothendieck topology.

Proof.

We need to check stability under pullback and composition. Let us begin with pullbacks. Assume
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p\}_{i\in I}
is a covering and
\displaystyle q\leq p
. Let
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
and choose
\displaystyle F\subset I
finite such that

\mu(p)-\mu(\bigvee_{i\in F}p_{i})<\epsilon.

But then also

\mu(q)-\mu(\bigvee_{i\in F}q\wedge p_{i})<\epsilon

by Lemma
4.2
.

We now check stability under composition. Assume
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p\}_{i\in I}
is a covering and
\displaystyle\{p_{ij}\leq p_{i}\}_{j\in J_{i}}
is a covering for each
\displaystyle i\in I
. Let
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
and choose
\displaystyle F\subset I
finite such that

\mu(p)-\mu(\bigvee_{i\in F}p_{i})<\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

Write
\displaystyle n=|F|
. Then choose
\displaystyle F_{i}\subset J_{i}
finite for each
\displaystyle i\in F
such that

\mu(p_{i})-\mu(\bigvee_{j\in F_{i}}p_{ij})<\frac{\epsilon}{2n}.

Now it follows by induction from Lemma
4.3
that

\mu(\bigvee_{i\in F}p_{i})-\mu(\bigvee_{i\in F}\bigvee_{j\in F_{i}}p_{ij})<\frac{\epsilon}{2}

and hence

\mu(p)-\mu(\bigvee_{i\in F}\bigvee_{j\in F_{i}}p_{ij})<\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}=\epsilon,

in other words
\displaystyle\{p_{ij}\leq p\}
is a covering.
∎

Since the inner topology on
\displaystyle D
always includes all finite covers, we have that propositional sheaves for the inner topology embed into propositional sheaves for the finite topology, as elaborated in Example
3.6
. More specifically we have the adjunction

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu;\mathbf{2})}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sh}(D,fin;\mathbf{2})}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Idl}(D)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Ind}(D).}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{sh}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

In this sense we identify
\displaystyle\mu
-ideals with special types of ideals, namely those that are closed under
\displaystyle\mu
-approximation. The sheafification of an ideal
\displaystyle U
can be directly computed as

U^{sh}=\{p\in D~|~\forall\epsilon>0~\exists q\in U,p\leq q\text{ s.t. }\mu(p)-\mu(q)<\epsilon\}

Theorem 4.22
.

Let
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
be a valuation site. The inner measure
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
is a locally finite and faithful measure on
\displaystyle L(D,\mu)
, and the Yoneda functor

[-]:D\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu;\mathbf{2})

is a valuation preserving lattice homomorphism preserving the bottom element.

Proof.

Since the inner topology contains the finite topology, the Yoneda functor
\displaystyle[-]:D\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu;\mathbf{2})
is a homomorphism of lower bounded distributive lattices. Moreover, it is clear that
\displaystyle\mu(p)=\mu_{*}([p])
. In particular,
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
restricted to the image of
\displaystyle[-]
is a finite valuation. It is also compatible with the induced Grothendieck pretopology, almost by definition. If
\displaystyle\{p_{i}\leq p~|~i\in I\}
is a
\displaystyle\mu
-approximation, then we have

\mu_{*}([p])=\mu(p)=\sup_{i\in I}\mu(p_{i})=\sup_{i\in I}\mu_{*}([p_{i}]).

Applying Theorem
4.12
we see that
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
restricted to the image of
\displaystyle D
extends to a locally finite measure, whose value by definition agrees with
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
.

We still need to argue faithfulness. Let
\displaystyle U\leq V
be two
\displaystyle\mu
-ideals such that

\mu_{*}(U\wedge K)=\mu_{*}(V\wedge K)

for any
\displaystyle\mu
-ideal
\displaystyle K
with
\displaystyle\mu_{*}(K)<+\infty
. If this is the case, we may in particular choose for
\displaystyle p\in V
the
\displaystyle\mu
-ideal
\displaystyle K=[p]\leq V
, and obtain

\mu_{*}(U\wedge[p])=\sup\{\mu(q\wedge p)~|~p\in U\}=\mu(p).

But this means that
\displaystyle p
can be approximated arbitrarily well from below by elements in
\displaystyle U
and hence
\displaystyle p\in U
. We conclude that
\displaystyle U=V
.
∎

Remark 4.23
.

Since
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu;\mathbf{2})
is a frame, it in particular has the top element
\displaystyle 1
, given as the maximal ideal
\displaystyle D
. Thus the valuation
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
is finite on
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu;\mathbf{2})
iff

\mu_{*}(1)=\sup_{p\in D}~\mu(p)<\infty,

which is the case iff the valuation
\displaystyle\mu
on
\displaystyle D
is bounded. A particular special case where this emerges is when
\displaystyle D
is assumed to have a maximal element
\displaystyle 1
, i.e. when
\displaystyle D
is a bounded distributive lattice, and
\displaystyle\mu
is a
probability valuation
on
\displaystyle D
, i.e.
\displaystyle\mu(1)=1
. In this case
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
is again a probability measure on
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu;\mathbf{2})
.

Example 4.24
.

An illustrative simple example is given by considering a set
\displaystyle S
, and taking
\displaystyle P=\mathcal{P}(S)^{\mathrm{fin}}
, the set of finite subsets of
\displaystyle S
, together with the counting measure, i.e.

\mu(M)=\#M

for
\displaystyle M\subset S
a finite subset. Then
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(P,\mu;\mathbf{2})
recovers the powerset
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(S)
of all subsets of
\displaystyle S
, and hence
\displaystyle L(P,\mu)
is the discrete locale corresponding to the set
\displaystyle S
.

Example 4.25
.

Let
\displaystyle S
be a set. The Stone-Čech compactification
\displaystyle\beta(S)
of
\displaystyle S
is obtained by equipping
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(S)
with the finite covering topology, and has as its points the set of ultrafilters on
\displaystyle S

[
Leh25
, Example 3.14.]
. Let
\displaystyle\mathcal{U}
be an ultrafilter on
\displaystyle S
, in other words a point of
\displaystyle\beta(S)
, which can be interpreted as a
\displaystyle\{0,1\}
-valued valuation
\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{U}}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(S)
, see
[
Gol22
, 5]
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{P}(S),\mu_{\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{2})\cong\mathbf{2}
and the induced valuation preserving functor

\mathcal{P}(S)\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{P}(S),\mu_{\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{2})\cong\mathbf{2}

agrees with the flat functor that induces the continuous map
\displaystyle\mathcal{U}:\mathrm{pt}\rightarrow\beta(S)
.

Example 4.26
.

More generally, for a lower bounded distributive lattice
\displaystyle D
, we have that
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu;\mathbf{2})\cong\mathbf{2}
for a valuation
\displaystyle\mu
iff
\displaystyle\mu
is a non-trivial,
\displaystyle\{0,1\}
-valued valuation on
\displaystyle D
. In this case, the natural functor
\displaystyle D\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu;\mathbf{2})\cong\mathbf{2}
can be identified with
\displaystyle\mu
and corresponds to the choice of a point of the locale given by the frame
\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}(D)
.

Example 4.27
.

Consider the lower bounded distributive lattice
\displaystyle([0,\infty),\leq)
, excluding
\displaystyle\infty
. Then the identity is trivially a finite valuation. The corresponding inner locale is given by
\displaystyle\overrightarrow{[0,+\infty)}
, as defined in Example
3.13
.

Example 4.28
.

Consider the Cantor space
\displaystyle C=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{2}
as a model for infinite sequences of coin tosses, and consider the poset of compact opens
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}
of
\displaystyle C
. It is obtained as

\mathcal{O}=\mathrm{colim}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{2}^{n}),

with the maps
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{2}^{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{2}^{n+1})
being given as
\displaystyle\pi_{n}^{*}
, for
\displaystyle\pi_{n}:\mathbf{2}^{n+1}\rightarrow\mathbf{2}^{n}
being the projection onto the first
\displaystyle n
factors. As a filtered colimit of Boolean algebras, it is itself a Boolean algebra. Equipping
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{2}^{n})
with the uniform probability measure
\displaystyle\mu_{2^{n}}
, we see that the maps
\displaystyle\pi_{n}^{*}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{2}^{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{2}^{n+1})
are valuation preserving, and we thus obtain a well defined, finite valuation
\displaystyle\mu
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}
. The corresponding inner locale
\displaystyle\mathrm{Ran}(\mathbf{2})=L(\mathcal{O},\mu)
is a sublocale of
\displaystyle C
, since
\displaystyle C
is obtained from
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}
by using the finite cover pretopology, and all finite covers are contained in the
\displaystyle\mu
-inner pretopology. It has been suggested by Simpson
[
Sim12
]
to call
\displaystyle\mathrm{Ran}(\mathbf{2})
the
locale of random sequences
.
6
6

6

The fact that
\displaystyle\mathrm{Ran}(\mathbf{2})
actually agrees with Simpson’s definition as the smallest sublocale of
\displaystyle C
of measure
\displaystyle 1
can be seen by observing that the uniform measure on
\displaystyle C
becomes faithful when restricted to
\displaystyle\mathrm{Ran}(\mathbf{2})
, compare with
[
Sim12
, Proposition 6.3]
.
The locale
\displaystyle\mathrm{Ran}(\mathbf{2})
is countably based, zero-dimensional, and has no points, and is what Simpson calls
random
: It is equipped with the faithful probability measure
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
. It is however not Boolean, i.e. it does not define a measurable locale in the sense of Section
6
. (See the follow-up discussion in Example
6.15
for an argument.)

In general, the construction
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})
behaves much like a
\displaystyle\mu
-weighted version of the Ind-completion of
\displaystyle D
, something that will be made more precise in Proposition
4.30
. Thinking geometrically, we can also think of the represented locale as a
\displaystyle\mu
-generic point. The next proposition states that a certain converse to Theorem
4.22
holds.

Proposition 4.29
.

Suppose
\displaystyle L
is a locale together with a locally finite and faithful measure
\displaystyle\mu
. Then
\displaystyle L\cong L(\mathcal{O}(L)^{fin},\mu)
, where
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(L)^{fin}
is the sub-poset of opens
\displaystyle U
of
\displaystyle L
such that
\displaystyle\mu(U)<+\infty
.

Proof.

Write
\displaystyle F=\mathcal{O}(L)
for the frame of opens of
\displaystyle L
and
\displaystyle F^{fin}=\mathcal{O}(L)^{fin}
for the subposet of opens of finite measure. By Lemma
4.4

\displaystyle F^{fin}
is an ideal on
\displaystyle F
and in particular a lower bounded distributive lattice, and
\displaystyle\mu
restricts to a finite valuation on
\displaystyle F^{fin}
. Furthermore, since
\displaystyle\mu
is locally finite, we know that

1=\bigvee_{U\text{ s.t. }\mu(U)<+\infty}U,

and thus any element
\displaystyle V
of
\displaystyle F
can be written as a supremum of elements in
\displaystyle F^{fin}
. This means that
\displaystyle F^{fin}
is a basis for
\displaystyle F
and
\displaystyle F
can be obtained from
\displaystyle F^{fin}
together with its induced Grothendieck topology, by Theorem
3.12
. We only need to verify that this topology agrees with the
\displaystyle\mu
-inner topology.

The induced coverings on
\displaystyle F^{fin}
can be split up into two cases:

•

Finite covers:
\displaystyle\{U_{i}\subset U\}_{i\in I}
cover if
\displaystyle I
finite and
\displaystyle\bigvee U_{i}=U
.

•

Directed covers:
\displaystyle\{U_{i}\subset U\}_{i\in I}
cover if
\displaystyle I
filtered and
\displaystyle\bigvee U_{i}=U
.

Finite covers tautologically agree with finite covers in the
\displaystyle\mu
-topology. Now let
\displaystyle\{U_{i}\subset U\}_{i\in I}
be a directed set. Suppose
\displaystyle\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}=U
. Then by continuity of
\displaystyle\mu
we see that

\mu(U)=\mu(\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i})=\sup_{i\in I}\mu(U_{i}).

and hence
\displaystyle\{U_{i}\subset U\}_{i\in I}
is a
\displaystyle\mu
-cover. Conversely, suppose

\sup_{i\in I}\mu(U_{i})=\mu(U).

Since
\displaystyle\mu(U)<+\infty
, by faithfulness of
\displaystyle\mu
we conclude that

\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}=U.

Hence
\displaystyle\mu
-covers are also induced covers.
∎

We now elaborate on the countability condition that locales constructed from valuation sites naturally inherit: Any proposition with finite measure can be obtained by an ascending sequence of basic propositions.

Proposition 4.30
.

Let
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
be a valuation site, and let
\displaystyle U
be a
\displaystyle\mu
-ideal with
\displaystyle\mu(U)<\infty
. Assume
\displaystyle U_{i},i\in I
is a directed collection of
\displaystyle\mu
-ideals such that
\displaystyle U_{i}\leq U
for all
\displaystyle i\in I
. Then

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}=U&\text{ iff }&\sup_{i\in I}\mu_{*}(U_{i})=\mu_{*}(U).\end{array}

Furthermore, for any
\displaystyle\mu
-ideal
\displaystyle U
with
\displaystyle\mu(U)<\infty
there exists an ascending sequence
\displaystyle d_{0}\leq d_{1}\leq d_{2}\leq\ldots
in
\displaystyle D
with
\displaystyle d_{n}\in U
such that
\displaystyle U=\bigvee_{n\in\mathbb{N}}[d_{n}]
.

We remark that the latter statement places a strong countability condition on the measure frame
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})
. We call a given sequence
\displaystyle d_{0}\leq d_{1}\leq d_{2}\leq\ldots
such that
\displaystyle U=\bigvee_{n\in\mathbb{N}}[d_{n}]
an
exhaustion
of
\displaystyle U
.

Proof.

By continuity of
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
, it is clear that if
\displaystyle\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}=U
, then also
\displaystyle\sup_{i\in I}\mu_{*}(U_{i})=\mu_{*}(U)
. We now prove the converse. Note that we always have

\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}\leq U.

Suppose
\displaystyle\alpha=\sup_{i\in I}\mu_{*}(U_{i})=\mu_{*}(U)
, which means that

\alpha=\sup_{i\in I}\sup_{d\in U_{i}}\mu(d)=\sup_{d\in U}\mu(d).

Let
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
and choose
\displaystyle i\in I
and
\displaystyle d\in U_{i}
such that
\displaystyle\alpha-\mu(d)<\epsilon
.

Now let
\displaystyle d^{\prime}\in U
. Then

\mu(d^{\prime})-\mu(d^{\prime}\wedge d)=\mu(d^{\prime}\vee d)-\mu(d)\leq\alpha-\mu(d)<\epsilon.

But this means that
\displaystyle d^{\prime}
can be
\displaystyle\mu
-approximated by elements of
\displaystyle\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}
, hence
\displaystyle\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}=U.

For the second statement, assume
\displaystyle U
such that
\displaystyle\mu(U)<\infty
. By induction define a sequence
\displaystyle d_{n}
of elements of
\displaystyle D
smaller than
\displaystyle U
such that
\displaystyle d_{n}\leq d_{n+1}
for
\displaystyle n\in\mathbb{N}
:

•

Set
\displaystyle\epsilon=1
. Then there exists
\displaystyle d_{1}
such that

\mu_{*}(U)-\mu(d_{1})=\sup_{d\in U}\mu(d)-\mu(d_{1})<1.

•

Assume by induction that we have constructed
\displaystyle d_{n}
such that
\displaystyle\mu_{*}(U)-\mu(d_{n})\leq\frac{1}{n}
. Choose
\displaystyle d^{\prime}
such that
\displaystyle\mu_{*}(U)-\mu(d^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{n+1}
. Define
\displaystyle d_{n+1}=d_{n}\vee d^{\prime}
. We verify that

\mu_{*}(U)-\mu(d_{n+1})\leq\mu_{*}(U)-\mu(d^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{n+1}.

We now have that
\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mu(d_{n})=\mu(U)
, hence by the previous point we conclude that
\displaystyle U=\bigvee_{n\in\mathbb{N}}[d_{n}]
.
∎

Let us add some useful computations in the frame
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})
, viewed as a sublocale of
\displaystyle\mathrm{Idl}(D)
.

Lemma 4.31
.

Let
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
be a valuation site, and
\displaystyle p\in D
. Then:

(1)

\displaystyle[p]=\{q\in D~|~\mu(q\wedge p)=\mu(q)\}.

(2)

The zero object in
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})
is given by the null ideal
\displaystyle N=\{q\in D~|~\mu(q)=0\}
.

(3)

\displaystyle y_{p}\rightarrow N=[p]\rightarrow N=\{q\in D~|~\mu(q\wedge p)=0\}.

(4)

For any ideal
\displaystyle J
we have

\displaystyle J\rightarrow N=\{q\in D~|~\mu(q\wedge p)=0\text{ for all }p\in J\}.

Proof.

Let us prove (1). Since it is clear that

\{q\in D~|~\mu(q\wedge p)=\mu(q)\}\subset[p],

we only need to verify that the left-hand set is downward closed, and closed under finite unions and
\displaystyle\mu
-approximations, since it already contains
\displaystyle p
.

Downward closed:
Suppose
\displaystyle q
is such that
\displaystyle\mu(q)=\mu(q\wedge p)
, and suppose
\displaystyle q^{\prime}\leq q
. Then

\mu(q^{\prime})-\mu(q^{\prime}\wedge p)\leq\mu(q)-\mu(q\wedge p)=0,

by Lemma
4.2
, hence
\displaystyle\mu(q^{\prime})=\mu(q^{\prime}\wedge p)
.

Finite covers:
Suppose we have
\displaystyle q_{1},q_{2}
with
\displaystyle q_{i}
such that
\displaystyle\mu(q_{i})=\mu(q_{i}\wedge p)
. Then

\mu(q_{1}\vee q_{2})=\mu(q_{1})+\mu(q_{2})-\mu(q_{1}\wedge q_{2})=\mu(q_{1}\wedge p)+\mu(q_{2}\wedge p)-\mu(q_{1}\wedge p\wedge q_{2}\wedge p)=\mu((q_{1}\vee q_{2})\wedge p).

\displaystyle\mu
-
approximations:
Suppose
\displaystyle q_{i},i\in I
is a directed set with
\displaystyle q_{i}\leq q
for all
\displaystyle i\in I
, and
\displaystyle\sup_{i\in I}\mu(q_{i})=\mu(q)
. We know that
\displaystyle\mu
-approximations are stable under intersection by Proposition
4.21
therefore
\displaystyle\sup_{i\in I}\mu(q_{i}\wedge p)=\mu(q\wedge p)
as well. But then

\mu(q)=\sup_{i\in I}\mu(q_{i})=\sup_{i\in I}\mu(q_{i}\wedge p)=\mu(q\wedge p).

The statement (2) is a special case of (1). To show (3), by adjunction the statement
\displaystyle[q]\leq(y_{p}\rightarrow N)
is equivalent to

[q\wedge p]=[q]\wedge[p]\leq N,

which is the case iff
\displaystyle\mu(q\wedge p)
.

The statement (4) now follows from (3) by writing
\displaystyle J=\bigvee_{p\in J}y_{p}
, and using

J\rightarrow N=(\bigvee_{p\in J}y_{p})\rightarrow N=\bigwedge_{p\in J}y_{p}\rightarrow N=\{q\in D~|~\mu(q\wedge p)=0\text{ for all }p\in J\}.

∎

Recall that a Grothendieck pretopology
\displaystyle(D,\tau)
is called subcanonical if all representables
\displaystyle y_{p}
are propositional sheaves.

Proposition 4.32
.

Let
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
be a valuation site. The
\displaystyle\mu
-inner topology on
\displaystyle D
is subcanonical iff
\displaystyle\mu
is faithful, i.e.
\displaystyle\mu:D\rightarrow[0,\infty)
is a conservative functor.

Proof.

The
\displaystyle\mu
-inner topology on
\displaystyle D
is subcanonical iff for all
\displaystyle p\in D
we have

\{q~|~q\leq p\}=y_{p}=[p]=\{q~|~\mu(q\wedge p)=\mu(q)\}.

Therefore, if
\displaystyle p\leq q
such that
\displaystyle\mu(p)=\mu(q)
, we also have
\displaystyle q\leq p
, hence
\displaystyle\mu
is conservative.

Conversely, assume
\displaystyle\mu
is conservative. Then
\displaystyle\mu(q\wedge p)=\mu(q)
implies
\displaystyle q\wedge p=q
, which is the case iff
\displaystyle q\leq p
.
∎

Combined with Lemma
3.9
, this means that the natural functor
\displaystyle[-]:D\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})
is injective iff
\displaystyle\mu
is faithful. If this is not the case, we can always enforce faithfulness of
\displaystyle\mu

by taking a suitable quotient of
\displaystyle D
. Define a congruence relation on
\displaystyle D
by

p\sim q~\text{ iff }~\mu(p)=\mu(p\wedge q)=\mu(q)

for
\displaystyle p,q\in D
. We note that by modularity this implies
\displaystyle\mu(p\vee q)=\mu(p)
as well.

Lemma 4.33
.

Let
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
be a valuation site. The relation
\displaystyle\sim
is in fact a congruence relation,
\displaystyle\mu
descends to a faithful valuation on
\displaystyle D/\sim
. Furthermore, the induced quotient functor
\displaystyle(D,\mu)\rightarrow(D/\sim,\mu)
is valuation preserving and induces an isomorphism

\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})\cong\mathrm{Sh}(D/\sim,\mu,\mathbf{2}).

Proof.

We need to show that
\displaystyle\sim
is a congruence relation. Let
\displaystyle p,q,r\in D
and assume
\displaystyle p\sim q
. W.l.o.g. assume that
\displaystyle q\leq p
, otherwise repeat the argument with
\displaystyle q
replaced by
\displaystyle p\wedge q
. Then

\mu(p\wedge r)-\mu(q\wedge r)\leq\mu(p)-\mu(q)=0

hence
\displaystyle p\wedge r\sim q\wedge r
. Now we can use modularity to also deduce

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mu(p\vee r)&=&\mu(p)+\mu(r)-\mu(p\wedge r)\\
=\mu(q)+\mu(r)-\mu(q\wedge r)&=&\mu(q\vee r)\end{array}

hence also
\displaystyle p\vee r\sim q\vee r
. Finally, we need to check that the relation preserves
\displaystyle 0
, i.e. the kernel
\displaystyle\{p\in D~|~p\sim 0\}
is an ideal. But
\displaystyle p\sim 0
simply means
\displaystyle\mu(p)=0
and it is clear that the set of null elements is downward closed and closed under finite joins.

The statements that
\displaystyle\mu
is well-defined and the quotient map
\displaystyle D\rightarrow D/\sim
is valuation preserving are clear. For the last statement, note that the Yoneda functor factors as

[-]:D\rightarrow D/\sim\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2}).

The induced right-hand functor is injective, therefore the claimed statement that

\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})\cong\mathrm{Sh}(D/\sim,\mu,\mathbf{2})

follows from the Basis Theorem
3.12
.
∎

One can also ask in what case one can recover
\displaystyle D
entirely from the associated frame? Note that
\displaystyle[-]
always has image in the set
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})^{fin}
of propositional sheaves of finite measure. However, while faithfulness of
\displaystyle\mu
guarantees that the map

[-]:D\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})^{fin}

is injective, it need not be surjective. Proposition
4.30
gives that the target is given by the closure under suprema of ascending chains
\displaystyle d_{1}\leq d_{2}\leq\cdots
in
\displaystyle D
such that
\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mu(d_{n})<\infty
. We can turn this condition into a definition.

Definition 4.34
.

A valuation site
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
is called
bounded
\displaystyle\sigma
-complete
, if whenever
\displaystyle d_{1}\leq d_{2}\leq\cdots
in
\displaystyle D
is a sequence such that
\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mu(d_{n})<\infty
, the supremum
\displaystyle d=\bigvee d_{i}
exists in
\displaystyle D
and has valuation
\displaystyle\mu(d)=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mu(d_{n})
.

Corollary 4.35
.

Let
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
be a valuation site. The natural functor

[-]:D\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})^{fin}

is an isomorphism iff
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
is faithful and bounded
\displaystyle\sigma
-complete.

4.4.
Functoriality

Let us now investigate the functoriality of the construction of
\displaystyle(D,\mu)\mapsto\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})
.

Lemma 4.36
.

Let
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:(D,\mu)\rightarrow(D^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be a morphism of valuation sites. Then
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}
is a partial morphisms of sites, with
\displaystyle P
and
\displaystyle P^{\prime}
being equipped with the
\displaystyle\mu
-inner respectively
\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}
-inner topology.

Proof.

By definition
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}
is a homomorphism of distributive lattices, and in particular preserves all existing finite limits. Since it is valuation-preserving, it also preserves
\displaystyle\mu
-coverings.
∎

Thus, any homomorphism of
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:(D,\mu)\rightarrow(D^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
of valuation sites extends to a partial frame homomorphism
\displaystyle f^{*}:(D,\mu)^{inn}\rightarrow(D^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})^{inn}
, in the sense that the square

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D^{\prime}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sh}(D^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},\mathbf{2})}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{f}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

commutes, by Theorem
3.23
.

Corollary 4.37
.

There exists a functor

(-)^{inn}:\mathrm{ValSite}\rightarrow\mathrm{MeasFrm}

which sends a valuation site
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
to
\displaystyle(D,\mu)^{inn}=\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})
.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:(D,\mu)\rightarrow(D^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be a homomorphism of valuation sites. We only need to check that
\displaystyle f^{*}:\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(D^{\prime},\mu,\mathbf{2})
preserves the inner valuations. Recall that

f^{*}(U)=\bigvee_{p\in U}\mathbf{f}(p).

We compute that

\mu_{*}(f^{*}(U))=\sup_{p\in J}\mu(f(p))=\sup_{p\in U}\mu(p)=\mu_{*}(U).

∎

We also have a functor

(-)^{fin}:\mathrm{MeasFrm}\rightarrow\mathrm{ValSite}

which sends a frame with measure
\displaystyle\mu
to its ideal of
\displaystyle\mu
-finite elements, see Lemma
4.4
. It is clear that a measure-preserving partial frame homomorphism
\displaystyle f^{*}:F\rightarrow F^{\prime}
restricts to a valuation-preserving homomorphism of distributive lattices
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:F^{fin}\rightarrow(F^{\prime})^{fin}.

Theorem 4.38
.

There is an adjunction

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ValSite}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{MeasFrm}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{inn}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{fin}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

with the counit of the adjunction for a measure frame
\displaystyle(F,\mu)
given by

i_{!}:\mathrm{Sh}(F^{fin},\mu,\mathbf{2})\rightarrow F

induced by left Kan extending the canonical inclusion
\displaystyle i:F^{fin}\rightarrow F
,
and unit of the adjunction for a valuation site
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
given by the Yoneda functor

[-]:D\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})^{fin}.

This adjunction is idempotent, inducing an equivalence of categories

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ValSite}_{\sigma,\mathrm{faithf}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{MeasFrm}_{\mathrm{l.f.~faithf}}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{inn}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{fin}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

where

•

\displaystyle{\mathrm{ValSite}_{\sigma,\mathrm{faithf}}}
is the full subcategory of
\displaystyle\mathrm{ValSite}
given by bounded
\displaystyle\sigma
-complete and faithful valuation sites, and

•

\displaystyle{\mathrm{MeasFrm}_{\mathrm{l.f.~faithf}}}
is the full subcategory of
\displaystyle{\mathrm{MeasFrm}}
given by faithful and locally finite measure frames.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle f^{*}:\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})\rightarrow(F,\nu)
be a measure-preserving partial frame homomorphism, with
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
a valuation site and
\displaystyle(F,\nu)
a measure frame. Then
\displaystyle f^{*}
is determined by the partial flat functor
\displaystyle[f^{*}]=\mathbf{f}:D\rightarrow F
, which factors through
\displaystyle F^{fin}
since
\displaystyle f^{*}
is measure-preserving. In other words, we have a natural injection

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{MeasFrm}}(\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2}),(F,\nu))\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{ValSite}}((D,\mu),(F^{fin},\nu)).

Surjectivity is provided by Theorem
3.19
: A valuation preserving homomorphism of lower bounded lattices
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:D\rightarrow F^{fin}
gives a partial morphism of sites

\mathbf{f}:D\rightarrow F^{fin}\hookrightarrow F,

which extends to partial frame homomorphism

f^{*}:\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})\rightarrow F

. It is straightforward to verify that
\displaystyle f^{*}
preserves valuations, as every propositional sheaf in
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})
is obtained as a directed supremum of basic propositions.

The fact that this adjunction is idempotent follows from the observation that
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu,\mathbf{2})
is a faithful measure frame, and for faithful measure frame
\displaystyle(F,\mu)
, the counit

\mathrm{Sh}(F^{fin},\mu,\mathbf{2})\rightarrow F

is an isomorphism, by Proposition
4.29
.

The statement about the unit being an equivalence iff
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
is faithful and bounded
\displaystyle\sigma
-complete is taken care of by Corollary
4.35
.
∎

5.
Zero-dimensional and Boolean locales

A particular feature that separates measure/probability from point-set topology is that it is a very natural assumption that for any chunk/event a complement exists - Philosophically speaking, if one knows how to measure a chunk
\displaystyle U
of some locale
\displaystyle X
