, then the measure of the complement
\displaystyle U^{c}
must be given as
\displaystyle\mu(X)-\mu(U)
(assuming the measure of
\displaystyle X
is finite) and is thus determined. A similar situation holds for probability theory. Therefore, one would expect (at least classical) measure/probability theory to work best in a locale theoretic context when combined with
Booleanness
. In fact, the ability to work with complete Boolean algebras as topological objects is one of the selling points of the locale theoretic approach, something that is impossible in classical point-set topology. Let us begin with some remarks on Boolean locales.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\displaystyle(D,\leq)
be a bounded distributive lattice and
\displaystyle U\in D
an element. A
complement
of
\displaystyle U
is an element
\displaystyle U^{c}
such that

U\wedge U^{c}=0\text{ and }U\vee U^{c}=1.

It is an elementary exercise to show that in a bounded distributive lattice complements, if they exist, are unique. A bounded distributive lattice is called a Boolean algebra if every element has a complement.

Recall that in a frame
\displaystyle F
, we already have the notion of Heyting implication
\displaystyle U\rightarrow V
, as defined in Section
2.1
.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a locale and
\displaystyle U
an open. The
negation
of
\displaystyle U
is defined as

\neg U=U\rightarrow 0.

We note that by definition, the negation
\displaystyle\neg U
of
\displaystyle U
is the largest open such that
\displaystyle\neg U\wedge U=0
. However, we do not in general have that
\displaystyle\neg U\vee U=1
. The following lemma is a straightforward verification.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a locale and
\displaystyle U
an open. Then
\displaystyle U
is complement iff
\displaystyle U\vee\neq U=1
. In particular the complement of
\displaystyle U
, if it exists is given by
\displaystyle\neg U
.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a locale. We call an open
\displaystyle U
of
\displaystyle L

closed and open
, or abbreviated
clopen
, if
\displaystyle U
has a complement.

The locale of sublocales
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(L)
together with its continuous map
\displaystyle\mathrm{can}:\mathfrak{Sl}(L)\rightarrow L
has a universal property with respect to maps
\displaystyle f:M\rightarrow L
such that
\displaystyle f^{*}(U)
is clopen for all opens
\displaystyle U
of
\displaystyle L
.

Theorem 5.5

(
[
PP12
]
, Proposition 6.3.1.)
.

Let
\displaystyle f:M\rightarrow L
be a map of locales such that
\displaystyle f^{*}(U)
is complemented in
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(M)
for each open
\displaystyle U
of
\displaystyle L
. Then there exists a unique lift
\displaystyle\tilde{f}:M\rightarrow\mathfrak{Sl}(L)
along
\displaystyle\mathrm{can}
.

Of course, the condition on
\displaystyle f:M\rightarrow L
necessary for Theorem
5.5
is automatic if every open of
\displaystyle M
is already clopen. We call this condition
Boolean
.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a locale.

•

\displaystyle L
is called
zero-dimensional
, or also
totally disconnected
, if it has a basis of clopens.

•

\displaystyle L
is called
Boolean
, if every open is clopen.

We will use the same terminology when talking about the associated frame. Denote by
\displaystyle\mathrm{BoolLoc}\subset\mathrm{Loc}
the full subcategory spanned by Boolean locales.

Example 5.7
.

A natural example of a Boolean locale is given by
\displaystyle S^{disc}
, obtained by equipping a set
\displaystyle S
with the discrete topology, or equivalently by considering the frame
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(S)
given by the power set of
\displaystyle S
. More generally, for any topological space
\displaystyle X
, we have the natural continuous map
\displaystyle X^{disc}\rightarrow X
. By Theorem
5.5
this map lifts along
\displaystyle\mathrm{can}:\mathfrak{Sl}(L)\rightarrow L
. We discuss this in Remark
2.22
.

Example 5.8
.

The locale
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(L)
for a locale
\displaystyle L
is always zero-dimensional, but usually not Boolean
[
PP12
, VI 4]
. The basis of clopens is given by sublocales of the form
\displaystyle U^{c}\vee V
, for
\displaystyle U
and
\displaystyle V
open. One verifies that for an open sublocale
\displaystyle U
, the complement is indeed given by the closed sublocale
\displaystyle U^{c}
.

Remark 5.9
.

By definition, Boolean locales correspond to complete Boolean algebras, viewed as frames. The ability to view complete Boolean algebras as space-like objects is unique to locale theory and does not exist when one works in classical point-set topology: A Boolean locale is spatial if and only if it is atomic, in which case the corresponding locale simply corresponds to a discrete set. Boolean locales can be thought of as generalized discrete spaces. They share many separation properties discrete spaces also satisfy. A remarkable fact is that the classical Stone-Čech compactification of a discrete set has a natural extension to all locales, given by sending a frame
\displaystyle F
to the frame
\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}(F)
. When restricted to complete Boolean algebras, this functor produces an equivalence between the category of Boolean locales and
Stonean spaces
, or in other words extremally disconnected compact Hausdorff spaces.
7
7

7

This category has seen a recent surge in interest due to work on condensed/pyknotic mathematics, see
[
Sch19
]
and
[
BH19
]
.
Stonean spaces which are not given as Stone-Čech compactifications of discrete sets are often viewed as mysterious, or also superfluous. When viewing complete Boolean locales as natural extensions of discrete locales, this mystery vanishes somewhat and it becomes true that any Stonean space
is
a Stone-Čech compactification. We remark that Stonean spaces are not themselves Boolean, unless they are finite, a common point of confusion.

Remark 5.10
.

A classical example of a totally disconnected locale that is not Boolean is given by a pro-finite set
\displaystyle S
, also called Stone space, such as e.g.
\displaystyle\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, the Cantor set
\displaystyle C
, and the Stone-Čech compactification
\displaystyle\beta(M)
for a set
\displaystyle M
. In fact, there is a classical Stone duality between (not necessarily complete!) Boolean algebras and pro-finite spaces, see
[
Joh82
]
, also
[
Leh25
, Section 3.2]
, under which a pro-finite set corresponds to its Boolean algebra of compact open subsets, and which extends the mentioned Stone duality between complete Boolean algebras and Stonean spaces mentioned in the previous remark. However, just as with Stonean spaces, pro-finite sets only give examples of Boolean locales if they correspond to finite sets.

Since complements are unique, it is clear that a frame
\displaystyle F
is Boolean iff
\displaystyle\neg:F^{op}\rightarrow F
is an isomorphism. This self-duality implies the following.

Lemma 5.11
.

A Boolean frame is automatically a coframe. A Boolean coframe is automatically a frame.

An
atom
in a Boolean frame is defined to be a minimal non-zero element. This gives a direct characterization of the set of points of a Boolean locale.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a Boolean locale. Then there is a bijection

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathrm{pts}(L)&\cong&\mathrm{Atom}(\mathcal{O}(L))\\
P&\mapsto&\bigwedge P\end{array}

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{O}(L)
be a minimal non-zero open. Then
\displaystyle P=\{U\in\mathcal{O}(L)~|~x\leq U\}
is clearly a proper filter. It is completely prime: If
\displaystyle x\leq\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}
we have

0\neq x=\bigvee_{i\in I}x\wedge U_{i}.

Minimality of
\displaystyle x
means that all the elements
\displaystyle x\wedge U_{i}
are either
\displaystyle 0
or
\displaystyle x
, therefore at least
\displaystyle x\wedge U_{i}\neq 0
for at least one
\displaystyle i\in I
.

Conversely, let
\displaystyle P
be a completely prime filter. Define
\displaystyle x=\bigwedge P
. We claim that
\displaystyle x\in P
, which would imply that
\displaystyle P
is principal, generated by
\displaystyle x
. Assume not. Since
\displaystyle x\vee\neg x=1\in P
, we then would have
\displaystyle\bigvee_{U\in P}\neg U=\neg x\in P
. Since
\displaystyle P
is completely prime, we therefore find
\displaystyle U\in P
such that
\displaystyle\neg U\in P
. But then
\displaystyle 0=U\wedge\neg U\in P
, which cannot be.
∎

Furthermore, the description of implication in a Boolean locale simplifies.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a Boolean locale and
\displaystyle U,V
opens. Then
\displaystyle U\rightarrow V=\neg U\vee V
.

Proof.

We claim that the functors
\displaystyle U\rightarrow-
and
\displaystyle\neg U\vee-
agree, for which we equivalently need to show that

U\wedge W\leq V~\text{ iff }~W\leq\neg U\vee V

for all
\displaystyle V,W
open. First assume
\displaystyle U\wedge W\leq V
holds. Apply
\displaystyle\neg U\vee-
to this inequality to obtain

W\leq\neg U\vee W=\neg U\vee(U\wedge W)\leq\neg U\vee V.

Now for the converse assume
\displaystyle W\leq\neg U\vee V
holds. Apply
\displaystyle U\wedge-
to obtain

U\wedge W\leq U\wedge(\neg U\vee V)=U\wedge V\leq V.

Hence both statements are equivalent.
∎

Lemma 5.14
.

Any sublocale of a Boolean locale is Boolean.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle i_{*}:L\rightarrow M
be an embedding, with
\displaystyle M
Boolean. Let
\displaystyle U\in L
. Then
\displaystyle i_{*}(U)
has a complement
\displaystyle V
in
\displaystyle M
, i.e.
\displaystyle i_{*}(U)\wedge V=0
and

\displaystyle i_{*}(U)\vee V=1
. Applying
\displaystyle i^{*}
and using
\displaystyle i^{*}i_{*}(U)=U
gives that
\displaystyle i^{*}(V)
is the complement to
\displaystyle U
.
∎

Perhaps surprisingly, Boolean locales can be generated fairly easily. As an example, to every locale
\displaystyle L
one can associate the
smallest dense sublocale

\displaystyle L_{\neg\neg}\hookrightarrow L
given by

\{\neg U~|~U\in F\}\subset F

where
\displaystyle F
is the frame of
\displaystyle L
, see
[
PP12
, III.8.3]
.
8
8

8

Also compare with Theorem 3 in
[
MM92
]
in case of topoi.
To see that this is in fact a sublocale, use Lemma
2.13
and Proposition
2.3
:

•

\displaystyle\{\neg U~|~U\in F\}
is closed under arbitrary infima, as

\bigwedge_{i\in I}(U_{i}\rightarrow 0)=(\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i})\rightarrow 0.

•

\displaystyle\{\neg U~|~U\in F\}
is closed under implication, as

V\rightarrow(U\rightarrow 0)=(V\wedge U)\rightarrow 0.

The associated nucleus is given by
\displaystyle U\mapsto\neg\neg U
. We note that this means in particular that
\displaystyle\neg\neg\neg U=\neg U
for any open
\displaystyle U
. Also, suprema of opens in
\displaystyle L_{\neg\neg}
are computed by applying
\displaystyle\neg\neg
to their suprema in
\displaystyle L
. The sublocale
\displaystyle L_{\neg\neg}
is in fact Boolean, which can be verified directly.

•

Applying
\displaystyle\neg\neg
to
\displaystyle\neg U\vee U
we get that

\neg\neg(\neg U\vee U)=\neg(\neg U\wedge\neg\neg U)=\neg(0)=1.

•

We also have

\displaystyle\neg\neg U\wedge\neg U=0

by definition.

Hence
\displaystyle\neg\neg U
is the complement of
\displaystyle\neg U
in
\displaystyle L_{\neg\neg}
. (But not necessarily in
\displaystyle L
!)

We can generalize this example quite a bit. Recall that given any open
\displaystyle N
, one can associate the inclusion of the closed complement
\displaystyle N^{c}\hookrightarrow L
, see Example
2.10
given on the level of frames as

\{V\in F~|~N\leq V\}\subset F,

which treats
\displaystyle N
as the new bottom element. We can compose these two inclusions to get the sublocale

b(N)=(N^{c})_{\neg\neg}\hookrightarrow L.

Note that the frame for
\displaystyle b(N)
is simply given as

\{U\rightarrow N~|~U\in F\}\subset F,

because by Proposition
2.3
(4), any element of the form
\displaystyle U\rightarrow N
automatically lives above
\displaystyle N
. Since any sublocale needs to be closed under implication, we also see that
\displaystyle b(N)
is equivalently the smallest sublocale containing
\displaystyle N
. In fact, this type of example is the only example for a Boolean sublocale.

Proposition 5.15

(
[
PP12
]
, III.10.4)
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a locale and
\displaystyle N
an open in
\displaystyle L
. Then
\displaystyle b(N)\rightarrow L
is a Boolean sublocale of
\displaystyle L
. Conversely, any Boolean sublocale of
\displaystyle L
is of the form
\displaystyle b(N)
for some open
\displaystyle N
.

Proof.

Let us reproduce the argument presented in
[
PP12
]
for completeness. We have seen that
\displaystyle b(N)
is a Boolean sublocale. For the converse, suppose
\displaystyle S\subset F
describes a Boolean sublocale of
\displaystyle L
. Let
\displaystyle N=\bigwedge S
, which is an element of
\displaystyle S
by closure under infima. It is clear that
\displaystyle b(N)\subset S
, since
\displaystyle S
is closed under implication. Conversely, if
\displaystyle U\in S
, since
\displaystyle S
is Boolean we have
\displaystyle U=(U\rightarrow N)\rightarrow N
, and so we see that
\displaystyle U\in b(N)
.
∎

If
\displaystyle L
is in particular a Boolean locale, then every sublocale is of the form
\displaystyle b(N)\hookrightarrow L
for some open
\displaystyle N
. But we can identify
\displaystyle b(N)=\{U\rightarrow N\}=\{\neg U\vee N\}

with the closed sublocale given by
\displaystyle N^{c}
.

Corollary 5.16
.

Suppose
\displaystyle L
is a Boolean locale. Then all sublocales of
\displaystyle L
are given by open, or equivalently closed, inclusions, i.e. the natural map

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(L)\rightarrow L
is an isomorphism.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a locale and
\displaystyle U,N
opens of
\displaystyle L
. Then the square

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b(U\wedge N)}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b(N)}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}

\displaystyle\lrcorner

is a pullback square of embeddings.

Proof.

By using that

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U\wedge N)^{c}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{c}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}

\displaystyle\lrcorner

is a pullback square, we can reduce the proof to the case of
\displaystyle N=0
. Since pullbacks of open embeddings are open embeddings, we have the pushout square of frames

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(L_{\neg\neg})_{\neg\neg U}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(L_{\neg\neg})}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(L)_{U}^{i^{*}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(L)^{j^{*}}}

Then
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(L_{\neg\neg})_{\neg\neg U}
is a sublocale of the Boolean locale
\displaystyle L_{\neg\neg}
, hence it is itself Boolean, and therefore of the form
\displaystyle b(i_{*}(0))
. The functor
\displaystyle i^{*}
is given by the restriction of
\displaystyle j^{*}=\neg\neg
and hence
\displaystyle i_{*}=j_{*}\wedge U
. But then
\displaystyle i_{*}(0)=j_{*}(0)\wedge U=0\wedge U=0
, and therefore
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(L_{\neg\neg})_{\neg\neg U}\cong\mathcal{O}(U_{\neg\neg})
, which is what we wanted to show.
∎

Let us now come to maps between Boolean locales.

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
be a continuous map between Boolean locales. Then
\displaystyle f
is open.

Proof.

First observe that
\displaystyle f^{*}
preserves negation: Let
\displaystyle U
be an open in
\displaystyle M
. Applying
\displaystyle f^{*}
to the equations

\begin{array}[]{rl}U\vee\neg U=1&\text{ and}\\
U\wedge\neg U=0\end{array}

we get

\begin{array}[]{rl}f^{*}(U)\vee f^{*}(\neg U)=1&\text{ and}\\
f^{*}(U)\wedge f^{*}(\neg U)=0\end{array}

and hence
\displaystyle f^{*}(\neg U)=\neg f^{*}(U)
. We can now verify that
\displaystyle f^{*}
preserves infima and implications.

f^{*}(\bigwedge_{i\in I}U_{i})=f^{*}(\neg\bigvee_{i\in I}\neg U_{i})=\neg\bigvee_{i\in I}\neg f^{*}(U_{i})=\bigwedge_{i\in I}f^{*}(U_{i}),

and

f^{*}(U\rightarrow V)=f^{*}(\neg U\vee V)=\neg f^{*}(U)\vee f^{*}(V)=f^{*}(U)\rightarrow f^{*}(V),

by Lemma
5.13
.
∎

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
be an open map between locales. Then
\displaystyle f
descends to a well-defined map on double negation sublocales, i.e. there is a commuting square

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L_{\neg\neg}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{\neg\neg}}.}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}

If this is the case, we have
\displaystyle\tilde{f}^{*}=\neg\neg f^{*}
.

Proof.

We need to show that
\displaystyle\neg\neg f^{*}\neg\neg=\neg\neg f^{*}
. Since
\displaystyle f
is open, the functor
\displaystyle f^{*}
preserves implication and
\displaystyle 0
, hence in particular negation. Therefore we have

\neg\neg f^{*}\neg\neg=\neg\neg\neg\neg f^{*}=\neg\neg f^{*},

since triple negation is the same as negating once.
∎

Remark 5.20
.

The condition for a map
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
to descend to double negation sublocales is slightly weaker than being open and called
skeletal
. Skeletal maps were studied originally in
[
BP94
]
. See also
[
Joh06
]
, in particular Section 3.

Theorem 5.21
.

There exists an adjunction

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{open}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{BoolLoc}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)_{\neg\neg}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

with the left adjoint given by the inclusion of the full subcategory of Boolean locales, and the right adjoint being given by
\displaystyle L\mapsto(L)_{\neg\neg}\hookrightarrow L
.

Proof.

The left adjoint is well-defined by Lemma
5.18
. The adjunction follows directly from Lemma
5.19
.
∎

We now try to integrate markings into the picture. Recall the definition of a marked locale
\displaystyle(L,U)
given in Definition
2.26
, as a locale
\displaystyle L
together with a choice of open
\displaystyle U
.

Corollary 5.22
.

Let
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
be an open map between locales and
\displaystyle N
an open of
\displaystyle M
. Then
\displaystyle f
induces a well-defined map

\tilde{f}:b(f^{*}(N))\rightarrow b(N).

The inverse image part is given by the formula
\displaystyle\tilde{f}^{*}(U)=f^{*}((U\rightarrow N)\rightarrow N)=(f^{*}(U)\rightarrow f^{*}(N))\rightarrow f^{*}(N)
.

Proof.

The claim follows at once by combining Lemma
2.25
, to get a well-defined open restriction
\displaystyle f|:f^{*}(N^{c})\rightarrow N^{c}
, and Lemma
5.19
, to descend further to double negation sublocales. The claimed formula follows from the observation that negation on the closed sublocale
\displaystyle f^{*}(N)^{c}
is given by
\displaystyle-\rightarrow f^{*}(N)
.
∎

We summarize the above statement into the existence of a useful functor for constructing Boolean locales.

Corollary 5.23
.

There exists an adjunction

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{MarkLoc}_{\mathrm{open}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{BoolLoc}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-,0)}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

with the left adjoint
\displaystyle L\mapsto(L,0)
obtained by equipping a Boolean locale with the marking given by the bottom element
\displaystyle 0
, and the right adjoint being given by
\displaystyle(L,U)\mapsto b(U)=(U^{c})_{\neg\neg}\hookrightarrow L
. Furthermore, the left adjoint is fully faithful.

Proof.

The adjunction is obtained by composing the adjunction given by Theorem
2.27
with the adjunction provided by Theorem
5.21

Fully faithfulness of the left adjoint follows because it is obtained by a composition of fully faithful left adjoints.
∎

5.1.
The analogy between topological spaces and matrix groups

The following is a side remark that will not play a role in the rest of the text, other than (potentially) aid the reader in intuition. Since the usage of locales might seem foreign to a reader educated in classical point-set topology, we want to highlight an analogy between the theory of groups and the theory of locales. While it is not true that the functor
\displaystyle\mathrm{Loc}:\mathrm{Top}\rightarrow\mathrm{Loc}
that associates to a topological space
\displaystyle X
its corresponding frame of opens is fully faithful, it
is
true that the functor
\displaystyle\mathrm{Top}\rightarrow\mathrm{Loc}^{\rightarrow}
into the category of maps between locales, that sends a topological space
\displaystyle X
to the map

\mathrm{Loc}(X^{\mathrm{disc}})\rightarrow\mathrm{Loc}(X)

is fully faithful. In other words the information of a topological space can be recovered from its set of opens, together with the information on how it embeds into the frame
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X^{\mathrm{disc}})=\mathcal{P}(X)
.
The functor
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(X)
is by definition an injective frame homomorphism, or equivalently a quotient map of locales.

This is analogous to how one would define a
matrix group
: As a subgroup
\displaystyle G\subset\mathrm{GL}_{n}(k)
. If, under this analogy, we think of general linear groups as analogous to Boolean locales, we get the following table of corresponding terms.

Group theory

Topology

Group
\displaystyle G

Locale
\displaystyle L

Presentation of
\displaystyle G\cong\left\langle S~|~R\right\rangle

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(L)\cong\mathrm{Sh}(P,\tau;\mathbf{2})

via generators and relations

for Grothendieck pretopology
\displaystyle(P,\tau)

Representation
\displaystyle G\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{n}(k)

Map
\displaystyle M\rightarrow L
with
\displaystyle M
Boolean

Zero representation
\displaystyle G\rightarrow 0

Inclusion of empty locale
\displaystyle\emptyset\rightarrow L

One-dimensional representation
\displaystyle G\rightarrow k^{\times}

Point
\displaystyle\mathrm{pt}\rightarrow L

Faithful representation
\displaystyle G\hookrightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{n}(k)

Quotient map
\displaystyle M\twoheadrightarrow L

Matrix group
\displaystyle G\subset\mathrm{GL}_{n}(k)

Topological space
\displaystyle X^{disc}\twoheadrightarrow X

Regular representation
\displaystyle G\rightarrow k[G]^{\times}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(L)_{\neg\neg}\twoheadrightarrow L

In the upcoming sections, particularly Section
8
, we use nice enough valuations on a class of spaces to construct maps
\displaystyle X^{\mu}\rightarrow X
, with
\displaystyle X^{\mu}
being a Boolean locale. This can be thought of as giving a
different representation
of
\displaystyle X
, which encapsulates the measure theoretic rather than point-set topological aspects of
\displaystyle X
.

6.
Measurable locales

In this section we will describe a class of locales which have the right properties for classical measure and integration theory: Measurable locales. These are defined to be Boolean locales that allow enough measures to distinguish opens. The category of measurable locales is anti-equivalent via a variant of Gelfand duality to the category of commutative von Neumann algebras, which is a major reason for the usefulness of the point-free approach to measure theory.

In this section, we will use the word
chunk
instead of open. Readers with an eye towards probability theory may also want to read the word
chunk
as
event
.

Definition 6.1
.

A Boolean locale
\displaystyle L
is called
measurable
if one of the following equivalent statements holds:

(1)

There exists a locally finite and faithful measure
\displaystyle\mu
on
\displaystyle L
.

(2)

\displaystyle 1
is the supremum over chunks
\displaystyle a
such that
\displaystyle a
admits a finite faithful measure.

(3)

For any
\displaystyle b\in L,b\neq 0
there exists a finite measure
\displaystyle\mu
on
\displaystyle L
such that
\displaystyle\mu(b)\neq 0
.

We define
\displaystyle\mathrm{MblLoc}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Loc}
as the full subcategory spanned by measurable locales.

We note that the statement that either of (2) or (3) imply (1) is non-trivial and requires the axiom of choice. Before we give the proof of the equivalence of the three conditions, we record two easy lemmata about measures on Boolean locales.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\displaystyle B
be a complete Boolean algebra. A locally finite measure
\displaystyle\mu
on
\displaystyle B
is faithful iff the condition

\mu(U)=0\text{ iff }U=0

holds.

Proof.

Suppose
\displaystyle\mu
is faithful, and let
\displaystyle\mu(U)=0
. Since
\displaystyle 0\leq U
are both opens with finite measure, we conclude
\displaystyle U=0
. Conversely, suppose

\mu(U)=0\text{ iff }U=0

holds. Now let
\displaystyle V\leq V^{\prime}
with
\displaystyle\mu(V)=\mu(V^{\prime})<+\infty
. By Booleanness we have

V\vee(V^{\prime}\setminus V)=V^{\prime}

where
\displaystyle V^{\prime}\setminus V=V^{\prime}\wedge\neg V
. But then by modularity we have
\displaystyle\mu(V^{\prime}\setminus V)=0
, therefore
\displaystyle V^{\prime}\setminus V=0
, and hence
\displaystyle V=V^{\prime}
. Now let
\displaystyle V\leq V^{\prime}
with

\mu(V\wedge K)=\mu(V^{\prime}\wedge K).

Use continuity of
\displaystyle\mu
together with local finiteness, we can reduce to the case of finite measure and conclude
\displaystyle V=V^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

A basic observation is that for a Boolean locale
\displaystyle L
with faithful measure
\displaystyle\mu
, all points
\displaystyle x
of
\displaystyle L
(identified with atoms of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(L)
under Lemma
5.12
) need to satisfy
\displaystyle\mu(x)>0
. This gives a direct criterion that shows that many useful measurable locales in practice cannot be spatial.

The presence of Booleanness also allows one to deduce statements about co-continuity of measures from continuity.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a Boolean locale and
\displaystyle\mu
a measure on
\displaystyle L
. Let
\displaystyle U_{i},i\in I,
be a downwards directed system of chunks such that there exists
\displaystyle i_{0}\in I
such that
\displaystyle\mu(U_{i_{0}})<\infty
. Then

\mu(\bigwedge U_{i})=\inf_{i\in I}\mu(U_{i}).

Proof.

Write
\displaystyle m=\mu(U_{i_{0}})<\infty
. Using directedness, we may assume w.l.o.g. that
\displaystyle U_{i}\leq U_{i_{0}}
for all
\displaystyle i\in I
, and therefore
\displaystyle\mu(U_{i})\leq m
for all
\displaystyle i\in I
. Then compute

\mu(\bigwedge_{i\in I}U_{i})+\mu(\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i_{0}}\setminus U_{i})=m=\mu(U_{i})+\mu(U_{i_{0}}\setminus U_{i})

for all
\displaystyle i\in I
, therefore

\mu(\bigwedge_{i\in I}U_{i})+\mu(\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i_{0}}\setminus U_{i})=\inf_{i\in I}\mu(U_{i})+\sup_{i\in I}\mu(U_{i_{0}}\setminus U_{i}).

Hence
\displaystyle\mu(\bigwedge U_{i})=\inf_{i\in I}\mu(U_{i})
follows from continuity of
\displaystyle\mu
.
∎

Theorem 6.5
.

The three conditions stated in Definition
6.1
are equivalent.

Proof.

Condition (1) implies (2): This is clear, as local finiteness of
\displaystyle\mu
means that
\displaystyle 1
can be obtained as a supremum of chunks
\displaystyle b
of finite measure, and
\displaystyle\mu
restricts to a finite faithful measure on
\displaystyle b
.

Condition (2) implies (1):
Consider the poset
\displaystyle P
given by pairs
\displaystyle(a,\mu_{a})
, where
\displaystyle a
is a chunk of
\displaystyle L
, and
\displaystyle\mu_{a}
is a locally finite and faithful measure on
\displaystyle a
, with
\displaystyle(a,\mu_{a})\leq(a^{\prime},\mu_{a}^{\prime})
if
\displaystyle a\leq a^{\prime}
and the restriction of
\displaystyle\mu_{a}^{\prime}
to
\displaystyle a
agrees with
\displaystyle\mu_{a}
. This poset is non-empty, as otherwise
\displaystyle 1
would not be the supremum of all
\displaystyle a
such that
\displaystyle a
admit a finite faithful measure. Let
\displaystyle(a_{i},\mu_{a_{i}})_{i\in I}
be a chain in
\displaystyle P
. Define
\displaystyle a=\bigvee_{i\in I}a_{i}
, and
\displaystyle\mu_{a}(b)=\sup_{i\in I}\mu_{a_{i}}(b\wedge a_{i})
. Then
\displaystyle\mu_{a}
is again a locally finite and faithful measure, by Corollary
4.17
. Now use Zorn’s Lemma to obtain a maximal element
\displaystyle(a,\mu_{a})
of
\displaystyle P
. Suppose
\displaystyle a\neq 1
. Since
\displaystyle 1
is obtained as the supremum of
\displaystyle b
such that
\displaystyle b
admits a finite faithful measure, the same is true for
\displaystyle\neg a\neq 0
. (Here we have used Booleanness.) Choose
\displaystyle b\leq\neg a,b\neq 0
together with a finite faithful measure
\displaystyle\mu_{b}
on
\displaystyle b
. Then
\displaystyle(a\vee b,\mu)
with
\displaystyle\mu(c)=\mu_{a}(c\wedge a)+\mu_{b}(c\wedge b)
for
\displaystyle c\leq a\vee b
satisfies
\displaystyle(a,\mu_{a})\leq(a\vee b,\mu)
in
\displaystyle P
, a contradiction to maximality.

Condition (2) and (3) are equivalent: This is treated in
[
Pav22
, Lemma 2.53]
.
∎

Aside from the obvious analogy with measurable spaces, another justification for calling these locales measurable comes from the following theorem. Given a locale
\displaystyle L
, we define the ring of bounded functions

C_{b}(L;\mathbb{C})=\mathrm{colim}_{r\rightarrow+\infty}\mathrm{Map}(L,B_{r}(0)),

where
\displaystyle B_{r}(0)\subset\mathbb{C}
is the disc of radius
\displaystyle r>0
centered at the origin. We obtain a functor

C_{b}:\mathrm{Loc}^{op}\rightarrow*\mathrm{CAlg},

where
\displaystyle*\mathrm{CAlg}
is the category of commutative
\displaystyle*
-algebras over
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
. We call the restriction of this functor to the category of measurable locales

L^{\infty}:\mathrm{MblLoc}^{op}\rightarrow*\mathrm{CAlg}.

We remark on the difference to classical measure theory: Whereas for a measure space
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{L},\mu)
, an element of
\displaystyle L^{\infty}(X)
is given as an
equivalence class
of measurable, essentially bounded functions, in the point-free setup an element of
\displaystyle L^{\infty}

is
a bounded continuous function.

Theorem 6.6

(
[
Pav22
]
)
.

The functor
\displaystyle L^{\infty}
induces an equivalence

L^{\infty}:\mathrm{MblLoc}^{op}\simeq\mathrm{CVNA},

where
\displaystyle\mathrm{CVNA}
is the category of commutative von Neumann algebras and normal
\displaystyle*
-morphisms. The inverse functor sends a commutative von Neumann algebra to its locale of projections.

This means already developed tools in measure theory can be readily imported to the world of measurable locales.

Remark 6.7
.

It seems plausible that the inclusion

\mathrm{MblLoc}\rightarrow\mathrm{Loc}

has a right adjoint

(-)_{\mathrm{Bor}}:\mathrm{Loc}\rightarrow\mathrm{MblLoc}

which would be suitably referred to as the Borel locale of a locale. This would stand in contrast to the case of the inclusion
\displaystyle\mathrm{BoolLoc}\rightarrow\mathrm{Loc}
. (The non-existence of such a right adjoint is discussed in
[
Joh82
, 57]
.) However, it is not clear to the author at the time of writing how the functor
\displaystyle(-)_{\mathrm{Bor}}
would be described explicitly.

Remark 6.8
.

Any commutative von Neumann algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}
is of the form
\displaystyle L^{\infty}(\Gamma,\mu)
for some locally compact space
\displaystyle\Gamma
together with Radon measure
\displaystyle\mu
, see e.g.
[
Tak79
, V Theorem 1.18.]
. We will independently reprove this fact, under the above Gelfand-type duality, by showing that any measurable locale is obtained from a regular content on a locally compact Hausdorff space, see Theorem
9.10
.

Given a measurable locale
\displaystyle M
, and a locale
\displaystyle L
, and an
\displaystyle L
-valued random variable on
\displaystyle M
is just defined as a continuous function
\displaystyle M\rightarrow L
.
9
9

9

In practice this matches the standard definition of random variable up to almost everywhere equivalence.

Since we can use valuation sites to generate locally finite and faithful measures, an important question is when the frame
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu;\mathbf{2})
generated from a valuation site
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
is actually a Boolean frame, in which case the corresponding locale is a measurable locale. This leads us to the notion of an
almost Boolean
valuation site.

Definition 6.9
.

Let
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
be a valuation site.

•

We call
\displaystyle(D,\mu)

almost disconnected
, if for any
\displaystyle c_{0}\leq c\in D
, and
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
there exists
\displaystyle d\leq c
such that

\begin{array}[]{l}\mu(d\wedge c_{0})=0,\text{ and}\\
\mu(c)-\mu(d\vee c_{0})<\epsilon.\end{array}

•

We call
\displaystyle(D,\mu)

almost Boolean
, if for any
\displaystyle c\in D
and for any ascending sequence
\displaystyle c_{0}\leq c_{1}\leq\ldots\leq c
of elements contained in
\displaystyle c
, and for any
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
there exists
\displaystyle N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\displaystyle d\leq c
such that

\begin{array}[]{l}\mu(d\wedge c_{n})=0\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N},\text{ and}\\
\mu(c)-\mu(d\vee c_{N})<\epsilon.\end{array}

Intuitively, we think of the element
\displaystyle d
as an approximate complement relative to
\displaystyle c
to the element
\displaystyle c_{0}
, respectively to the subobject represented by the union of
\displaystyle c_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Example 6.10
.

The set
\displaystyle P=P(S)^{\mathrm{fin}}
of finite subsets of a given set
\displaystyle S
, together with the counting measure, is almost Boolean. To see this, let
\displaystyle A
be a finite subset of
\displaystyle S
and
\displaystyle B_{n}\subset A,n\in\mathbb{N}
an ascending sequence of subsets. Since
\displaystyle A
is finite, there exists a number
\displaystyle n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
where the maximum
\displaystyle B=B_{n_{0}}
is achieved. Now set
\displaystyle C=A\setminus B
as the almost complement.

Example 6.11
.

Let
\displaystyle(L,\delta_{x})
be a locale with a point
\displaystyle x
, equipped with the Dirac measure. Then
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(L)
is almost Boolean. To see this, let
\displaystyle U
be an open of
\displaystyle L
. If
\displaystyle x\not\in U
, there is nothing to show. Assume
\displaystyle x\in U
. If
\displaystyle F_{n}\subset U,n\in\mathbb{N}
is an ascending chain, then
\displaystyle x\in\bigvee_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F_{n}
iff
\displaystyle x\in F_{n_{0}}
for some
\displaystyle n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
. If this is the case, take
\displaystyle C=0
. Otherwise,
\displaystyle x\not\in F_{n}
for all
\displaystyle n\in\mathbb{N}
, in which case it suffices to take
\displaystyle C=U
as the approximate complement.

Example 6.12
.

The poset
\displaystyle([0,\infty),\leq)
equipped with the tautological valuation is not almost disconnected.

Example 6.13
.

The Sierpinski space
\displaystyle\mathbb{S}
given by the frame
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{S})=\{0\leq U\leq 1\}
together with the valuation
\displaystyle\mu_{p}
defined via
\displaystyle\mu_{p}(U)=p
and
\displaystyle\mu_{p}(1)=1
is an example of a valuation site that is
not
almost disconnected for
\displaystyle p<1
.

Example 6.14
.

Consider a valuation
\displaystyle\mu
on a Boolean algebra
\displaystyle B
. Then
\displaystyle(B,\mu)
is almost disconnected, since for any
\displaystyle c\leq d
, we can take
\displaystyle d\setminus c=d\wedge\neg c
as the relative complement. However, in general
\displaystyle(B,\mu)
is not almost Boolean, as can be seen from the following example.

Example 6.15
.

We continue with Example
4.28
, given by Cantor space
\displaystyle C
. The poset of compact opens of
\displaystyle C
,

\mathcal{O}=\mathrm{colim}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{2}^{n})

is a Boolean algebra. Therefore
\displaystyle(\mathcal{O},\mu)
, where
\displaystyle\mu
is the uniform valuation, is an example of an almost disconnected valuation site. However, it is not almost Boolean, as seen by the following counterexample, which is a combinatorial version of (the open complement of) the classical Smith–Volterra–Cantor set, also lovingly referred to as
Fat Cantor set
.

First of all, by cofinality we can write

\mathcal{O}\cong\mathrm{colim}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{2}^{n^{2}}).

We have the projection
\displaystyle p_{n}:\mathbf{2}^{n^{2}}\rightarrow\mathbf{2}^{(n-1)^{2}}
. Note that
\displaystyle p_{n}
is measure-preserving. We will give a sequence of subsets
\displaystyle A_{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{2}^{n^{2}})
constructed inductively.

•

The set
\displaystyle A_{1}
is chosen to be a one-element subset of
\displaystyle\mathbf{2}^{1^{2}}=\mathbf{2}
. We have
\displaystyle\mu(A_{1})=\frac{1}{2}
.

•

Define
\displaystyle A_{n}
as a union of
\displaystyle p_{n}^{-1}(A_{n-1})\subset\mathbf{2}^{n^{2}}
together with a choice of elements
\displaystyle x_{y}\in\mathbf{2}^{n^{2}}
for each of the sets
\displaystyle p_{n}^{-1}({y})
for
\displaystyle y\not\in A_{n-1}
. A calculation yields

\mu(A_{n})=\mu(A_{n-1})+\frac{1-\mu(A_{n-1})}{2^{2n-1}}.

The sequence
\displaystyle(A_{n},n\in\mathbb{N})
can be identified with an ascending sequence in
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}
. We verify by estimation that

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mu(A_{n})\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{2n-1}}=\frac{2}{3}<1.

However, there cannot exist an
\displaystyle\epsilon
-complement for this sequence for any
\displaystyle\epsilon
. Assume
\displaystyle B
is an element, such that
\displaystyle\mu(B\cap A_{n})=0
for all
\displaystyle n\in\mathbb{N}
. The set
\displaystyle B
must live in some finite stage
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{2}^{k^{2}})
for some
\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{N}
. By assumption, we have
\displaystyle B\cap A_{k}=\emptyset
. But, assuming that
\displaystyle B
is non-empty, we must have
\displaystyle p_{k+1}^{-1}(B)\cap A_{k+1}\neq\emptyset
, as the intersection contains at least one element by construction of
\displaystyle A_{k+1}
. Therefore
\displaystyle\mu(p_{k+1}^{-1}(B)\cap A_{k+1})>0
, a contradiction.

Remark 6.16
.

The existence of Fat Cantor sets hints at a deeper result about the potential structure of measurable locales. As a consequence of Maharam’s theorem
[
Fre00
, Ch. 33]
, there cannot exist a non-discrete measurable locale with a countable basis. This also implies indirectly that the locale
\displaystyle\mathrm{Ran}(\mathbf{2})
of random sequences cannot be Boolean, as we’ve seen explicitly from the computation above.

Proposition 6.17
.

Let
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
be an almost disconnected valuation site. Then for every
\displaystyle c\in D
, the propositional sheaf
\displaystyle[c]\in\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu;\mathbf{2})
is clopen. In particular, the
\displaystyle\mu
-inner locale
\displaystyle L(D,\mu)
is zero-dimensional.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle e\in D
. We need to show that
\displaystyle[e]
has a complement, which is the case iff

1=[e]\vee([e]\rightarrow N).

where
\displaystyle 1=D
is the maximal
\displaystyle\mu
-ideal. Recall that
\displaystyle[e]\rightarrow N=\{d~|~\mu(d\wedge e)=0\}
by Lemma
4.31
. In other words, we need to show that for any
\displaystyle c\in D
, we have

[c]=[c\wedge e]\vee([c]\wedge([e]\rightarrow N)).

Denote
\displaystyle c_{0}=c\wedge e
. Then the above holds iff for all
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
we find
\displaystyle d\leq c
such that
\displaystyle\mu(d\wedge e)=\mu(d\wedge c_{0})=0
and
\displaystyle\mu(c)-\mu(d\vee c_{0})<\epsilon
. But this is just the condition for
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
to be almost disconnected.
∎

Thus we see that almost disconnectedness of
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
leads to the locale
\displaystyle L(D,\mu)
being zero-dimensional. In order to guarantee that it is Boolean, we need the stronger condition of
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
being almost Boolean.

Theorem 6.18
.

Let
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
be a valuation site. Let
\displaystyle N\subset D
be the ideal of null sets. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\displaystyle(D,\mu)
is almost Boolean.

(2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu;\mathbf{2})
is a Boolean frame.

(3)

\displaystyle L(D,\mu)
is a measurable locale.

(4)

\displaystyle Sh(D,\mu;\mathbf{2})=b(N)\subset\mathrm{Sh}(D,fin;\mathbf{2})\cong\mathrm{Ind}(D)
.

Proof.

The equivalence of (2) and (3) is by definition. The equivalence of (2) and (4) is an immediate consequence from Proposition
5.15
.

Assume (1), i.e. that
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
is almost Boolean. Let
\displaystyle I\subset D
be a
\displaystyle\mu
-ideal. We need to show that
\displaystyle I
has a complement, which is the case iff

1=I\vee(I\rightarrow N).

Keeping in mind that
\displaystyle 1=D
is the maximal
\displaystyle\mu
-ideal, this is equivalent to the statement:

For all
\displaystyle c\in C,\epsilon>0
there exist elements
\displaystyle k,d\leq c
such that:

•

\displaystyle k\in I.

•

\displaystyle d
satisfies
\displaystyle\mu(d\wedge k^{\prime})=0\text{ for all }k^{\prime}\in I
.

•

\displaystyle\mu(c)-\mu(d\vee k)<\epsilon.

Let us prove this claim. Let
\displaystyle c\in C
and
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
. Since
\displaystyle\mu_{*}([c]\wedge I)\leq\mu(c)<\infty
, we can use Proposition
4.30
to get an exhaustion
\displaystyle c_{1}\leq c_{2}\leq\ldots
such that

[c]\wedge I=\bigvee_{n\in\mathbb{N}}[c_{n}].

Using that
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
is almost Boolean, we can find
\displaystyle m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\displaystyle d\in D
such that

\mu(d\wedge c_{n})=0\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N},\text{ and }\mu(c)-\mu(c_{m}\vee d)<\epsilon.

Setting
\displaystyle k=c_{m}
we are almost done with the argument. We are left to show that
\displaystyle\mu(d\wedge k^{\prime})=0
for all
\displaystyle k^{\prime}\in I
, or in other words
\displaystyle\mu_{*}([d]\wedge I)=0
. This follows as
\displaystyle d\leq c
by

[d]\wedge I=[d]\wedge[c]\wedge I=\bigvee_{n\in\mathbb{N}}[d]\wedge[c_{n}]=\bigvee_{n\in\mathbb{N}}N=N.

To show (2) implies (1), let
\displaystyle c\in C
,
\displaystyle c_{0}\leq c_{1}\leq\ldots\leq c
and
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
. Define
\displaystyle I=\bigvee_{n\in\mathbb{N}}[c_{n}]
. By construction
\displaystyle I\leq[c]
. Since
\displaystyle Sh(D,\mu;\mathbf{2})
is assumed to be Boolean, we have

[c]=I\vee((I\rightarrow N)\wedge[c])=I\vee\{d\in D~|~\forall n\in\mathbb{N}~\mu(d\wedge c_{n})=0\text{ and }\mu(d)=\mu(d\wedge c)\}

where we used the description of
\displaystyle I\rightarrow N
provided by Lemma
4.31
.

This can only be the case if
\displaystyle c
itself has a
\displaystyle\mu
-approximation by elements from the right-hand side, which is equivalent to
\displaystyle(D,\mu)
being almost Boolean.
∎

Example 6.19
.

A major source of examples of almost Boolean valuation sites is given by the notion of a
regular content

\displaystyle\lambda
on the set
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
of compact subsets of a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
, as discussed later with Theorem
8.17
. A particular classical example to keep in mind is the
\displaystyle d
-dimensional Lebesgue measure
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}
on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}
when restricted to the set
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{d}=\mathcal{K}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of compact subsets of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which we elaborate on in Section
10.1
. This produces the measurable locale of
Lebesgue reals

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}_{Leb}=L(\mathcal{K}^{d},\mu)
. The corresponding complete Boolean algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{Leb})
is also called
random algebra
, and more classically obtained by modding out the Boolean algebra of Lebesgue measurable sets by the ideal of
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}
-null sets. (We will prove this isomorphism later in Theorem
12.11
.) The random algebra was originally studied by von Neumann, see
[
NH98
, p.253, Example 2]
.

Example 6.20
.

Keeping with the theme of measures on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}
, one may instead of
\displaystyle(\mathcal{K}^{d},\mu)
look at the sub-valuation site
\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}^{d},\mu)
spanned by finite unions of
boxes
, where a
box
is a subset of the form
\displaystyle[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
\displaystyle a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

[a,b]=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}~|~a_{i}\leq x_{i}\leq b_{i}\text{ for all }i=1,\ldots,d\}.

The locale
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}_{Jor}=L(\mathcal{J}^{d},\mu)
might be suitably called the
Jordan reals
as it captures the essence of the so called
Jordan measure
, see
[
Tao11
, Section 1.1.2.]
, and comes with a continuous, measure-preserving map
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}_{Jor}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Every inclusion
\displaystyle D\subset D^{\prime}
of finite unions of boxes admits an
\displaystyle\epsilon
-complement, however
\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}^{d},\mu)
is not almost Boolean. As an example, consider the construction of a Smith-Volterra-Cantor set, also referred to as Fat Cantor set, as for example described in
[
AB98
, 141]
, for some
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
. This is obtained as the complement of a countable union of ascending open sets
\displaystyle U_{n}\subset[0,1]
, each being given by a finite union of open intervals, such that for
\displaystyle U=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}U_{n}
we have
\displaystyle\mu(U)=1-\epsilon<1
, yet the intersection of any interval
\displaystyle[a,b]
with
\displaystyle U
will always have positive measure. Simply doing the same construction with closed intervals provides an ascending sequence of sets
\displaystyle A_{n}
, each consisting of a finite union of closed intervals, that cannot have approximate complements.

7.
The locally coherent space associated to a Hausdorff space

An important example of a measure is that of a Radon measure on a Hausdorff space. Before we begin with the measure-theoretic aspects, we will take care of some categorical constructions that are possible for Hausdorff spaces in this section. Since Hausdorff spaces are automatically sober
[
PP12
, Ch. I 1.2]
, we obtain a fully faithful embedding

L:\mathrm{HausSpc}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Loc}

Henceforth we will identify a Hausdorff topological space with its corresponding locale. Hausdorffness of a topological space
\displaystyle X
guarantees that the poset
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
of compact subsets of
\displaystyle X
interacts favourably with the locale corresponding to
\displaystyle X
. Let us state a lemma that will be useful later on.

Lemma 7.1

(Separation lemma,
[
Coh94
]
7.1.2)
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff space. Assume
\displaystyle K_{1},K_{2}
are two compact subsets of
\displaystyle X
such that
\displaystyle K_{1}\cap K_{2}=\emptyset
. There exist
\displaystyle U_{1},U_{2}
open sets of
\displaystyle X
such that
\displaystyle U_{1}\cap U_{2}=\emptyset
and
\displaystyle K_{1}\subset U_{1}
as well as
\displaystyle K_{2}\subset U_{2}
.

Since Hausdorffness implies that intersections of compact subsets are still compact we see that
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
is a lower bounded distributive lattice. We can equip it with the
finite covering
topology, where

\{K_{i}\subset K~|~i\in I\}

is a covering if
\displaystyle I
is finite and
\displaystyle\bigcup_{i\in I}K_{i}=K
.

Definition 7.2
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff space. Define
\displaystyle X^{\mathcal{K}}
to be the locale corresponding to the
\displaystyle 0
-site
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
equipped with the finite covering topology
\displaystyle fin
.

Remark 7.3
.

Recall that this means concretely that

\mathcal{O}(X^{\mathcal{K}})\cong\mathrm{Idl}(\mathcal{K}(X))\cong\mathrm{Ind}(\mathcal{K}(X)).

The locale
\displaystyle X^{\mathcal{K}}
is an example of a locally coherent locale. This means it is spatial and corresponds to a locally compact, but not necessarily Hausdorff, space. For more detail, see
[
Leh25
]
.

For any topological space
\displaystyle X
, there is a functorial assignment

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\theta_{X}^{*}:\mathcal{O}(X)&\rightarrow&\mathcal{O}(X^{\mathcal{K}})\\
U&\mapsto&\{K\subset U~|~K\text{ compact}\}\end{array}

It is clear that
\displaystyle\theta^{*}
maps every open
\displaystyle U
to a
\displaystyle fin
-proposition, and that
\displaystyle\theta^{*}
preserves finite meets.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff space. Then
\displaystyle\theta_{X}^{*}
is a frame homomorphism. If
\displaystyle X
is locally compact then the resulting map of locales

\theta:X^{\mathcal{K}}\rightarrow X

is an epimorphism of locales.

We remark that a morphism
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
of locales is an epimorphism iff
\displaystyle f^{*}:\mathcal{O}(Y)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(X)
is injective, see e.g.
[
PP12
, IV]
. For the proof of this proposition we will need an elementary lemma about compact subsets in Hausdorff spaces.

Lemma 7.5

(
[
Coh94
]
, 7.1.10)
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff space. Assume
\displaystyle K
is a compact subset and
\displaystyle K\subset U_{1}\cup\ldots\cup U_{n}
with
\displaystyle U_{i}
being open sets for
\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,n
. Then
\displaystyle K=K_{1}\cup\cdots\cup K_{n}
with
\displaystyle K_{i}
compact and
\displaystyle K_{i}\subset U_{i}
for all
\displaystyle i=1,\ldots,n
.

Proof of Proposition
7.4
.

We need to show that
\displaystyle\theta^{*}
preserves suprema. Let
\displaystyle U=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
be an arbitrary union of open subsets of
\displaystyle X
. Then

\theta^{*}(U)=\{K\subset U~|~K\text{ compact}\}=\{K~|~K\text{ compact and }K\subset\bigcup_{i\in F}U_{i},~F\subset I\text{ finite}\},

whereas

\bigvee_{i\in I}\theta^{*}(U_{i})=\{\bigcup_{i\in F}K_{i}~|~F\subset I\text{ finite},K_{i}\subset U_{i}\text{ for all }i\in F\}.

These two sets agree for
\displaystyle X
Hausdorff because of Lemma
7.5
.

To verify that
\displaystyle\theta^{*}
induces an epimorphism of frames in the case that
\displaystyle X
is locally compact, we need to argue that it is injective. Assume

\theta^{*}(U)=\{K\subset U~|~K\text{ compact}\}=\{K\subset V~|~K\text{ compact}\}=\theta^{*}(V).

But then
\displaystyle U
and
\displaystyle V
contain the same points, as for any
\displaystyle x\in U
by local compactness we can simply choose a compact neighborhood of
\displaystyle x
contained in
\displaystyle U
and vice versa. Therefore
\displaystyle U=V
.
∎

Remark 7.6
.

The injectivity of
\displaystyle\theta^{*}
in the case of a locally compact Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
is a localic analogue of the known fact that the
\displaystyle\infty
-category of
\displaystyle K
-sheaves can be identified with the
\displaystyle\infty
-category sheaves on
\displaystyle X
, as discussed e.g. in
[
Lur12
, Section 7.3.4]
or
[
Efi25
, Proposition 6.5.]
. In the locale theoretic language, if
\displaystyle X
is locally compact Hausdorff, then a propositional sheaf
\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{O}(X^{\mathcal{K}})
is of the form
\displaystyle\mathcal{J}=\theta^{*}(U)
iff it satisfies the condition

K\in\mathcal{J}\text{ iff }\exists K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}:K\ll K^{\prime},

where
\displaystyle K\ll K^{\prime}
means that there exists an open
\displaystyle V\subset X
such that
\displaystyle K\subset V\subset K^{\prime}
. The corresponding open set
\displaystyle U
is then given as
\displaystyle U=\bigcup_{V\subset K,K\in\mathcal{J}}V
.

Remark 7.7
.

The map
\displaystyle\theta:X^{\mathcal{K}}\rightarrow X
is compatible with open inclusions, in the sense that whenever
\displaystyle U\subset X
is an open subset, we have the pullback square

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U^{\mathcal{K}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\mathcal{K}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

To see this note that pullback along
\displaystyle\theta
of
\displaystyle U
gives the open sublocale given by
\displaystyle\theta^{*}(U)
of
\displaystyle X^{\mathcal{K}}
by Lemma
2.17
. Observe that

\mathcal{O}(X^{\mathcal{K}})_{/\theta^{*}(U)}=\mathrm{Idl}(\mathcal{K}(X))_{/\theta^{*}(U)}\cong\mathrm{Idl}(\mathcal{K}(U))=\mathcal{O}(U^{\mathcal{K}})

since an ideal
\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\subset\theta^{*}(U)=\{K\subset U~|~K\text{ compact}\}
is by definition just an ideal of compact sets contained in
\displaystyle U
.

7.1.
Functoriality with respect to partial proper maps

We now analyse the functoriality of the assignment
\displaystyle X\mapsto X^{\mathcal{K}}
. There are two distinct cases we would like to separate.

•

The case where
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
is partially defined with open support, and
proper
, that is
\displaystyle f^{-1}
preserves compact sets. This case will allow us to construct measure-preserving partial maps.

•

The case where
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
is continuous, with global support. This case induces an
open
map
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}:X^{\mathcal{K}}\rightarrow Y^{\mathcal{K}}
, which will be useful in constructing maps between Boolean locales without reference to a choice of measure.

We will analyse the first case in this section.

Definition 7.8
.

A partial continuous map
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
between Hausdorff spaces
\displaystyle X,Y
with open support is called
proper
if
\displaystyle f^{-1}(K)\subset X
is compact for every compact subset
\displaystyle K\subset Y
.

Theorem 7.9
.

There exists a functor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(-)^{\mathcal{K}}:\mathrm{HausSpc}_{\mathrm{part}~\mathrm{prop}}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{part}}\\
X&\mapsto&X^{\mathcal{K}}\end{array}

whereby a partial proper map
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
is sent to the partial map
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}:X^{\mathcal{K}}\rightarrow Y^{\mathcal{K}}
obtained by extending the partial morphism of sites
\displaystyle f^{-1}:\mathcal{K}(Y)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(X)
. Moreover, we have a commuting square

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\mathcal{K}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\mathcal{K}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y,}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\mathcal{K}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{X}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{Y}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in other words,
\displaystyle\theta
is a natural transformation
\displaystyle(-)^{\mathcal{K}}\rightarrow L
, where
\displaystyle L:\mathrm{HausSpc}\rightarrow\mathrm{Loc}
is the fully faithful inclusion of Hausdorff spaces into locales.

Proof.

The functoriality of
\displaystyle(-)^{\mathcal{K}}
is immediate, as
\displaystyle f^{-1}:\mathcal{K}(Y)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(X)
preserves finite unions and intersections and is thus a partial morphism of sites. We obtain the extension from Theorem
3.23
.

We still need to show that
\displaystyle\theta
is a natural transformation. Consider the square

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}(X^{\mathcal{K}})}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}(Y^{\mathcal{K}})}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}(X)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}(Y).}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{*}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{*}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{-1}}

We want to show that this square commutes. Let
\displaystyle V\subset Y
be open. Tracing through both composites we get

\begin{array}[]{r}\theta(f^{-1}(V))=\{K\subset f^{-1}(V)\text{ compact}\}=\{K\subset\mathrm{dom}(f)\subset X\text{ compact}~|~f(K)\subset V\}\\
=\{K\subset X\text{ compact}~|~f(K)\subset C\subset V,C\text{ compact}\}\\
=\{K\subset X\text{ compact}~|~K\subset f^{-1}(C),C\subset V\text{ compact}\}\\
=(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}(\theta(V))\end{array}

where we note that
\displaystyle f(K)
is well-defined, as
\displaystyle K\subset f^{-1}(V)\subset\mathrm{dom}(f)
, hence we conclude the proof.
∎

Remark 7.10
.

The class of partially defined proper maps with open support may seem unusal to some readers. The choice is fairly natural however at least in the case of locally compact Hausdorff spaces, as there exist equivalences

\mathrm{LocCHaus}_{\mathrm{part}~\mathrm{prop}}\simeq\mathrm{CHaus}_{*}\simeq\mathrm{Comm}C^{*}\mathrm{-Alg}^{op}

with
\displaystyle\mathrm{CHaus}_{*}
being the category of pointed compact Hausdorff spaces and base-point preserving continuous maps, and
\displaystyle\mathrm{Comm}C^{*}\mathrm{-Alg}
being the category of
non-unital
commutative
\displaystyle C^{*}
-algebras, with the equivalences given by one-point compactification and Gelfand duality, respectively. (See e.g.
[
Joh82
,
\nopp
IV 4.1]
.) Hence partially defined proper maps simply correspond to
\displaystyle*
-homomorphisms.

7.2.
Functoriality with respect to globally defined continuous map

Let
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
be a globally defined, continuous map between Hausdorff spaces. Since
\displaystyle f
maps compact sets to compact sets, an assignment which preserves finite unions, we have an associated left adjoint functor
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}_{!}:\mathcal{O}(X^{\mathcal{K}})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(Y^{\mathcal{K}})
, given by left Kan extending the assignment
\displaystyle K\mapsto f(K)
for
\displaystyle K\subset X
compact. We claim this is the direct image part of an open map
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}:X^{\mathcal{K}}\rightarrow Y^{\mathcal{K}}
.

Theorem 7.11
.

There exists a functor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(-)^{\mathcal{K}}:\mathrm{HausSpc}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{open}}\\
X&\mapsto&X^{\mathcal{K}}\end{array}

whereby a map
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
is sent to the open map
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}:X^{\mathcal{K}}\rightarrow Y^{\mathcal{K}}
with:

•

The direct image part
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}_{!}:\mathcal{O}(X^{\mathcal{K}})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(Y^{\mathcal{K}})
is obtained by extending the covering preserving functor
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(Y^{\mathcal{K}}),K\mapsto[f(K)]
.

•

The inverse image part
\displaystyle(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}:\mathcal{O}(Y^{\mathcal{K}})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(X^{\mathcal{K}})
is obtained by extending the flat functor
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(Y)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(X^{\mathcal{K}}),C\mapsto\{K\subset f^{-1}(C)\}
.

Moreover, we have a commuting square

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\mathcal{K}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\mathcal{K}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y,}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\mathcal{K}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{X}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{Y}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in other words,
\displaystyle\theta
is a natural transformation
\displaystyle U(-)^{\mathcal{K}}\rightarrow L
, where
\displaystyle U:\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{open}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Loc}
is the forget functor, and
\displaystyle L:\mathrm{HausSpc}\rightarrow\mathrm{Loc}
is the fully faithful inclusion of Hausdorff spaces into locales.

Proof.

First note that both
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}_{!}
and
\displaystyle(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}
are left adjoints. This is clear for
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}!
, as
\displaystyle K\mapsto f(K)
preserves finite unions. To observe the same for
\displaystyle(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}
, note that for
\displaystyle C_{1},C_{2}\subset Y
compact we have

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}f^{*}(C_{1}\cup C_{2})&=&\{K\subset f^{-1}(C_{1}\cup C_{2})\}&=&\{K\subset f^{-1}(C_{1})\cup f^{-1}(C_{2})\}\\
&=&\{K_{1}\cup K_{2}~|~K_{1}\subset f^{-1}(C_{1}),K_{2}\subset f^{-1}(C_{2})\}&=&f^{*}(C_{1})\vee f^{*}(C_{2}).\end{array}

For the middle equality, note that any
\displaystyle K\subset f^{-1}(C_{1})\cup f^{-1}(C_{2})
can be written as

K=(K\cap f^{-1}(C_{1}))\cup(K\cap f^{-1}(C_{2})).

Note that Hausdorffness of
\displaystyle X
is crucial here, as
\displaystyle f^{-1}(C_{1})
is in general not compact, but always closed and
\displaystyle K\cap f^{-1}(C_{1})
a closed subset of a compact set, therefore compact.

We next establish that
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}_{!}
is in fact left adjoint to
\displaystyle(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}
. We note that this already implies that
\displaystyle(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}
is a frame homomorphism, as it is then both a left and a right adjoint, therefore preserves both arbitrary suprema as well as infima. Let
\displaystyle U\in\mathcal{O}(X^{\mathcal{K}})
and
\displaystyle V\in\mathcal{O}(Y^{\mathcal{K}})
. We need to show

f^{\mathcal{K}}_{!}(U)\leq V~\text{ iff }~U\leq(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}(V).

(1)

We have
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}_{!}(U)=\bigvee_{K\in U}[f(K)]
, therefore

f^{\mathcal{K}}_{!}(U)\leq V~\text{ iff }~\forall K\in U:f(K)\in V

(2)

We have

(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}(V)=\{K\subset X\text{ compact}~|~K\subset f^{-1}(C)\text{ for some }C\in V\},

therefore

U\leq(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}(V)~\text{ iff }~\forall K\in U\exists C\in V:K\subset f^{-1}(C).

Assume (1) holds, i.e.
\displaystyle f(K)\in V
. Then setting
\displaystyle C=f(K)
we see that
\displaystyle K\subset f^{-1}(C)
for
\displaystyle C\in V
, hence condition (2) holds. For the converse, assume (2), i.e.
\displaystyle f(K)\subset f^{-1}(C)
. This is the case iff
\displaystyle f(K)\subset C
. By downward closure of
\displaystyle V
, since
\displaystyle C\in V
, this also implies
\displaystyle f(X)\in V
.

Let us know show that
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}_{!}\dashv(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}
satisfies the Frobenius identity

f^{\mathcal{K}}_{!}(U\wedge(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}(U))=f^{\mathcal{K}}_{!}(U)\wedge V

for
\displaystyle U\in\mathcal{O}(X^{\mathcal{K}})
and
\displaystyle V\in\mathcal{O}(Y^{\mathcal{K}})
, which then concludes the statement that
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}:X^{\mathcal{K}}\rightarrow Y^{\mathcal{K}}
is an open map. Since both
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}_{!}
and
\displaystyle(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}
are functors preserving suprema, it suffices to show the claim for
\displaystyle U=[K],V=[C]
, where
\displaystyle K\subset X
and
\displaystyle C\subset Y
are compact. We compute:

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}f^{\mathcal{K}}_{!}([K]\wedge\bigvee_{K^{\prime}\subset f^{-1}(C)}[K^{\prime}])&=&f^{\mathcal{K}}_{!}(\bigvee_{K^{\prime}\subset f^{-1}(C)}[K\cap K^{\prime}]&=&f^{\mathcal{K}}_{!}(\bigvee_{K^{\prime\prime}\subset K\cap f^{-1}(C)}[K^{\prime\prime}]\\
=f^{\mathcal{K}}_{!}([K\cap f^{-1}(C)])&=&[f(K\cap f^{-1}(C))]&=&[f(K)\cap C],\end{array}

where we used that
\displaystyle K\cap f^{-1}(C)
is itself compact, hence a maximal element for the set of all
\displaystyle K^{\prime\prime}\subset K\cap f^{-1}(C)
compact, and in the last line the Frobenius identity for image and preimage of functions between sets.

The argument that
\displaystyle\theta
is a natural transformation is analogous to the argument given in the proof of Theorem
7.9
.
∎

Remark 7.12
.

Since the locales
\displaystyle X^{\mathcal{K}}
are locally coherent—and hence spatial—the theorem above could, in principle, be phrased entirely in classical point-set topology.

Theorem
7.11
will prove useful in providing a clean construction of a Boolean locale out of a Hausdorff space. Recall the notion of a marked locale defined in
2.26
. We will use a similar notion for Hausdorff spaces.

Definition 7.13
.

A pair
\displaystyle(X,N)
where
\displaystyle X
is a Hausdorff space and
\displaystyle N\subset\mathcal{K}(X)
is an ideal of compact subsets is called a
compactly marked Hausdorff space
. If
\displaystyle(X,N),(Y,M)
are both compactly marked, we say that a continuous map
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
is
compatible
if:

•

For all
\displaystyle K\in N
we have
\displaystyle f(K)\in M
.

•

For all
\displaystyle C\in M
we have
\displaystyle\{K\subset f^{-1}(C)\text{ compact}\}\subset N
.

We denote the resulting category of compactly marked Hausdorff spaces and compatible maps by
\displaystyle\mathrm{CMHS}
.

We remark right away that in the upcoming section the central example of a compactly marked Hausdorff space will be that of a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
equipped with a measure
\displaystyle\mu
, and
\displaystyle N
being the set of compact
\displaystyle\mu
-null sets.

Corollary 7.14
.

There is a functor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}b:\mathrm{CMHS}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{BoolLoc}\\
(X,N)&\mapsto&b(X,N)\end{array}

where
\displaystyle b(X,N)=(N^{c})_{\neg\neg}\hookrightarrow X^{\mathcal{K}}
.

Proof.

The functor
\displaystyle(-)^{\mathcal{K}}:\mathrm{HausSpc}\rightarrow\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathrm{open}}
refines to a functor
\displaystyle(-)^{\mathcal{K}}:\mathrm{CMHS}\rightarrow\mathrm{MarkLoc}_{\mathrm{open}}
by construction. Our wanted functor is now obtained by composing with the functor
\displaystyle b:\mathrm{MarkLoc}_{\mathrm{open}}\rightarrow\mathrm{BoolLoc}
from Theorem
5.21
.
∎

8.
Radon measures on Hausdorff spaces - The locale theoretic approach

We now come to the core example of a Boolean measure locale: The
Radon locale
constructed from a
Radon valuation
on a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
. This notion is a slight generalization of that of a
Radon measure
. This notion will give us access to almost all measure spaces useful for mathematical practice within the context of localic measure theory.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff space. A
Radon valuation
on
\displaystyle X
is a finite and almost Boolean valuation
\displaystyle\mu:\mathcal{K}(X)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
on the set of compact subsets of
\displaystyle X
. Denote by
\displaystyle X^{\mu}
the associated inner locale together with its locally finite and faithful measure

\mu_{*}:\mathcal{O}(X^{\mu})=\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{K}(X),\mu;\mathbf{2})\rightarrow[0,\infty].

provided by Theorem
4.22
.

Example 8.2
.

The main examples of Radon valuations will come from
regular contents
, which we discuss in the following in Section
8.3
. In the situation of a regular content
\displaystyle\mu
, there is an associated Radon measure
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
. The frame of the locale
\displaystyle X^{\mu}
is isomorphic to the complete Boolean algebra given by measurable sets modulo null sets
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{N}
of
\displaystyle X
, a fact that will be proven in Theorem
12.11
.

Let us discuss some consequences of the existence of a Radon valuation. Since the
\displaystyle\mu
-inner topology on
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
includes all coverings of the topology
\displaystyle fin
, we have a continuous embedding
\displaystyle X^{\mu}\hookrightarrow X^{\mathcal{K}}
. We also have the continuous map
\displaystyle\theta:X^{\mathcal{K}}\rightarrow X
by Proposition
7.4
, resulting in the composition

p_{\mu}:X^{\mu}\hookrightarrow X^{\mathcal{K}}\rightarrow X.

Since
\displaystyle X^{\mu}
is Boolean this further lifts uniquely by Theorem
5.5
,

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{Sl}(X)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\mu}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\mathcal{K}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{can}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}

Pushing forward the inner measure
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
on
\displaystyle X^{\mu}
equips all locales in this picture with measures and makes all maps involved measure-preserving. In particular, we obtain a measure
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
on the frame of sublocales
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(X)^{op}
of
\displaystyle X
.
10
10

10

Technically speaking we obtain a
co-measure
on the coframe
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(X)
, as we obtain a contravariant functor
\displaystyle\mu_{*}:\mathrm{Sl}(X)^{op}\rightarrow[0,\infty]
that satisfies modularity,
\displaystyle\mu_{*}(1)=0
and
\displaystyle\mu_{*}(\bigwedge_{i\in I}S_{i})=\sup_{i\in I}\mu_{*}(S_{i})
. This does not cause many issues in practice however, as for
complemented sublocales
, which contains finite unions and intersections of closed and open sublocales, we can simply pass to the co-measure of the complement.
The value at an open set
\displaystyle U
of
\displaystyle X
is computed as

\mu_{*}(U)=\sup_{K\subset U\text{ compact}}\mu(K),

and the value at a closed set
\displaystyle C
of
\displaystyle X
is given as

\mu_{*}(C)=\sup\{\mu(K)~|~K\text{ s.t. }\mu(K\cap K^{\prime})=0\text{ for all }K^{\prime}\subset C^{c}\text{ compact}\}.

Remark 8.3
.

Any subset
\displaystyle S
of
\displaystyle X
will be assigned a consistent value that we can reasonably call the
measure
of
\displaystyle S
. To see this, note that we have a lift

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{Sl}(X)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{disc}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

since
\displaystyle X^{disc}
is Boolean, by the universal property of the map
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(X)\rightarrow X
given in Theorem
5.5
. Therefore we can use the functor
\displaystyle j_{*}:\mathcal{P}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{Sl}(X)^{op}
to define
\displaystyle\mu(S)=\mu_{*}(j_{*}(S))
for a subset
\displaystyle S\subset X
. This will however not give a measure on
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(X)
in the traditional sense, as
\displaystyle j_{*}
only preserves infima, and not necessarily joins. We will see later, that if
\displaystyle\mu
is a regular content, this induced measure agrees with the classical notion of measure, see Corollary
8.22
.

Moreover, if
\displaystyle A\hookrightarrow X
is a sublocale of
\displaystyle X
, define the map
\displaystyle p|_{A}:A^{\mu}\rightarrow A
via pullback along
\displaystyle p_{\mu}
, i.e. as

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\mu}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\mu}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p|_{A}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mu}}

Since the pullback of an inclusion of a sublocale along any map is again a sublocale, and
\displaystyle X^{\mu}
is Boolean and thus any sublocale of
\displaystyle X^{\mu}
is open, this means that
\displaystyle A^{\mu}\hookrightarrow X^{\mu}
is an open inclusion, and
\displaystyle A^{\mu}
is, as a sublocale of a Boolean locale, again Boolean. The measure
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
on
\displaystyle X^{\mu}
thus restricts to a measure
\displaystyle{\mu|_{A}}_{*}
on
\displaystyle A^{\mu}
, which is again locally finite and faithful. Pushing forward along
\displaystyle p|_{A}
equips the sublocale
\displaystyle A
with a measure
\displaystyle\mu|_{A}
, which we call the restriction of
\displaystyle\mu
.

We call a continuous map
\displaystyle f:X^{\mu}\rightarrow Y
, for
\displaystyle Y
a locale, a
\displaystyle Y
-
valued random variable
on
\displaystyle X^{\mu}
. The map
\displaystyle p_{\mu}:X^{\mu}\rightarrow X
is a particular such example, and induces the function

\mathrm{Map}(X,Y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}(X^{\mu},Y)

via precomposition, which realizes continuous functions on
\displaystyle X
as random variables.

More generally, suppose we have a partition of
\displaystyle X^{\mu}
into pieces of the form
\displaystyle X^{\mu}=\coprod_{i\in I}A_{i}^{\mu}
. Then any choice of individual random variables
\displaystyle f_{i}:A_{i}^{\mu}\rightarrow Y
for each
\displaystyle i\in I
determines a unique random variable
\displaystyle f:X^{\mu}\rightarrow Y
. Thus for example piece-wise defined continuous functions always give associated random variables, assuming the domains of definition cover all of
\displaystyle X
, up to null sets. On top of that, from these basic examples of random variables, assuming the usual completeness assumptions on the target
\displaystyle Y
, more examples of random variables can be constructed via limit procedures. This covers practically all useful measurable functions considered in mathematical analysis, constructed in a point-free manner. Unlike in the classical approach to measure theory, no passage to equivalence classes up to almost everywhere equivalence is necessary. Moreover, any construction made to random variables in our sense will always be measurable in the classical sense. From here, the theory of integration could be developed. However, this remains outside of the scope of this article.

Remark 8.4
.

The theory works smoothest in the case
\displaystyle X
is additionally locally compact, and
\displaystyle\lambda
is a regular content. We will develop some of the consequences of both of this cases in the upcoming sections. However, measures on spaces, such as spaces of functions, that are not necessarily locally compact are important in practice.

8.1.
Functoriality for Radon locales

Let us discuss the functoriality of the assignment
\displaystyle X\mapsto X^{\mu}
. Here we want to consider two different cases of maps between Hausdorff spaces equipped with Radon valuations: That of (globally defined) continuous measure-preserving maps, as well as the case of measure-preserving partially defined proper maps with open support.

Definition 8.5
.

A continuous map
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
between Hausdorff spaces
\displaystyle X,Y
equipped with valuations
\displaystyle\mu,\nu
on the set of compact sets
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
, respectively
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(Y)
, is called
compactly measure-preserving
if the induced map
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}:X^{\mathcal{K}}\rightarrow Y^{\mathcal{K}}
is measure-preserving when equipping
\displaystyle X^{\mathcal{K}}
and
\displaystyle Y^{\mathcal{K}}
with the induced inner measures
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
and
\displaystyle\nu_{*}
. Equivalently,
\displaystyle f
is compactly measure-preserving iff for all compact sets
\displaystyle C\subset Y
it holds that

\mu_{*}((f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}(C))=\sup_{K\subset f^{-1}(C)\text{ compact}}\mu(K)=\nu(C).

Denote by
\displaystyle\mathrm{ValHausSpc}_{\mathrm{glob}}
the resulting category of pairs
\displaystyle(X,\mu)
with
\displaystyle X
a Hausdorff spaces and
\displaystyle\mu
a valuation on
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
, and compact measure-preserving maps between them, and
\displaystyle\mathrm{RadHausSpc}_{\mathrm{glob}}
the full subcategory spanned by pairs
\displaystyle(X,\mu)
such that
\displaystyle\mu
is a Radon valuation.

In the following, recall that
\displaystyle\mathrm{MeasLoc}
is the category of locales equipped with measures, and measure-preserving partial maps. We denote by
\displaystyle\mathrm{MeasLoc}_{\mathrm{glob}}
the wide subcategory given by restricting to globally defined measure-preserving maps.

Note that any compactly measure-preserving map
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
is in particular a measure-preserving map
\displaystyle(X,\mu_{*})\rightarrow(Y,\nu_{*})
. To see this, consider the commuting square

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\mathcal{K}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\mathcal{K}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y,}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\mathcal{K}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{X}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{Y}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

given by Theorem
7.11
. Then
\displaystyle f
being compactly measure-preserving is equivalent to
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}
being measure-preserving. Since
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
and
\displaystyle\nu_{*}
are defined via pushforward along
\displaystyle\theta
, this means all maps in this square become measure-preserving. In particular, this gives a forget functor
\displaystyle L:\mathrm{ValHausSpc}_{\mathrm{glob}}\rightarrow\mathrm{MeasLoc}_{\mathrm{glob}}
, which sends
\displaystyle(X,\mu)
to
\displaystyle(X,\mu_{*})
.

Theorem 8.6
.

There exists a functor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathrm{Rad}:\mathrm{ValHausSpc}_{\mathrm{glob}}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{MeasLoc}_{\mathrm{l.f.~faithf,glob}}\\
X&\mapsto&X^{\mu}\end{array}

where
\displaystyle\mathrm{MeasLoc}_{\mathrm{l.f.~faithf,glob}}
is the category of locally finite and faithful measure locales, and measure-preserving globally defined maps between them, together with a natural transformation
\displaystyle p:U\mathrm{Rad}\rightarrow L
, where
\displaystyle U:\mathrm{MeasLoc}_{\mathrm{l.f.~faithf,glob}}\rightarrow\mathrm{MeasLoc}_{glob}
is the canonical inclusion, given in components by the map

p_{\mu}:X^{\mu}\rightarrow X.

The functor
\displaystyle\mathrm{Rad}
restricts to a functor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathrm{Rad}:\mathrm{RadHausSpc}_{\mathrm{glob}}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{BoolMeasLoc}_{\mathrm{l.f.~faithf,glob}}\\
\end{array}

where
\displaystyle\mathrm{BoolMeasLoc}_{\mathrm{l.f.~faithf,glob}}
is the category of Boolean, locally finite and faithful measure locales, with measure-preserving globally defined maps between them.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
be a continuous, compactly measure-preserving map with
\displaystyle X,Y
both both Hausdorff spaces, and
\displaystyle\mu
,
\displaystyle\nu
valuations on their respective sets of compact subsets of
\displaystyle X
and
\displaystyle Y
. We have the natural commutative square given by Theorem
7.11
,

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\mathcal{K}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\mathcal{K}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\mathcal{K}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{X}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{Y}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

To show that
\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{K}}
further refines to a map
\displaystyle\mathrm{Rad}(f):X^{\mu}\rightarrow Y^{\nu}
it suffices to show that the induced functor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*})^{sh}:\mathcal{K}(Y)&\rightarrow&\mathcal{O}(X^{\mu})=\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{K}(X),\mu;\mathbf{2})\\
C&\mapsto&\{K\subset f^{-1}(C)\text{ compact }\}^{sh}\end{array}

is flat with respect to the
\displaystyle\nu
-inner pretopology on
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(Y)
. The case of finite covers is clear. Let
\displaystyle\{C_{i}\subset C~|~i\in I\}
be a directed
\displaystyle\nu
-approximation. Using that
\displaystyle f
is measure-preserving, we have

\sup_{i\in I}\mu_{*}((f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}(C_{i})^{sh})=\sup_{i\in I}\mu_{*}((f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}(C_{i}))=\sup_{i\in I}\nu(C_{i})=\nu(C)=\mu_{*}((f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}(C))=\mu_{*}((f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}(C)^{sh})<\infty.

Since
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
is faithful on
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{K}(X),\mu;\mathbf{2})
, this means that

\bigvee_{i\in I}(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}(C_{i})^{sh}=(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*}(C)^{sh}

in
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{K}(X),\mu;\mathbf{2})
, in other words,
\displaystyle(f^{\mathcal{K}})^{*})^{sh}
is flat. It is also clear that the induced map
\displaystyle\mathrm{Rad}(f)
is measure-preserving. We thus obtain the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\mu}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\nu}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\mathcal{K}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\mathcal{K}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Rad}(f)}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\mathcal{K}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Since all measures in question are defined via pushforward, all maps in this diagram are measure-preserving. This settles the question of naturality. For the last point, the restriction to
\displaystyle\mathrm{RadHausSpc}_{\mathrm{glob}}
maps into Boolean locales by Theorem
6.18
.
∎

We now come to the analogous situation when considering partially defined proper maps instead.

Definition 8.7
.

A partial proper map
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
between Hausdorff spaces
\displaystyle X,Y
equipped with valuations
\displaystyle\mu,\nu
on the set of compact sets
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
, respectively
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(Y)
, is called
compactly measure-preserving
if
\displaystyle\mu(f^{-1}(K))=\nu(K)
for all compact
\displaystyle K\subset Y
. Denote by
\displaystyle\mathrm{ValHausSpc}
the category of Hausdorff spaces
\displaystyle X
equipped with such valuations
\displaystyle\mu
, and compactly measure-preserving partial proper maps between them. We denote the full subcategory given by
\displaystyle X
equipped with a Radon valuation
\displaystyle\mu
by
\displaystyle\mathrm{RadHausSpc}
.

Similarly to the case of globally defined maps, any compactly measure-preserving partial proper map
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
