is in particular a measure-preserving partial map
\displaystyle(X,\mu_{*})\rightarrow(Y,\nu_{*})
, using the same reasoning with Theorem
7.9
instead of Theorem
7.11
. Denote the forget functor which sends
\displaystyle(X,\mu)
to
\displaystyle(X,\mu_{*})
by
\displaystyle L:\mathrm{ValHausSpc}\rightarrow\mathrm{MeasLoc}
.

Theorem 8.8
.

There exists a functor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(-)^{\mu}:\mathrm{ValHausSpc}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{MeasLoc}_{\mathrm{l.f.~faithf}}\\
X&\mapsto&X^{\mu}\end{array}

where
\displaystyle\mathrm{MeasLoc}_{\mathrm{l.f.~faithf}}
is the category of locally finite and faithful measure locales, and measure-preserving partial maps between them, together with a natural transformation
\displaystyle p:U(-)^{\mu}\rightarrow L
, where
\displaystyle U:\mathrm{MeasLoc}_{\mathrm{l.f.~faithf}}\rightarrow\mathrm{MeasLoc}
is the canonical inclusion, given in components by the map

p_{\mu}:X^{\mu}\rightarrow X.

This functor restricts to a functor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(-)^{\mu}:\mathrm{RadHausSpc}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{BoolMeasLoc}_{\mathrm{l.f.~faithf}}\\
\end{array}

where
\displaystyle\mathrm{BoolMeasLoc}_{\mathrm{l.f.~faithf}}
is the category of Boolean, locally finite and faithful measure locales, with measure-preserving partial maps between them.

Proof.

Since
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
being compactly measure-preserving means that
\displaystyle f^{-1}:\mathcal{K}(Y)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(X)
is a valuation-preserving homomorphism of lower bounded distributive lattices, we have a functor
\displaystyle\mathrm{ValHausSpc}^{op}\rightarrow\mathrm{ValSite}
. Composing with the functor
\displaystyle(-)^{inn}
provided by Theorem
4.38
gives the functor
\displaystyle(-)^{\mu}
.

Naturality of
\displaystyle p
follows analogously to the proof given for Theorem
8.6
by using that we have the natural diagram

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\mu}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\nu}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\mathcal{K}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\mathcal{K}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Rad}(f)}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\mathcal{K}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with the bottom square given by Theorem
7.9
, and the top square given by the observation that
\displaystyle f^{-1}:\mathcal{K}(Y)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(X)
is a morphism of sites. Analogously to the same statement made in Theorem
8.6
, the restriction to
\displaystyle\mathrm{RadHausSpc}
maps into Boolean locales by Theorem
6.18
.
∎

8.2.
Functoriality of the induced measure on the frame of sublocales

We also have functoriality with respect to the induced measure on sublocales in the case of Radon valuation. This uses the universal property of the map
\displaystyle\mathrm{can}:\mathfrak{Sl}(X)\rightarrow X
, which requires the use of globally defined measure-preserving maps. Denote by
\displaystyle\mathrm{RadHausSpc}_{glob}
the category of Hausdorff spaces equipped with Radon valuations, and globally defined measure-preserving proper maps between them.

Theorem 8.9
.

There exists a lift of the functor
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}:\mathrm{RadHausSpc}_{glob}\rightarrow\mathrm{Loc}
to a functor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{Sl}:\mathrm{RadHausSpc}_{glob}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{MeasLoc}_{glob}\\
(X,\mu)&\mapsto&(\mathfrak{Sl}(X),\mu_{*})\end{array}

where
\displaystyle\mathrm{MeasLoc}_{glob}
is the category of locales equipped with measures and globally defined measure-preserving maps between them.

Remark 8.10
.

This in particular means that the inner measure
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
defined on the locale
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
of sublocales of
\displaystyle X
is invariant under measure-preserving homeomorphisms of
\displaystyle X
.

Proof.

This is mostly a formal consequence of Theorem
8.6
. As described in the beginning of Section
8
, for a given Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
with Radon valuation
\displaystyle\mu
, we have a natural lift

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{Sl}(X)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\mu}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{can}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}

obtained by using Theorem
5.5
, since
\displaystyle X^{\mu}
is Boolean. Since
\displaystyle p:U\mathrm{Rad}\rightarrow L
is natural as proven in Theorem
8.6
, so is the lift to
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}
. All measures involved are induced via pushforward, and so all maps become measure-preserving.
∎

Corollary 8.11
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff space equipped with a Radon valuation
\displaystyle\mu
. Then there exists a measure
\displaystyle\mu_{*}
on the locale of sublocales
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
of
\displaystyle X
, which is invariant under measure-preserving homeomorphisms of
\displaystyle X
, and the natural continuous map
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(X)\rightarrow X
is measure-preserving.

Remark 8.12
.

In spirit at least this result is analogous to the results that were obtained independently by Simpson
[
Sim12
]
and Leroy
[
Ler13
]
. In both cases, Simpson and Leroy put a valuation
\displaystyle\mu^{*}
on
\displaystyle(\mathrm{Sl}(X),\subset)
, which mimics the construction of an outer measure. In the case of Simpson,
\displaystyle X
is assumed to be a fitted
\displaystyle\sigma
-locale, in the case of Leroy,
\displaystyle X
is assumed to be a regular locale, in both cases
\displaystyle\mu:\mathcal{O}(X)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
is assumed to be a valuation satisfying further conditions. The valuation
\displaystyle\mu*
is then shown to be cocontinuous, i.e.

\mu(\bigwedge_{i\in I}S_{i})=\inf_{i\in I}\mu(S_{i})

for any downward directed set of sublocales
\displaystyle S_{i}
(assuming finiteness in the case of Simpson), as well as
\displaystyle\sigma
-continuous, i.e.

\mu(\bigvee_{n\in\mathbb{N}}S_{n})=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mu(S_{n}).

The main conceptual difference for the approach presented in this article is that we instead construct the corresponding
co-measure

\displaystyle\mu_{*}
on
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(X)^{op}
. This has the main conceptual advantage that we are never leaving the world of locales.

8.3.
Regular contents

A particularly nice class of Radon valuations is obtained from the notion of a
regular content
. Regular contents have long been used in the classical construction of Radon measures. It is therefore not surprising that employing them in the construction of measure locales yields a theory essentially equivalent to the classical one. Their use also makes it easy to transfer classical constructions into the point-free setting, since in practice the essential geometric or logical information of a given measure theoretic situation is already captured in the construction of a regular content.

Definition 8.13

(See
[
Hal13
]
, Section 53)
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a topological space. A
content
on
\displaystyle X
is a function
\displaystyle\lambda:\mathcal{K}(X)\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
on the set of compact subsets such that the following conditions hold.

•

Monotone:
If
\displaystyle C\subset D
are compact sets, then
\displaystyle\lambda(C)\leq\lambda(D)
.

•

Subadditive for unions:
For all compact sets
\displaystyle C,D
we have

\mu(C\cup D)\leq\mu(C)+\mu(D).

•

Additive for disjoint unions:
For all compact sets
\displaystyle C,D
with
\displaystyle C\cap D=\emptyset
we have

\mu(C\cup D)=\mu(C)+\mu(D).

Note that
\displaystyle\mu(\emptyset)=\mu(\emptyset\cup\emptyset)=\mu(\emptyset)+\mu(\emptyset)
, therefore
\displaystyle\mu(\emptyset)=0
.

Definition 8.14
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a topological space, and let
\displaystyle K_{1},K_{2}
be compact subsets. We say
\displaystyle K_{1}\ll K_{2}
if there exists
\displaystyle U
open such that
\displaystyle K_{1}\subset U\subset K_{2}
.

Definition 8.15

(See
[
Hal13
]
, Section 54)
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a topological space. A content
\displaystyle\lambda:\mathcal{K}(X)\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
is called
regular
if
\displaystyle\lambda(K)=\inf\{\lambda(K^{\prime})~|~K\ll K^{\prime},~K^{\prime}\text{ compact}\}
for all compact subsets
\displaystyle K
.

Example 8.16
.

Every Radon measure
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
on a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
gives rise to a regular content, see the upcoming Section
12.2
for details. In fact, a standard procedure in order to construct a Radon measure on locally compact Hausdorff space is either to give a direct construction of a content, or equivalently to give a positive linear functional
\displaystyle C_{c}(X)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, where
\displaystyle C_{c}(X)
is the space of continuous functions with compact support via the Riesz–Markov–Kakutani representation theorem (See e.g.
[
Coh94
, Theorem 7.2.8]
). As such we can give many different examples of regular contents.

•

A discrete set
\displaystyle S
equipped with the counting measure on
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(S)=\mathcal{P}(S)^{fin}
.

•

The Lebesgue measure
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}
on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}
. More on this in Section
10.1
.

•

More generally, the Haar measure
\displaystyle\mu
on a locally compact Hausdorff group
\displaystyle G
, discussed in Section
10.2
.

•

The Dirac measure
\displaystyle\lambda_{x}
for any point
\displaystyle x\in X
.

•

The Volume measure on a Riemannian manifold
\displaystyle(M,g)
.

•

The Wiener measure on
\displaystyle C([0,1];\mathbb{R})
, modelling Brownian motion (See e.g.
[
Sch73
, VI.5]
).

We now want to compare the notion of a regular content with that of a valuation. Note that
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
need not be a lower bounded distributive lattice for general topological spaces, since intersections of compact sets need not be compact. For this reason we restrict our attention to
\displaystyle X
being Hausdorff.

Theorem 8.17
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff topological space and
\displaystyle\lambda:\mathcal{K}(X)\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
a regular content. Then
\displaystyle\lambda
is an almost Boolean valuation on
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
.

Proof.

We need to verify two things for the function
\displaystyle\lambda
:

Modularity:
Let
\displaystyle C_{1},C_{2}
be two compact subsets. We want to show that

\lambda(C_{1})+\lambda(C_{2})=\lambda(C_{1}\cup C_{2})+\lambda(C_{1}\cap C_{2}).

Using regularity of
\displaystyle\lambda
, let
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
and choose
\displaystyle C_{1}\cap C_{2}\subset V\subset K
with
\displaystyle V
open and
\displaystyle K
compact, such that
\displaystyle\mu(K)-\mu(C_{1}\cap C_{2})<\epsilon
.

We estimate for
\displaystyle i=1,2
:

\lambda(C_{i})\leq\lambda(C_{i}\setminus V\cup K)\leq\lambda(C_{i}\setminus V)+\lambda(K)\leq\lambda(C_{i}\setminus V)+\lambda(C_{1}\cap C_{2})+\epsilon\leq\lambda(C_{i})+\epsilon

and

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\lambda(C_{1}\cup C_{2})&\leq&\lambda(C_{1}\setminus V\cup C_{2}\setminus V\cup K)&\leq&\lambda(C_{1}\setminus V)+\lambda(C_{2}\setminus V)+\lambda(K)\\
&\leq&\lambda(C_{1}\setminus V)+\lambda(C_{2}\setminus V)+\lambda(C_{1}\cap C_{2})+\epsilon&\leq&\lambda(C_{1}\cup C_{2})+\epsilon.\end{array}

Putting these inequalities together we obtain

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\lambda(C_{1}\cup C_{2})+\lambda(C_{1}\cap C_{2})&\leq&\lambda(C_{1}\setminus V)+\lambda(C_{2}\setminus V)+2\lambda(C_{1}\cap C_{2})+\epsilon&\leq&\lambda(C_{1})+\lambda(C_{2})+\epsilon\\
&\leq&\lambda(C_{1}\setminus V)+\lambda(C_{2}\setminus V)+2\lambda(C_{1}\cap C_{2})+3\epsilon&\leq&\lambda(C_{1}\cup C_{2})+\lambda(C_{1}\cap C_{2})+3\epsilon.\end{array}

Letting
\displaystyle\epsilon
tend to zero shows the claim.

Almost Boolean:
Let
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
,
\displaystyle C
a compact set and assume that
\displaystyle C_{1}\subset C_{2}\subset\ldots
is an ascending chain of compact sets such that
\displaystyle C_{n}\subset C
for all
\displaystyle n\in\mathbb{N}
. Using regularity of
\displaystyle\lambda
, for all
\displaystyle n\in\mathbb{N}
we find

\displaystyle C_{n}\subset U_{n}\subset K_{n},
where
\displaystyle U_{n}
are open and
\displaystyle K_{n}
compact, such that

\mu(K_{n})-\mu(C_{n})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

Without loss of generality, we may also assume that
\displaystyle U_{n}\subset U_{n+1}
and
\displaystyle K_{n}\subset K_{n+1}
. Define
\displaystyle U=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}U_{n}
. We claim that the compact set
\displaystyle C\setminus U
is an approximate complement to the sequence
\displaystyle C_{n}
relative to
\displaystyle C
. To see this, again using regularity, choose
\displaystyle C\setminus U\subset W\subset L
with
\displaystyle W
open and
\displaystyle L
compact such that

\mu(L)-\mu(C\setminus U)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

The open sets
\displaystyle U_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}
together with
\displaystyle W
form an open cover of
\displaystyle C
. By compactness, we conclude that there exists
\displaystyle N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\displaystyle C\subset U_{N}\cup W
. Since
\displaystyle U_{N}\subset K_{N}
and
\displaystyle W\subset L
we have

\mu(C)\leq\mu(K_{N})+\mu(L)\leq\mu(C_{N})+\mu(C\setminus U)+\epsilon,

which concludes the proof.
∎

If
\displaystyle\lambda
is a regular content, the map
\displaystyle\tilde{p}:X^{\lambda}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
induces a measure
\displaystyle\lambda_{*}
on the frame
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(X)^{op}
, or equivalently a co-measure on
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}
. The measure of an open
\displaystyle U
, equivalently co-measure of the sublocale
\displaystyle U^{c}\hookrightarrow X
is given as
\displaystyle\lambda_{*}(U)=\sup_{K\subset U\text{ compact}}\lambda(K)
, almost by definition. We claim that the same formula holds for the measure of a closed subset, or equivalently the co-measure of the open inclusion
\displaystyle U\hookrightarrow X
.

Proposition 8.18
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff space and
\displaystyle\lambda
a regular content on
\displaystyle X
. Let
\displaystyle C\subset X
be a closed subset, identified with the open sublocale
\displaystyle C^{c}\hookrightarrow X\in\mathrm{Sl}(X)^{op}
. Then

\tilde{p}^{*}(C)=\{K\subset C~|~K\text{ compact}\}^{sh}\in\mathcal{O}(X^{\lambda}).

In particular

\mu_{*}(C)=\sup_{K\subset C\text{ compact}}\lambda(K).

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle U=C^{c}
. Under the inverse image functor of the map
\displaystyle X^{\lambda}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
, the closed set
\displaystyle C
is sent to

\neg p_{\lambda}^{*}(U)=\{K~|~K\text{ compact, s.t. }\mu(K\cap K^{\prime})=0\text{ for all }K^{\prime}\subset U\text{ compact}\}.

by Lemma
4.31
. We claim that this ideal arises as the closure under
\displaystyle\mu
-approximations of the set

\{K\subset C~|~K\text{ compact}\}.

Let
\displaystyle M
be compact such that
\displaystyle\mu(M\cap K^{\prime})=0
for all
\displaystyle M\subset U\text{ compact}
. Then
\displaystyle M\setminus U
is again compact. Let
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
and use regularity of
\displaystyle\lambda
to find
\displaystyle M\setminus U\subset V\subset K^{\prime\prime}
, with
\displaystyle V
open,
\displaystyle K^{\prime\prime}
compact, such that
\displaystyle\mu(K^{\prime\prime})-\mu(M\setminus U)<\epsilon
.
Then
\displaystyle M\setminus V\subset M\cap C
and therefore
\displaystyle\mu(M\setminus V)=0
. Therefore

\mu(M)\leq\mu(K^{\prime\prime})+\mu(M\setminus V)<\mu(M\setminus U)+\mu(M\setminus V)+\epsilon=\mu(M\setminus U)+\epsilon.

Since
\displaystyle\epsilon
was arbitrary, we conclude
\displaystyle\mu(K\setminus U)=\mu(K)
. But this means that
\displaystyle K
is in the
\displaystyle\mu
-closure of
\displaystyle\{K\subset C~|~K\text{ compact}\}
.
∎

Corollary 8.19
.

Let
\displaystyle\lambda
be a regular content on a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
, and
\displaystyle K\subset X
compact. Then
\displaystyle[K]
and
\displaystyle p^{*}(K^{c})
are complementary in
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X^{\lambda})
.

Proof.

Proposition
8.18
applied to
\displaystyle K
gives that
\displaystyle\tilde{p}^{*}(C)=[K]
, which is by construction of the map
\displaystyle\tilde{p}
the complement of
\displaystyle p^{*}(K^{c})=\tilde{p}^{*}(K^{c})
.
∎

Theorem 8.20
.

Let
\displaystyle\lambda
be a regular content on a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
. Then the induced map
\displaystyle\tilde{p}:X^{\lambda}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
is an embedding.

Proof.

By definition of
\displaystyle\tilde{p}:X^{\lambda}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
, it’s inverse image functor
\displaystyle\tilde{p}:\mathrm{Sl}(X)^{op}
sends an open sublocale
\displaystyle U\hookrightarrow X
to the complement of
\displaystyle p^{*}(U)
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X^{\mu})
. Lemma
8.19
implies that all chunks
\displaystyle[K]\in\mathcal{O}(X^{\mu})
lie in the image of
\displaystyle\tilde{p}
, for
\displaystyle K\subset X
compact. Since these generate all of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X^{\mu})
under suprema, we see that
\displaystyle\tilde{p}^{*}
is surjective.
∎

Remark 8.21
.

The fact that the measure algebra can be obtained as a Boolean sublocale of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(L)
has been observed by Leroy in
[
Ler13
, Théoremè 2]
in the context of a locally finite measure
\displaystyle\mu
on a regular locale
\displaystyle L
. Leroy characterizes this sublocale of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(L)
as given by the set of
\displaystyle\mu
-reduced sublocales of
\displaystyle L
. Leroy’s characterization provides an independent approach to the construction of the corresponding measurable locale.

Let
\displaystyle X^{disc}
denote the underlying set of
\displaystyle X
equipped with the discrete topology. As remarked earlier in Remark
8.3
, we have a continuous map
\displaystyle\mathrm{can}:X^{disc}\rightarrow X
. Since
\displaystyle X^{disc}
is Boolean, there exists a lift to a map
\displaystyle\varphi
into
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
,

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{Sl}(X)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{disc}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{can}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!~\varphi}

Concretely,
\displaystyle\varphi
gives an adjunction

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}(X)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sl}(X)^{op}}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

where
\displaystyle\varphi^{*}
sends a closed sublocale
\displaystyle U^{c}
to the open subset
\displaystyle U\subset X
, and the open sublocale
\displaystyle U
to the closed subset
\displaystyle U^{c}\subset X
. In particular, a sublocale of the form
\displaystyle U\vee V^{c}
for opens
\displaystyle U,V
is sent to the
locally closed
subset
\displaystyle U^{c}\cap V\subset X
. The right adjoint
\displaystyle\varphi_{*}
can also be described concretely. If
\displaystyle S\subset X
is a subset, it is sent to

\varphi_{*}(S)=\bigvee_{M~|~i^{*}(M)\subset S}M=\bigvee_{U,V\text{ open}~|~U^{c}\cap V\subset S}U\wedge V^{c}

with the suprema and meet taken in the frame
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(X)^{op}
. (It corresponds to the infimum over
\displaystyle U\vee V^{c}
in the co-frame
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(X)
.) In other words, the sublocale associated to a subset can be understood by the locally closed sets contained in it.

Corollary 8.22
.

Let
\displaystyle\lambda
be a regular content on a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
, and let
\displaystyle S\subset X
be a subset. The induced measure of
\displaystyle S
is given by

\lambda_{*}(S)=\sup\{\lambda(K)~|~K\subset S\text{ compact}\}.

Remark 8.23
.

In particular, the induced measure agrees with the classical measure when
\displaystyle S
is a measurable subset of
\displaystyle X
.

Proof.

By the above discussion we have

\lambda_{*}(S)=\sup\{\lambda_{*}(U^{c}\cap V)~|~U^{c}\cap V\subset S\text{ s.t. }U,V\text{ open}\}.

Therefore the formula reduces to the case of locally closed sets. The case for open sets holds by definition, the case for closed sets is given by Proposition
8.18
. The measure of the intersection of both cases is computed via the measure of the intersection of the corresponding
\displaystyle\mu
-ideals of compact sets, hence the formula is true for locally closed sets.
∎

The map
\displaystyle X^{\lambda}\rightarrow X
is actually universal among maps from Boolean locales equipped with locally finite and faithful measures, a result that was anticipated by Simon Henry in the mathoverflow post
[
httb
]
.

Theorem 8.24
.

Let
\displaystyle\lambda
be a regular content on a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
and assume that the induced measure
\displaystyle\lambda_{*}
on
\displaystyle X
is locally finite.
11
11

11

This condition is automatically satisfied if
\displaystyle X
is locally compact, see Lemma
9.3
.
Suppose
\displaystyle f:(Y,\mu)\rightarrow(X,\lambda_{*})
is a measure-preserving continuous map, with
\displaystyle Y
being Boolean, and
\displaystyle\mu
a locally finite and faithful measure on
\displaystyle Y
. Then there exists a unique measure-preserving map
\displaystyle\tilde{f}:(Y,\mu)\rightarrow(X^{\lambda},\lambda_{*})
making the triangle

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\lambda}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

commute.

Proof.

By virtue of
\displaystyle Y
being Boolean, there exists a unique lift
\displaystyle\bar{f}
of
\displaystyle f
against
\displaystyle\mathrm{can}:\mathfrak{Sl}(X)\rightarrow X
by Theorem
5.5
,

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{Sl}(X)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{f}}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{can}}

Since all measures involved are defined via pushforwards, this lift is automatically measure-preserving. Denote by
\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}_{*}
the induced measure on
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
. Showing that
\displaystyle\bar{f}
factors through the embedding
\displaystyle\tilde{p}:X^{\lambda}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
provided by Theorem
8.20
is equivalent to showing that for each pair
\displaystyle S\leq S^{\prime}
of sublocales in
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sl}(X)^{op}
such that
\displaystyle\tilde{p}^{*}(S)=\tilde{p}^{*}(S^{\prime})
we have
\displaystyle\bar{f}^{*}(S)=\bar{f}^{*}(S^{\prime})
. Note that

\mu(\bar{f}^{*}(S))=\tilde{\lambda}_{*}(S)=\lambda_{*}(\tilde{p}_{*}(S))=\lambda_{*}(\tilde{p}_{*}(S^{\prime}))=\tilde{\lambda}_{*}(S^{\prime})=\mu(\bar{f}^{*}(S^{\prime})).

Using that
\displaystyle\mu
is locally finite and faithful, this means that
\displaystyle\bar{f}^{*}(S)=\bar{f}^{*}(S^{\prime})
for each pair of sublocale
\displaystyle S\leq S^{\prime}
of
\displaystyle X
of finite measure. To show the same even in the case of infinite measure, note that the condition that
\displaystyle\lambda_{*}
is locally finite on
\displaystyle X
implies that
\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}_{*}
is also locally finite on
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(X)
by Lemma
4.11
. But this means that any sublocale
\displaystyle S
can be written as a supremum of sublocales of finite measure, and hence we can reduce to the case of finite measure sublocales. This shows that
\displaystyle\bar{f}^{*}
factors through
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X^{\mu})
.
∎

Finally, we give a criterion for identifying points of
\displaystyle X^{\mu}
.

Proposition 8.25
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff space,
\displaystyle\lambda
a regular content and
\displaystyle X^{\lambda}
the associated Boolean inner measure locale. Then the natural map
\displaystyle p_{\lambda}:X^{\lambda}\rightarrow X
induces an injection

\mathrm{pts}(X^{\lambda})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{pts}(X)

which identifies the left-hand side with the set of point
\displaystyle x
of
\displaystyle X
such that
\displaystyle\lambda(\{x\})>0
.

Proof.

Since
\displaystyle X^{\lambda}
is Boolean, by Lemma
5.12
points of
\displaystyle X^{\lambda}
can be identified with minimal, non-zero
\displaystyle\lambda
-ideals of
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
. By minimality, such a
\displaystyle\lambda
-ideal needs to be of the form
\displaystyle[K]
for some compact
\displaystyle K
with
\displaystyle\lambda(K)>0
. If
\displaystyle K
contains a point
\displaystyle x
such that
\displaystyle\lambda(\{x\})>0
, we again by minimality conclude that
\displaystyle[K]=[\{x\}]
. This point is sent under
\displaystyle p_{\lambda}
to the open neighbourhood filter of
\displaystyle X
, or equivalently just
\displaystyle x
. Now we argue by contradiction. Assume that for all points
\displaystyle x
of
\displaystyle K
we have
\displaystyle\lambda(\{x\})=0
. Let
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
, such that
\displaystyle 2\epsilon<\mu(K)
. Using regularity twice we find for each
\displaystyle x\in X
a sequence
\displaystyle x\in U_{x}\subset K_{x}\subset V_{x}\subset C_{x}
with
\displaystyle U_{x},V_{x}
open and
\displaystyle K_{x},C_{x}
compact, and

\begin{array}[]{l}\lambda(K_{x})<\epsilon\text{ as well as}\\
\lambda(C_{x})<2\epsilon.\end{array}

The sets
\displaystyle\{U_{x}\}_{x\in K}
form an open cover of
\displaystyle K
, therefore there exist finitely many
\displaystyle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in K
such that the sets
\displaystyle U_{x_{1}},\ldots,U_{x_{n}}
cover
\displaystyle K
. We can write

K=(K\cap K_{x_{1}})\cup\ldots\cup(K\cap K_{x_{1}})

as a finite union of compact sets of measure less then
\displaystyle\epsilon
. Since
\displaystyle\lambda(K)>0
, we must have that
\displaystyle\lambda(K\cap K_{x_{k}})>0
for at least one
\displaystyle k
. Observe that:

•

\displaystyle\lambda(K\setminus V_{x_{k}})<\lambda(K)
, as

\lambda(K\setminus V_{x_{k}})+\lambda(K\cap K_{x_{k}})\leq\lambda(K)

and
\displaystyle\lambda(K\cap K_{x_{k}})>0
.

•

\displaystyle\lambda(K\setminus V_{x_{k}})>0
, as

2\epsilon<\mu(K)\leq\mu(C_{x_{k}})+\lambda(K\setminus V_{x_{k}})<2\epsilon+\lambda(K\setminus V_{x_{k}}).

This means that
\displaystyle[K\setminus V_{x_{k}}]\neq 0
, and also
\displaystyle[K\setminus V_{x_{k}}]\leq[K]
but
\displaystyle[K\setminus V_{x_{k}}]
cannot be equal to
\displaystyle[K]
, since its measure is strictly smaller. This is a contradiction to minimality of
\displaystyle[K]
.
∎

8.4.
Density of
\displaystyle p_{\mu}

We will describe a useful property of the map
\displaystyle p_{\mu}:X^{\mu}\rightarrow X
in the case of many practical situations.

Definition 8.26
.

Let
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
be a map of locales. Then
\displaystyle f
is called
dense
if
\displaystyle f_{*}(0)=0
.

Proposition 8.27
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff space and
\displaystyle\mu
a Radon valuation. Assume that
\displaystyle\mu_{*}(U)>0
for all non-empty open sets
\displaystyle U\subset X
, or equivalently that for all non-empty open sets
\displaystyle U
there exists
\displaystyle K\subset U
compact with
\displaystyle\mu(K)>0
. Then the map

p_{\mu}:X^{\mu}\rightarrow X

is dense.

Example 8.28
.

An important class of examples of Radon valuations
\displaystyle\mu
satisfying the condition
\displaystyle\mu(U)>0
for all non-empty open sets
\displaystyle U\subset X
arises when
\displaystyle X
is a locally compact Hausdorff group and
\displaystyle\mu
is the Haar measure on
\displaystyle X
, as described in Section
10.2
.

Example 8.29
.

As a trivial example where Proposition
8.27
fails, consider the Dirac measure
\displaystyle\delta_{x}
at a point
\displaystyle x\in X
. For this example we have that
\displaystyle X^{\mu}=\mathrm{pt}
, and density of
\displaystyle p_{\mu}
only if
\displaystyle X
itself is a point.

Proof.

Under the composition

\mathcal{O}(X^{\mu})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}(X^{\mathcal{K}})\xrightarrow{\theta_{*}}\mathcal{O}(X)

the element
\displaystyle 0
is first sent to the ideal
\displaystyle N=\{K\subset X~|~\mu(K)=0\}
, then further to

\theta_{*}(N)=\bigvee_{U~:~\theta^{*}(U)\leq N}U=\bigvee\{U~|~\mu(K)=0\text{ for all }K\subset U\text{ compact}\}=\bigvee_{U~:~\mu_{*}(U)=0}U.

This open is the empty set iff
\displaystyle\mu_{*}(U)>0
for all non-empty opens.
∎

Recall that dense continuous maps between topological spaces behave like epimorphisms with respect to target spaces that are Hausdorff. A similar situation holds for dense maps between locales, although the situation is not quite equivalent.

Definition 8.30
.

A locale
\displaystyle L
is called
Hausdorff
, (also
Isbell-Hausdorff
), if the diagonal

L\rightarrow L\times L

is a closed map of locales.

Remark 8.31
.

Hausdorff topological spaces need not be Isbell-Hausdorff if they are considered as locales. The problem lies in the fact that products of topological spaces are not necessarily preserved by the functor
\displaystyle\mathrm{Top}\rightarrow\mathrm{Loc}
. This subtlety disappears for locally compact spaces, where the two notions of Hausdorffness agree. It is also true that Isbell-Hausdorffness of the corresponding locale of a topological space
\displaystyle X
implies that
\displaystyle X
is Hausdorff.

Proposition 8.32

(
[
PP12
]
, V 2.5.3)
.

Let
\displaystyle f:L\rightarrow M
be a dense map between locales, and
\displaystyle g_{1},g_{2}:M\rightarrow N
be two maps with
\displaystyle N
a Hausdorff locale. Suppose
\displaystyle g_{1}f=g_{2}f
. Then
\displaystyle g_{1}=g_{2}
.

We obtain as an immediate consequence.

Corollary 8.33
.

Suppose the situation of Proposition
8.27
holds, and let
\displaystyle Y
be a Hausdorff locale. Then the continuous map
\displaystyle p_{\mu}:X^{\mu}\rightarrow X
induces an injection

\mathrm{Map}(X,Y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}(X^{\mu},Y)

of continuous maps into random variables.

Remark 8.34
.

One common criticism towards a point-free approach to measure theory is the statement that modding out by null sets makes it awkward or even impossible to study the local behaviour of equivalence classes of measurable functions. Proposition
8.27
shows that this fear is mostly misplaced. Not only does the point-free approach avoid talking about equivalence classes altogether, but moreover in many practical applications it is simply a (local) property of a map
\displaystyle X^{\mu}\rightarrow Y
to be induced by a continuous map
\displaystyle X\rightarrow Y
, assuming one asks for the existence of enough open neighbourhoods of non-zero measure.

9.
Measures on locally compact Hausdorff spaces

Quite a few things simplify when working with regular contents on
locally compact
Hausdorff spaces. Recall that a space
\displaystyle X
is called locally compact, if for each open
\displaystyle U
and
\displaystyle x\in U
, there exist
\displaystyle V,K
such that
\displaystyle x\in V\subset K\subset U
with
\displaystyle V
open and
\displaystyle K
compact. It follows that the same holds when
\displaystyle x
is replaced by a compact subset.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be locally compact Hausdorff and
\displaystyle K\subset U
with
\displaystyle K
compact and
\displaystyle U
open. Then there exist
\displaystyle V
open and
\displaystyle K^{\prime}
compact such that
\displaystyle K\subset V\subset K^{\prime}\subset U
.

It follows that the relation
\displaystyle\ll
between compact sets becomes
interpolative
, which means that whenever
\displaystyle K\ll K^{\prime}
for two compact subsets, there exists
\displaystyle K^{\prime\prime}
such that
\displaystyle K\ll K^{\prime\prime}\ll K^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Choose for each
\displaystyle x\in K
sets
\displaystyle V(x)\subset K(x)\subset U
with
\displaystyle V(x)
open and
\displaystyle K(x)
compact. Since
\displaystyle K
is compact, finitely many such
\displaystyle x
suffice for the sets
\displaystyle V(x)
to cover
\displaystyle K
. Define
\displaystyle V
and
\displaystyle K^{\prime}
as these finite unions.
∎

Remark 9.2
.

Unlike the notion of Hausdorffness, the notion of locally compact Hausdorff spaces can be done in a completely point free fashion, as the category of locally compact Hausdorff spaces is equivalent to the category of completely regular and locally compact locales, where a locale
\displaystyle L
is called locally compact if its frame
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(L)
is a
continuous
frame. For more details, see
[
PP12
]
, Chapters V.5 and VII.6.

Lemma 9.3
.

Suppose
\displaystyle X
is locally compact Hausdorff and
\displaystyle\mu
a valuation on
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
. Then the associated measure
\displaystyle\mu_{*}:\mathcal{O}(X)\rightarrow[0,\infty]
is locally finite.

Proof.

Recall that
\displaystyle\mu_{*}(U)=\sup_{K\subset U\text{ compact}}\mu(K)
for an open
\displaystyle U
by definition. If
\displaystyle U
has compact closure
\displaystyle\bar{U}
, then
\displaystyle\mu(K)\leq\mu(\bar{U})<\infty
for all
\displaystyle K\subset U
compact and therefore also
\displaystyle\mu_{*}(U)\leq\mu(\bar{U})<\infty
. Since
\displaystyle X
is locally compact, we have that

X=\bigcup\{U~|~U\text{ open with compact closure}\}

is a union of opens with finite measure.
∎

Regular contents are particularly natural in the context of locally compact Hausdorff spaces, in which case they correspond uniquely to locally finite measures.

Theorem 9.4
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space. The assignment

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(-)_{*}:\mathrm{RegCont}(X)&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Meas}_{\mathrm{loc.~fin.}}(X)\\
\lambda&\mapsto&\lambda_{*}\end{array}

is a bijection between the set of regular contents on
\displaystyle X
and the set of locally finite measures on
\displaystyle X
.

Proof.

Let us define an inverse to
\displaystyle(-)_{*}
. Given a locally finite measure
\displaystyle\mu
on
\displaystyle X
, define

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mu^{\mathcal{K}}:\mathcal{K}(X)&\rightarrow&[0,\infty)\\
K&\mapsto&\mu^{\mathcal{K}}(K)=\inf\{\mu(U)~|~K\subset U,U\text{ open}\}\end{array}

First of all, this is well-defined. Since
\displaystyle\mu
is locally finite, there exists a cover
\displaystyle U_{i},i\in I,
of
\displaystyle X
such that
\displaystyle\mu(U_{i})<\infty
. If
\displaystyle K
is compact, it must be contained in finitely many of the
\displaystyle U_{i}
, therefore
\displaystyle K
is contained in an open of finite measure, and hence
\displaystyle\mu^{\mathcal{K}}(K)<\infty
. Now let us show that
\displaystyle\mu^{\mathcal{K}}
is a regular content.

•

Monotone:
Let
\displaystyle K_{1}\subset K_{2}
be two compact sets. Then
\displaystyle K_{2}\subset U
for
\displaystyle U
open implies
\displaystyle K_{1}\subset U
, therefore
\displaystyle\mu^{\mathcal{K}}(K_{1})\leq\mu^{\mathcal{K}}(K_{2})
.

•

Subadditive:
Let
\displaystyle K_{1},K_{2}
be compact. Then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mu^{\mathcal{K}}(K_{1}\cup K_{2})&=&\inf\{\mu(U)~|~K_{1}\cup K_{2}\subset U,U\text{ open}\}\\
&\leq&\inf\{\mu(U_{1}\cup U_{2})~|~K_{1}\subset U_{1},K_{2}\subset U_{2},U_{1},U_{2}\text{ open}\}\\
&\leq&\inf\{\mu(U_{1})+\mu(U_{2})~|~K_{1}\subset U_{1},K_{2}\subset U_{2},U_{1},U_{2}\text{ open}\}\\
&=&\mu^{\mathcal{K}}(K_{1})+\mu^{\mathcal{K}}(K_{2})\end{array}

•

Additive on disjoint pairs:
Now let
\displaystyle K_{1},K_{2}
be compact such that
\displaystyle K_{1}\cap K_{2}=\emptyset
. Since
\displaystyle X
is Hausdorff, by Lemma
7.1
there exists disjoint open sets
\displaystyle V_{1},V_{2}
such that
\displaystyle K_{1}\subset U_{1}
and
\displaystyle K_{2}\subset U_{2}
. Therefore, if
\displaystyle K_{1}\cup K_{2}\subset U
for
\displaystyle U
open, we obtain
\displaystyle U_{1}=U\cap V_{1},U_{2}=U\cap V_{2}
such that
\displaystyle U_{1}
and
\displaystyle U_{2}
are disjoint and

K_{1}\subset U_{1},K_{2}\subset U_{2}\text{ and }U_{1}\cup U_{2}\leq U.

Therefore

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mu^{\mathcal{K}}(K_{1}\cup K_{2})&=&\inf\{\mu(U)~|~K_{1}\cup K_{2}\subset U,U\text{ open}\}\\
&=&\inf\{\mu(U_{1}\cup U_{2})~|~K_{1}\subset U_{1},K_{2}\subset U_{2},U_{1},U_{2}\text{ open and disjoint}\}\\
&=&\inf\{\mu(U_{1})~|~K_{1}\subset U_{1},U_{1}\text{ open}\}+\inf\{\mu(U_{2})~|~K_{2}\subset U_{2},U_{2}\text{ open}\}\\
&=&\mu^{\mathcal{K}}(K_{1})+\mu^{\mathcal{K}}(K_{2}).\end{array}

•

Regular:
Let
\displaystyle K
be compact. By definition if
\displaystyle K\ll K^{\prime}
, there exists open
\displaystyle U
such that
\displaystyle K\subset U\subset K^{\prime}
, hence

\mu^{\mathcal{K}}(K)\leq\mu(U)\leq\mu^{\mathcal{K}}(K^{\prime}).

Conversely, if
\displaystyle K\subset U
for
\displaystyle U
open, since
\displaystyle X
is locally compact Hausdorff, by Lemma
9.1
there exist
\displaystyle K\subset V\subset K^{\prime}\subset U
with
\displaystyle V
open and
\displaystyle K^{\prime}
compact. Therefore

\mu^{\mathcal{K}}(K)=\inf\{\mu(U)~|~K\subset U,U\text{ open}\}=\inf\{\mu^{\mathcal{K}}(K^{\prime})~|~K\ll K^{\prime}\}.

We now claim that
\displaystyle\mu
will be recovered from
\displaystyle\mu^{\mathcal{K}}
. If
\displaystyle U
is open, we have

U=\bigcup_{V\ll U}V

where we write
\displaystyle V\ll U
whenever there exists a compact
\displaystyle K
such that
\displaystyle V\subset K\subset U
. Using continuity of
\displaystyle\mu
we see that

\mu(U)=\sup_{V\ll U}\mu(V)=\sup_{K\subset U,\text{ compact}}\mu^{\mathcal{K}}(K)=(\mu^{\mathcal{K}})_{*}(U).

Conversely, if we start with a regular content
\displaystyle\lambda
, we know that

\lambda(K)=\inf_{K\ll K^{\prime}}\lambda(K^{\prime})=\inf_{K\subset U,\text{ open}}\lambda_{*}(U)=(\lambda_{*})^{\mathcal{K}}(K).

This shows that
\displaystyle(-)_{*}
and
\displaystyle(-)^{\mathcal{K}}
are inverse to each other.
∎

Remark 9.5
.

Since pushforward of a locally finite measure against a partially defined proper map
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
with open support again produces a locally finite measure (Proposition
4.8
), this means that we obtain a functor

\mathrm{RegCont}:\mathrm{LocCHaus}_{\mathrm{part}~\mathrm{prop}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Set}

with
\displaystyle\mathrm{LocCHaus}_{\mathrm{part}~\mathrm{prop}}
being the category of locally compact Hausdorff spaces and partially defined proper maps with open support between them, and
\displaystyle\mathrm{RegCont}(X)\cong\mathrm{Meas}_{\mathrm{loc.fin}}(X)
the set of regular contents, and can be identified classically with the set of positive Radon measures on
\displaystyle X
. This functor is a sheaf when restricted to open inclusions, as shown in Theorem
4.19
.

Example 9.6
.

As a slightly exotic, but interesting example of a regular content, consider Example
4.14
. Recall that we fix
\displaystyle X
to be a geometry, such as Euclidean space
\displaystyle E^{n}
, the sphere
\displaystyle S^{n}
or hyperbolic space
\displaystyle H^{n}
, of dimension
\displaystyle n
, and consider the associated lower bounded distributive lattice
\displaystyle D(X)
given by
\displaystyle n
-dimensional polytopes. The locally coherent space
\displaystyle L(D(X),fin)={{X_{\mathrm{poly}}}}
is in fact Hausdorff, since
\displaystyle D(X)
is a Boolean ring (see Definition
9.12
and Lemma
9.13
below), and comes with a continuous, proper map
\displaystyle{X_{\mathrm{poly}}}\rightarrow X
, which is
\displaystyle G
-equivariant, where
\displaystyle G
is the group of isometries of
\displaystyle X
, and for which
\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{poly}}
is a polytopal, highly disconnected version of the classical cover
\displaystyle C\rightarrow[0,1]
by the Cantor set.

Extending the volume of a polytope produces the locally finite measure
\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{n}
on
\displaystyle{X_{\mathrm{poly}}}
, and therefore a regular content on the set of compact subsets of
\displaystyle{X_{\mathrm{poly}}}
(generated via intersections of compact opens, which correspond formally to polytopes). This means there is an associated measurable locale
\displaystyle(X_{\mathrm{poly}})_{Leb}=(X_{\mathrm{poly}})^{\mathrm{Vol}_{n}}
together with an induced faithful and locally finite measure
\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{n}
and a measure-preserving map

(X_{\mathrm{poly}})_{Leb}\rightarrow X_{\mathrm{poly}},

and with it an integration theory built purely formally from the combinatorial (!) notion of
\displaystyle n
-dimensional polytopes, together with their volume, which works formally the same as traditional Lebesgue measure. If
\displaystyle G
is a group of isometries of
\displaystyle X
, it acts naturally on
\displaystyle(X_{\mathrm{poly}})_{Leb}
.

Furthermore, pushforward of the measure along
\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{poly}}\rightarrow X
equips
\displaystyle X
with the classical Lebesgue measure, as for any open set
\displaystyle U\subset X
, it is a standard fact that

\mu(U)=\sup\{\mu(P)~|~P\subset U,~P\text{ is }n\text{-dim. polytope}\}.

In this sense, Lebesgue measure is obtained from the combinatorial notion of measure of a polytope, a fact that should not be too surprising, but nonetheless pleasant to observe from purely formal constructions.

Remark 9.7
.

If
\displaystyle X
is locally compact Hausdorff, then any valuation
\displaystyle\nu:\mathcal{K}(X)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
can be used to construct a regular content. The measure
\displaystyle\nu_{*}
on
\displaystyle X^{\nu}=L(\mathcal{K}(X),\nu)
induces via pushforward along
\displaystyle p_{\nu}:X^{\nu}\rightarrow X
a measure on
\displaystyle X
. This measure is locally finite, and hence induces the regular content

\tilde{\nu}(K)=\inf_{K\ll K^{\prime}}\nu(K^{\prime}).

We want to state a similar statement with regard to contents. Any content on a locally compact Hausdorff space can be used to construct a regular content.

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space and let
\displaystyle\lambda:\mathcal{K}(X)\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
be a content. Then the function

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\tilde{\lambda}:\mathcal{K}(X)&\rightarrow&[0,+\infty)\\
K&\mapsto&\inf\{\lambda(K^{\prime})~|~K\ll K^{\prime}\}\end{array}

is a regular content on
\displaystyle X
. The associated inner measure
\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}_{*}
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X)
is given by

\tilde{\lambda}_{*}(U)=\sup_{K\subset U\text{ compact}}\lambda(K).

Remark 9.9
.

Traditionally, Lemma
9.8
appears quite often implicitly when constructing a Radon measure. Typically, a content
\displaystyle\lambda
is used to construct an induced Radon measure, see e.g. Halmos
[
Hal13
, §53 and §54]
. Restricting this induced measure to the set of compact subsets gives
\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}
. The proof of Lemma
9.8
follows closely with the proof of §54, Theorem C in Halmos.

Proof.

We verify the four conditions.

•

Monotone:
Let
\displaystyle K_{1}\subset K_{2}
be two compact sets. Suppose
\displaystyle K_{2}\ll K^{\prime}
. Then clearly
\displaystyle K_{1}\ll K^{\prime}
as well. Therefore

\tilde{\lambda}(K_{1})=\inf\{\lambda(K^{\prime})~|~K_{1}\ll K^{\prime}\}\leq\inf\{\lambda(K^{\prime})~|~K_{2}\ll K^{\prime}\}=\tilde{\lambda}(K_{2}).

•

Subadditive:
Let
\displaystyle K_{1}
and
\displaystyle K_{2}
be two compact sets. Suppose
\displaystyle K_{1}\ll K_{1}^{\prime}
and
\displaystyle K_{2}\ll K_{2}^{\prime}
. Then clearly
\displaystyle K_{1}\cup K_{2}\ll K_{1}^{\prime}\cup K_{2}^{\prime}
. Therefore

\begin{array}[]{rl}&\tilde{\lambda}(K_{1}\cup K_{2})\leq\inf\{\lambda(K_{1}^{\prime}\cup K_{2}^{\prime})~|~K_{1}\ll K_{1}^{\prime}\text{ and }K_{2}\ll K_{2}^{\prime}\}\\
\leq&\inf\{\lambda(K_{1}^{\prime})+\lambda(K_{2}^{\prime})~|~K_{1}\ll K_{1}^{\prime}\text{ and }K_{2}\ll K_{2}^{\prime}\}=\tilde{\lambda}(K_{1})+\tilde{\lambda}(K_{2}).\end{array}

•

Additive on disjoint pairs:
Let
\displaystyle K_{1}
and
\displaystyle K_{2}
be two disjoint compact sets. Since
\displaystyle X
is Hausdorff, the two compact sets can be separated, i.e. there exists disjoint opens
\displaystyle U_{1},U_{2}
such that
\displaystyle K_{1}\subset U_{1}
and
\displaystyle K_{2}\subset U_{2}
by Lemma
7.1
. Using that
\displaystyle X
is locally compact as well, by Lemma
9.1
we also find
\displaystyle K_{1}^{\prime},K_{2}^{\prime}
compact such that
\displaystyle K_{1}\ll K_{1}^{\prime}\subset U_{1}
and
\displaystyle K_{2}\ll K_{2}^{\prime}\subset U_{2}
. In particular,
\displaystyle K_{1}^{\prime}
and
\displaystyle K_{2}^{\prime}
are disjoint as well. Therefore, if
\displaystyle K_{1}\cup K_{2}\ll K^{\prime}
for some compact
\displaystyle K^{\prime}
, we have

K_{1}\cup K_{2}\ll(K^{\prime}\cap K_{1}^{\prime})\cup(K^{\prime}\cap K_{2}^{\prime})\subset K^{\prime}

as well, with
\displaystyle K_{1}\ll(K^{\prime}\cap K_{1}^{\prime})
and
\displaystyle K_{2}\ll(K^{\prime}\cap K_{2}^{\prime})
, and the sets
\displaystyle K^{\prime}\cap K_{1}^{\prime}
and
\displaystyle K^{\prime}\cap K_{2}^{\prime}
being disjoint. We conclude that

\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip 21.52771pt\tilde{\lambda}(K_{1}\cup K_{2})=\inf\{\lambda(K_{1}^{\prime}\cup K_{2}^{\prime})~|~K_{1}\ll K_{1}^{\prime},K_{2}\ll K_{2}^{\prime}\text{ and }K_{1}^{\prime}\cap K_{2}^{\prime}=\emptyset\}=\tilde{\lambda}(K_{1})+\tilde{\lambda}(K_{2}).\end{array}

•

Regular:
Let
\displaystyle K
be compact. Since
\displaystyle X
is locally compact, whenever
\displaystyle K\ll K^{\prime}
, there exists
\displaystyle K^{\prime\prime}
compact such that
\displaystyle K\ll K^{\prime\prime}\ll K^{\prime}
. Therefore

\tilde{\lambda}(K)=\inf\{\lambda(K^{\prime})~|~K\ll K^{\prime}\}=\inf\{\tilde{\lambda}(K^{\prime\prime})~|~K\ll K^{\prime\prime}\}.

For the claim about the induced measure on
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X)
, let
\displaystyle U\subset X
be open. For any
\displaystyle K\subset U
compact there exists
\displaystyle K^{\prime}
compact with
\displaystyle K\ll K^{\prime}\subset U
by Lemma
9.1
hence

\tilde{\lambda}_{*}(U)=\sup_{K\subset U\text{ compact}}\tilde{\lambda}(K)=\sup_{K\subset U\text{ compact}}\inf_{K\ll K^{\prime}}\lambda(K^{\prime})=\sup_{K\subset U\text{ compact}}\lambda(K).

∎

9.1.
A representation theorem for measurable locales

Next we want to address a type of representation theorem: Every measurable locale arises from a regular content on a locally compact Hausdorff space.

Theorem 9.10
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a measurable locale. Then there exists a locally compact Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
together with a regular content
\displaystyle\lambda
such that
\displaystyle L\cong X^{\lambda}
.

Remark 9.11
.

Theorem
9.10
has a classical sister theorem in the context of operator algebras, that states that every commutative von Neumann algebra is obtained as
\displaystyle L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
for a Radon measure
\displaystyle\mu
on a locally compact Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
, see
[
Tak79
, V Theorem 1.18.]
. In fact, under the duality provided by Theorem
6.6
, these two theorems are equivalent.

Before we prove Theorem
9.10
, let us give a slight generalization of classical Stone-duality for profinite spaces.

Definition 9.12
.

A
Boolean ring
is a lower bounded distributive lattice
\displaystyle(D,\leq)
, such that for each
\displaystyle c,d\in D
there exists an element
\displaystyle d\setminus c
such that
\displaystyle c\wedge d\setminus c=0
and
\displaystyle c\vee d\setminus c=c\vee d
.

Lemma 9.13
.

Let
\displaystyle D
be a Boolean ring and let
\displaystyle L
be the locale associated to the frame
\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}(D)\cong\mathrm{Sh}(D,fin;\mathbf{2})
. Then
\displaystyle L
is spatial, corresponding to a locally compact Hausdorff space.

Remark 9.14
.

For a Boolean ring
\displaystyle D
, the posets
\displaystyle D_{/d}
always form Boolean algebras. Under Stone duality, this means that
\displaystyle X
is in fact locally profinite, i.e. has a cover by profinite spaces.

Proof.

The locale
\displaystyle L
is locally coherent, and thus spatial and locally compact, as described in
[
Leh25
, Section 3.1]
. To see that it is Hausdorff, note that by Example
3.21
points of
\displaystyle L
are given by prime filters of
\displaystyle D
. Let
\displaystyle x\neq y\subset D
be two such prime filters. Since both are non-empty filters, there must exist
\displaystyle d_{x}\in x
and
\displaystyle d_{y}\in y
. Then
\displaystyle d=d_{x}\vee d_{y}
is a common element in both
\displaystyle x
and
\displaystyle y
. Furthermore, since
\displaystyle x
and
\displaystyle y
are distinct filters, there needs to exist
\displaystyle c\in x,c\not\in y
. We have
\displaystyle c\vee d\setminus c=d\in y
, and also
\displaystyle c\not\in y
, it follows that since
\displaystyle y
is a prime filter, we have
\displaystyle d\setminus c\in y
. But viewed geometrically, this means that the representables
\displaystyle[c]
and
\displaystyle[d\setminus c]
in
\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}(D)
are two disjoint opens that separate
\displaystyle x
and
\displaystyle y
.
∎

Remark 9.15
.

In fact, the above proof shows that
\displaystyle X
is totally disconnected as well: Any two points can be separated via compact open subsets, which in the presence of Hausdorffness are closed and open.

Lemma 9.16
.

Let
\displaystyle D
be a Boolean ring and let
\displaystyle X
be the topological space associated to the frame
\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}(D)\cong\mathrm{Sh}(D,fin;\mathbf{2})
. Assume
\displaystyle K\subset X
is a compact subset. Then
\displaystyle K=\bigcap_{i\in I}U_{i}
for a collection of compact opens
\displaystyle U_{i}
.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle K\subset X
be a compact subset. Since
\displaystyle 1=\bigvee_{d\in D}[d]
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X)
is a directed union of compact opens, there exists a compact open
\displaystyle U
such that
\displaystyle K\subset U
. Furthermore, every compact set is also closed, since
\displaystyle X
is Hausdorff, therefore every open is obtained via unions from clopen sets. By passing to complements, we get that every closed set is obtained as an intersection of clopen sets, in particular
\displaystyle K
. By intersecting with
\displaystyle U
, we can assume this intersection consists of compact opens.
∎

Proof of Theorem
9.10
.

Choose a locally finite and faithful measure
\displaystyle\mu
on
\displaystyle L
and consider the Boolean ring
\displaystyle D=\mathcal{O}(L)^{\mu\text{-}fin}
of
\displaystyle\mu
-finite chunks of
\displaystyle L
. Define
\displaystyle X
to be the locally compact Hausdorff space associated to the frame
\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}(D)\cong\mathrm{Sh}(D,fin;\mathbf{2})
. Since
\displaystyle L
is given by the frame
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu\text{-}inn;\mathbf{2})
by Theorem
4.29
, and the
\displaystyle\mu
-inner topology contains all
\displaystyle fin
-coverings, we have an embedding

i:L\hookrightarrow X.

Then the pushforward along
\displaystyle i
equips
\displaystyle X
with a measure
\displaystyle i_{*}\mu
. It is immediate that this measure is locally finite, as
\displaystyle X
is generated by the set of compact opens (identified with)
\displaystyle D
, which have finite measure. By Theorem
9.4
the measure
\displaystyle\mu
induces a regular content
\displaystyle\lambda
on
\displaystyle X
.

We claim that
\displaystyle L\cong X^{\lambda}
, or equivalently that

\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu\text{-}inn;\mathbf{2})\cong\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{K}(X),\lambda\text{-}inn;\mathbf{2}).

To see this, it suffices to show that the image of
\displaystyle D\cong\mathcal{K}^{o}(X)
as the set of compact opens of
\displaystyle X
is a basis for the right-hand side, and that the induced Grothendieck pretopology agrees with the
\displaystyle\mu
-inner topology.

Every compact set
\displaystyle K
of
\displaystyle X
is obtained as a directed intersection of compact opens
\displaystyle U_{i},i\in I,
by Lemma
9.16
. But note that the right adjoint

i_{*}:\mathrm{Sh}(D,\mu\text{-}inn;\mathbf{2})\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}(D,fin;\mathbf{2})=\mathcal{O}(X)

preserves infima. Since elements of finite measure are closed under infima, and infima in
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X)
are computed as interiors of intersections, we get that the open interior

K^{o}=(\bigcap_{i\in I}U_{i})^{o}=\bigwedge_{i\in I}U_{i}

is again compact open. We also see that the inclusion

K^{o}\subset K

gives an equality in measure, i.e.
\displaystyle\lambda(K^{o})=\lambda(K)
, as the right hand side is calculated as
\displaystyle\inf_{i\in I}\mu(U_{i})
by definition, and the left hand side is calculated by the same term by Lemma
6.4
. Therefore
\displaystyle\{K^{o}\subset K\}
is in fact a
\displaystyle\lambda
-approximation and we see that all representables
\displaystyle[K]\in\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{K}(X),\lambda\text{-}inn;\mathbf{2})
can be covered by compact opens of
\displaystyle X
, in other words, the image of
\displaystyle D
is a basis for
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{K}(X),\lambda\text{-}inn;\mathbf{2})
. It is also clear that the restriction of the
\displaystyle\lambda
-inner pretopology on
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
to
\displaystyle D
agrees with the
\displaystyle\mu
-inner pretopology, as the functor
\displaystyle D\cong\mathcal{K}^{o}(X)\subset\mathcal{K}(X)
is valuation-preserving.
∎

10.
Examples

A theory is only as good as the examples it describes. As such, we will describe a few classical examples of measure spaces. The results here are well known—The main point we want to demonstrate is that the locale-theoretic picture is fairly easily constructed from well-known results in the literature.

10.1.
The Lebesgue reals

The most fundamental example of a measure is that of the Lebesgue measure
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}
on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Lebesgue measure is an example of a Radon measure, which means that restricting
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}
to the set
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of compact sets of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}
gives a regular content. Let us quickly sketch how one can construct this (regular) content without reference to the standard literature on measure theory and show some elementary properties.

Let
\displaystyle n\geq 0
. Define a
dyadic cube of thinness
\displaystyle n
to be a set of the form

\prod_{i=1}^{d}\left[\frac{a_{i}}{2^{n}},\frac{a_{i}+1}{2^{n}}\right]\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}

for values
\displaystyle a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Call a set
\displaystyle D
a
standard set of thinness
\displaystyle n
if it is given as a finite union of dyadic cubes of thinness
\displaystyle n
. Define Lebesgue measure
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}
on the set of standard sets of thinness
\displaystyle n
as

\lambda_{d}(D)=\frac{\#\{\text{distinct dyadic boxes of thinness }n\text{ contained in }D\}}{2^{nd}}.

Approximating a compact set
\displaystyle K
from above via dyadic boxes

Any compact set
\displaystyle K\in\mathcal{K}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is obtained as

K=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}D_{n}

where
\displaystyle D_{n}
is the smallest finite union of dyadic boxes of thinness
\displaystyle n
containing
\displaystyle K
. Define the Lebesgue measure on
\displaystyle K
as

\mu(K)=\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{d}(D_{n}).

It is elementary to check that
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}
is a regular content on
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

With this defined, we get a corresponding measurable locale
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}_{Leb}=\mathbb{R}^{\lambda_{d}}
together with an induced measure
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}
on the locale of sublocales
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Sl}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and in particular on all open subsets of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{\lambda_{d}}
. The locale
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}_{Leb}
has no points for
\displaystyle d>0
, as one sees by an application of Proposition
8.25
. One can also observe that any open
\displaystyle U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
contains a dyadic cube and therefore satisfies
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}(U)>0
, hence the canonical map
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}_{Leb}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
is dense by Proposition
8.27
. The relation to the classical Lebesgue measure is that the frame corresponding to
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}_{Leb}
is in fact isomorphic to the (complete) Boolean algebra obtained by taking the quotient of the
\displaystyle\sigma
-algebra of Lebesgue measurable subsets of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}
modulo the ideal of
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}
-null sets, a fact that we will more generally prove with Theorem
12.11
in the last part of this article.

It is a geometric argument to see that
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}
is translation invariant, i.e. if
\displaystyle v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}(S+v)=\lambda_{d}(S)
for any sublocale
\displaystyle S
. Since the measurable locale
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}_{Leb}
is obtained as the result of applying a functor to the valuation site
\displaystyle(\mathcal{K}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\lambda_{d})
it suffices to show invariance of
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}
when acting on compact subsets, which in turn reduces to the same statement for standard sets. If
\displaystyle v
is a dyadic vector, i.e. of the form
\displaystyle\left(\frac{a_{i}}{2^{n}}\right)_{i=1}^{d}
for some
\displaystyle n\geq 0
and
\displaystyle a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}
, the statement is clear. The case of a general translation follows from the density of dyadic numbers in
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}
.

One can also show that
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}
is rotation invariant. Here one observes more generally that
\displaystyle\lambda_{d}
is invariant under the action of the special linear group
\displaystyle\mathrm{SL}_{d}(\mathbb{R})
, which contains the group of rotations. The group
\displaystyle\mathrm{SL}_{d}(\mathbb{R})
is generated by
elementary matrices

\displaystyle E_{ij}(a)=I+ae_{ij}
with
\displaystyle a\in\mathbb{R}
and
\displaystyle i\neq j
, where
\displaystyle I
is the identity matrix, and
\displaystyle e_{ij}
the matrix with single non-zero entry
\displaystyle 1
at position
\displaystyle(i,j)
, hence it suffices to verify that such elementary matrices act in a measure-preserving way. Again, it suffices to show this for dyadic boxes, for which it is an elementary geometric argument, as elementary matrices act via shearing transformations.

Let
\displaystyle m\geq n
. Denote by
\displaystyle p:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
the projection onto the first
\displaystyle n
factors. Consider the restriction of
\displaystyle p|_{[0,1]^{m}}
to the
\displaystyle m
-dimensional cube
\displaystyle[0,1]^{m}
as a map
\displaystyle[0,1]^{m}\rightarrow[0,1]^{n}
. Since
\displaystyle{p|_{[0,1]^{m}}}^{-1}(K)=K\times[0,1]^{m-n}
, one observes that this map is measure-preserving. We obtain the classical corollary.

Lemma 10.1
.

Suppose
\displaystyle C\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a compact null set and
\displaystyle K\subset p^{-1}(C)\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is compact. Then
\displaystyle K
is again a compact null set.
12
12

12

This lemma of course also follows from the classical Fubini-Tonelli theorem.

Proof.

By covering
\displaystyle K
with a finite (!) set of cubes, we may as well assume
\displaystyle K\subset[0,1]^{m}
and
\displaystyle C\subset[0,1]^{n}
. But then the standard projection
\displaystyle p:[0,1]^{m}\rightarrow[0,1]^{n}
is measure-preserving, therefore
\displaystyle\lambda_{m}(K)\leq\lambda_{m}(p^{-1}(C))=\lambda_{n}(C)=0.

∎

10.2.
The Haar measure on a locally compact Hausdorff group

In the following, let
\displaystyle G
be a locally compact Hausdorff group. We sketch the classical construction of the Haar measure on
\displaystyle G
.

Definition 10.2
.

A (left)
Haar measure
on
\displaystyle G
is a non-trivial, left invariant and locally finite measure
\displaystyle\mu
on
\displaystyle G
.

Concretely, we say that a measure
\displaystyle\mu:\mathcal{O}(G)\rightarrow[0,\infty]
is
left invariant
if the homeomorphisms
\displaystyle g\cdot-:G\rightarrow G
are measure-preserving for every
\displaystyle g\in G
.

A Haar measure automatically satisfies that
\displaystyle\mu(U)>0
for all non-empty opens
\displaystyle U
. W.l.o.g. assume that the neutral element
\displaystyle e\in U
, and let
\displaystyle K\subset G
be a compact set. Then

K\subset\bigcup_{k\in K}kU,

hence by compactness of
\displaystyle K
a finite set of translations of
\displaystyle U
covers
\displaystyle K
. Therefore, if there exists a single non-empty open set
\displaystyle U
with
\displaystyle\mu(U)=0
, the induced regular content on all compact sets must be trivial, and hence
\displaystyle\mu
itself is trivial as well. Let us state the classical existence-and-uniqueness theorem of the Haar measure.

Theorem 10.3
.

Let
\displaystyle G
be a locally compact Hausdorff group. Then there exists a Haar measure
\displaystyle\mu
.

Sketch of proof.

Since
\displaystyle G
is assumed locally compact, the construction of a left-invariant locally finite measure on
\displaystyle G
is equivalent to the the construction of a left-invariant regular content by Theorem
9.4
. It furthermore suffices to give a left-invariant (not-necessarily regular) content
\displaystyle\lambda
by Lemma
9.8
. We sketch the standard argument given in e.g. Halmos
[
Hal13
, Chapter XI]
for the construction of such
\displaystyle\lambda
.
Given a compact set
\displaystyle U
with non-empty interior, and a compact set
\displaystyle K
, define the
ratio

(K:U)=\min\left\{\#I\mid I\subset G,\text{ s.t. }K\subset\bigcup_{g\in I}gU\right\}.

This number is finite: By non-emptiness of
\displaystyle U
there exists a cover of
\displaystyle K
by sets of the form
\displaystyle gU
for
\displaystyle g\in G
, and since
\displaystyle K
is compact, a finite set of such elements
\displaystyle g
suffices to cover
\displaystyle K
.

Now fix a compact set
\displaystyle A\subset G
with non-empty interior (such a set exists since
\displaystyle G
is assumed to be locally compact Hausdorff). We think of
\displaystyle A
as being a
reference scale
. Then define for each compact
\displaystyle U
with non-empty interior the function

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\lambda_{U}:\mathcal{K}(G)&\rightarrow&[0,\infty)\\
K&\mapsto&\frac{(K:U)}{(A:U)}.\end{array}

One verifies that this is monotone, subadditive and left invariant, and satisfies additivity for a restricted class of compacts, see
[
Hal13
,
\nopp
§58 Theorem A]
. If
\displaystyle K_{1}
,
\displaystyle K_{2}
are compact sets such that
\displaystyle K_{1}U^{-1}\cap K_{2}U^{-1}=\emptyset,
then

\lambda_{U}(K_{1}\cup K_{2})=\lambda_{U}(K_{1})+\lambda_{U}(K_{2}).

Furthermore it is easy to verify that
\displaystyle 0\leq\lambda_{U}(K)\leq(K:A)
.
In order to obtain an actual content
\displaystyle\lambda
, one needs to take a suitable limit over the net indexed by the neighborhood filter
\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{c}(e)
of compact neighborhoods of the identity element
\displaystyle e\in G
, that sends
\displaystyle U
to the function
\displaystyle\lambda_{U}
. This may not exist uniquely, but to show the existence of a candidate for
\displaystyle\lambda
one argues that this net lives in the compact Hausdorff space
\displaystyle\prod_{K\in\mathcal{K}(G)}[0,(K:A)]
(by Tychonoff’s theorem), and then chooses a convergent sub-net. Finally, one verifies that the corresponding limit
\displaystyle\lambda
actually fulfills the conditions for being a left-invariant content
[
Hal13
,
\nopp
§58 Theorem B]
.
∎

Remark 10.4
.

The use of choice for the construction of
\displaystyle\lambda
, which then has to further be modified to define the actual left invariant regular content

\tilde{\lambda}(K)=\inf_{K\ll K^{\prime}}\lambda(K^{\prime})

is somewhat unsatisfactory, as the actual value for a given compact set
\displaystyle K
is quite inexplicit. Another argument for the construction of
\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}(K)
via the construction of a left-invariant mean on the space
\displaystyle C_{c}(G)
of compactly supported real-valued functions on
\displaystyle G
is due to Alfsen
[
Alf63
]
. It would be preferable to have an elementary argument that gives the value of
\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}(K)
directly.

Example 10.5
.

Suppose
\displaystyle G
is a locally compact Hausdorff group and
\displaystyle K
a compact subgroup. Then the quotient map
\displaystyle G\rightarrow G/K
is a proper map, and therefore Proposition
4.8
equips
\displaystyle G/K
with a locally finite measure
\displaystyle\mu
via pushforward of a choice of Haar measure on
\displaystyle G
. This measure can be shown to be
\displaystyle G
-invariant
[
Fol16
, Corollary 2.53]
.

We obtain many classical examples together with their measures as a corollary: Hyperbolic
\displaystyle n
-space
\displaystyle H^{n}=\mathrm{SO}^{+}(n,1)/\mathrm{SO}(n)
, and the
\displaystyle n
-sphere
\displaystyle S^{n}=\mathrm{O}(n)/\mathrm{O}(n-1)
to mention just two.

11.
Measurable locales generated from Hausdorff spaces

It can sometimes be useful to have a way to generate measurable locales without the need to specify one particular measure. We will discuss this in the following. Recall the notion of a compactly marked Hausdorff space
\displaystyle(X,N)
(Definition
7.13
), given by a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
and an ideal
\displaystyle N\subset\mathcal{K}(X)
of compact subsets. If
\displaystyle U\subset X
is an open subset, denote by
\displaystyle N|_{U}\subset\mathcal{K}(U)
the ideal of compacts
\displaystyle K\in N
such that
\displaystyle K\subset U
.

Definition 11.1
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff space. An ideal
\displaystyle N\subset\mathcal{K}(X)
of compact sets is called
measurable
if there exists a cover
\displaystyle U_{i},i\in I
of
\displaystyle X
such that for each
\displaystyle i\in I
there exists a Radon valuation
\displaystyle\mu_{i}
on
\displaystyle U_{i}
such that
\displaystyle N|_{U_{i}}
agrees with the set of compact
\displaystyle\mu_{i}
-null sets. We will refer to compacts
\displaystyle K\in N
as
null sets
.

The category
\displaystyle\mathrm{MMHS}
of measurably marked Hausdorff spaces is defined as the corresponding full subcategory of compactly marked Hausdorff spaces
\displaystyle\mathrm{CMHS}
.

Observe that for any
\displaystyle K\in N
, since
\displaystyle K
is compact, there exist finitely many
\displaystyle i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\in I
such that
\displaystyle K\subset U_{i_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup U_{i_{n}}
. Using Lemma
7.5
, we see that
\displaystyle K
decomposes as

K=K_{i_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup K_{i_{n}}

with
\displaystyle K_{i_{j}}\in N|_{U_{i_{j}}}.
In other words,
\displaystyle N
is generated under finite unions from the ideals
\displaystyle N|_{U_{i}},i\in I,
or equivalently
\displaystyle N=\bigvee_{i\in I}N|_{U_{i}}
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(X^{\mathcal{K}})
. We think of this as saying that an abstract compact null set is equivalently given as a finite union of local pieces that are actual null sets.

Remark 11.2
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,\mu),(Y,\nu)
be two Hausdorff spaces equipped with Radon valuations, viewed as measurably marked in the canonical way. Denote by
\displaystyle N
the set of compact
\displaystyle\mu
-null sets and by
\displaystyle M
the set of compact
\displaystyle\nu
-null sets. Recall that a continuous map
\displaystyle f:X\rightarrow Y
is called compatible if:

(1)

For all
\displaystyle K\in N
we have
\displaystyle f(K)\in M
.

(2)

For all
\displaystyle C\in M
we have
\displaystyle\{K\subset f^{-1}(C)\text{ compact}\}\subset N
.

If
\displaystyle f
is measure-preserving, then condition (2) is automatically satisfied. Hence a measure-preserving map is compatible if images of compact null sets remain compact null sets.

The requirement (1) is not automatically satisfied for a measure-preserving map. Let
\displaystyle(X,\delta_{x})
be an arbitrary space equipped with the Dirac measure at a point
\displaystyle x\in X
. Then the unique map
\displaystyle f:(X,\delta_{x})\rightarrow(\mathrm{pt},\delta_{\mathrm{pt}})
is measure-preserving. However, if
\displaystyle X
contains another point
\displaystyle y
, it does not satisfy the condition that null sets are preserved, since

0=\delta_{x}(\{y\})\neq\delta_{\mathrm{pt}}(f(\{y\}))=\delta_{\mathrm{pt}}(\mathrm{pt})=1.

Recall the functor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}b:\mathrm{CMHS}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{BoolLoc}\\
(X,N)&\mapsto&b(X,N)\end{array}

provided by Corollary
7.14
.

Proposition 11.3
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,N)
be a measurably marked Hausdorff space. Then
\displaystyle b(X,N)=(N^{c})_{\neg\neg}\hookrightarrow X^{\mathcal{K}}
is a measurable locale.

Proof.

Choose a covering
\displaystyle U_{i},i\in I,
of
\displaystyle X
together with Radon valuations
\displaystyle\mu_{i}
on
\displaystyle U_{i}
such that
\displaystyle N|_{U_{i}}
agrees with the ideal of compact
\displaystyle\mu_{i}
-null sets. Pulling back this covering along the map
\displaystyle p:b(N)\hookrightarrow X^{\mathcal{K}}\rightarrow X
gives a covering
\displaystyle p^{*}(U_{i}),i\in I,
of
\displaystyle b(N)
. We can identify the individual opens as
\displaystyle p^{*}(U_{i})=b(N|_{U_{i}})
: For each
\displaystyle i\in I
, we have the composition of pullback squares

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b(N|_{U_{i}})}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b(N)}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}^{\mathcal{K}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathcal{K}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{i}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

\displaystyle\lrcorner

\displaystyle\lrcorner

with the top square given by Lemma
5.17
and the bottom square given by Remark
7.7
. The locale
\displaystyle b(N|_{U_{i}})
agrees with the inner locale
\displaystyle{U_{i}}^{\mu_{i}}
by Theorem
6.18
, and is thus equipped with the faithful and locally finite measure
\displaystyle{\mu_{i}}_{*}
, hence
\displaystyle b(N)
can be covered by opens equipped with faithful and finite measures.
∎

The following theorem is now immediate from Corollary
7.14
and Proposition
11.3
.

Theorem 11.4
.

The functor
\displaystyle b
restricts to a well-defined functor

b:\mathrm{MMHS}\rightarrow\mathrm{MblLoc}.

11.1.
The measurable locale associated to a smooth manifold

Theorem
11.4
has an immediate application in the construction of a functor on smooth manifolds, that associates to a smooth manifold
\displaystyle M
the measurable
Lebesgue
locale
\displaystyle M_{Leb}
together with a natural map

M_{Leb}\rightarrow M.

Recall that an
\displaystyle n
-dimensional
chart

\displaystyle(U,\varphi)
of a topological space
\displaystyle M
consists of an open subset
\displaystyle U\subset M
, together with a homeomorphism
\displaystyle\varphi:U\rightarrow\varphi(U)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
\displaystyle\varphi(U)
is an open subset of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
. A Hausdorff space
\displaystyle M
is called a
large smooth n-manifold
if there exists a collection of charts
\displaystyle(U_{i},\varphi_{i}),i\in I,
such that
\displaystyle\{U_{i}\subset M~|~i\in I\}
is a covering of
\displaystyle M
, and all transition functions
\displaystyle\varphi_{j}{\varphi_{i}}^{-1}:\varphi_{i}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})\rightarrow\varphi_{j}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})
are smooth maps. (Compare, e.g.,
[
Lee03
]
Chapter 1)

Note that any large smooth manifold is a locally compact Hausdorff space. We call a large smooth manifold
\displaystyle M
of
standard size
or simply a
smooth manifold
if it is second-countable.

Definition 11.5
.

Let
\displaystyle M
be a (large) smooth manifold of dimension
\displaystyle n
. A compact subset
\displaystyle K\subset M
is called
null
if there exists a finite collection of charts
\displaystyle(U_{i},\varphi_{i}),i\in I,
such that
\displaystyle K
can be written as
\displaystyle K=\bigcup K_{i}
with
\displaystyle K_{i}\subset U_{i}
a compact subset, and
\displaystyle\varphi_{i}(K_{i})\subset\varphi_{i}(U_{i})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
being a null set for the Lebesgue measure on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Note that any compact set
\displaystyle K\subset M
is always contained in a finite collection of charts. For this reason, for many proofs we may assume w.l.o.g. that
\displaystyle K
is actually a compact subset of a smooth manifold of standard size. The main reason to require smoothness for our manifolds is the following crucial lemma.
13
13

13

Strictly speaking, the use of
\displaystyle C^{1}
-manifolds would suffice.

Lemma 11.6

(
[
Lee03
]
Theorem 6.9)
.

Let
\displaystyle f:M\rightarrow N
be a smooth map between two smooth manifolds
\displaystyle M,N
. Then
\displaystyle f
maps compact null sets to null sets.

Lemma 11.7
.

Let
\displaystyle K\subset M
be a compact subset of a large smooth
\displaystyle n
-manifold
\displaystyle M
. The following are equivalent.

(1)

\displaystyle K
is a compact null set.

(2)

For any covering of
\displaystyle M
by charts
\displaystyle(U_{i},\varphi_{i}),i\in I,
there exist a
finite
subset
\displaystyle F\subset I
and compact subsets
\displaystyle K_{i}\subset U_{i}
for each
\displaystyle i\in F
, such that
\displaystyle K=\bigcup_{i\in F}K_{i}
and
\displaystyle\varphi_{i}(K_{i})\subset\varphi_{i}(U_{i})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a null set for the Lebesgue measure on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
for each
\displaystyle i\in F
.

Proof.

The implication from (2) to (1) is immediate. Now assume that
\displaystyle K
is a compact null set, i.e. there exists a finite collection of charts
\displaystyle(V_{j},\psi_{j}),j\in J,
and compact sets
\displaystyle K_{j}\subset V_{j}
, such that
\displaystyle K=\bigcup_{j\in J}K_{j}
and
\displaystyle\psi_{j}(K_{j})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a Lebesgue null set for each
\displaystyle j\in J
. Let
\displaystyle(U_{i},\varphi_{i}),i\in I,
be a covering of
\displaystyle M
by charts. Since
\displaystyle K
is compact and
\displaystyle M
is Hausdorff, there exists a finite subset
\displaystyle F\subset I
and
\displaystyle K_{i}\subset U_{i}
compact subsets for
\displaystyle i\in F
such that
\displaystyle K=\bigcup_{i\in F}K_{i}
.

We need to argue that
\displaystyle\varphi_{i}(K_{i})\subset\varphi_{i}(U_{i})
is a Lebesgue null set. We can write
\displaystyle K_{i}=\bigcup_{j\in J}K_{i}\cap K_{j}
with
\displaystyle K_{i}\cap K_{j}\subset U_{i}\cap V_{j}
for each
\displaystyle j\in J
. Now since
\displaystyle\psi_{j}(K_{j})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a Lebesgue null set, the same holds for
\displaystyle\psi_{j}(K_{i}\cap K_{j})
. Since the transition function
\displaystyle\varphi_{i}\psi_{j}^{-1}
is a smooth function, by
11.6
the same holds for the compact set
\displaystyle\varphi_{i}(K_{i}\cap K_{j})
. But this means that
\displaystyle\varphi_{i}(K_{i})=\bigcup_{j\in J}\varphi_{i}(K_{i}\cap K_{j})
is a finite union of compact Lebesgue null sets, and hence itself a Lebesgue null set.
∎

Note that in particular, if a compact set
\displaystyle K
is contained entirely in a chart
\displaystyle(U,\varphi)
, it is null iff its image in
\displaystyle\varphi(U)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a Lebesgue null set. It is also straightforward to see that finite unions of compact null sets remain null sets. Hence the set of compact null sets forms an ideal
\displaystyle N
.

Definition 11.8
.

Let
\displaystyle M
be a large smooth manifold. Let
\displaystyle N\in\mathcal{O}(M^{\mathcal{K}})
be the ideal of compact null sets. Then
the
Lebesgue locale
associated to
\displaystyle M
is defined as

M_{Leb}=b(N)\hookrightarrow M^{\mathcal{K}}.

It comes with a canonical map
\displaystyle\mathrm{can}:M_{Leb}\hookrightarrow M^{\mathcal{K}}\xrightarrow{\theta}M.

Proposition 11.9
.

Let
\displaystyle M
be a large smooth manifold of dimension
\displaystyle n
and let
\displaystyle N
be the ideal of compact null sets of
\displaystyle M
. Then
\displaystyle N
is a measurable ideal. In particular,
\displaystyle M_{Leb}
is a measurable locale.

Proof.

Choose a covering of
\displaystyle M
by charts
\displaystyle(U_{i},\varphi_{i}),i\in I
. Then
\displaystyle\varphi_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow\varphi_{i}(U_{i})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a homeomorphism such that
\displaystyle N|_{U_{i}}
is mapped to the set of compact Lebesgue null sets contained in
\displaystyle\varphi_{i}(U_{i})
, which, as an open subset of
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
, is a Hausdorff space equipped with the Radon valuation given by Lebesgue measure. The statement that
\displaystyle M_{Leb}
is a measurable locale is given by Proposition
11.3
.
∎

Lemma 11.10
.

The canonical map
\displaystyle\mathrm{can}:M_{Leb}\rightarrow M
is dense.

Proof.

We need to show that
\displaystyle\mathrm{can}_{*}(0)=\emptyset
for the composite

\mathrm{can}:b(N)\xrightarrow{i}M^{\mathcal{K}}\xrightarrow{\theta}M.

By definition
\displaystyle i_{*}(0)=N
. Analogous to the proof given for Proposition
8.27
we have

\theta_{*}(N)=\bigvee_{U~:~\theta^{*}(U)\leq N}U=\bigvee\{U~|~\text{ for all }K\subset U\text{ compact }K\text{ is null}\}.

Now let
\displaystyle U
be a non-empty open subset of
\displaystyle M
. By choosing a non-empty chart
\displaystyle(V,\varphi)
contained in
\displaystyle U
, we can find a compact set
\displaystyle K\subset V\subset U
such that the Lebesgue measure of
\displaystyle\varphi(K)\subset\varphi(V)
is not zero. Therefore
\displaystyle U
is not contained in
\displaystyle\theta_{*}(N)
, in other words, we must have
\displaystyle\mathrm{can}_{*}(0)=\theta_{*}(N)=\emptyset
.
∎

We now address the question of functoriality of the assignment
\displaystyle M\mapsto M_{Leb}
. Recall that a
submersion

\displaystyle f:M\rightarrow N
is a smooth map between (potentially large) smooth manifolds such that the differential
\displaystyle df_{p}
has full rank at each point
\displaystyle p\in M
. (Compare
[
Lee03
, Chapter 4]
.) We require the following standard result about submersions, namely that they are locally projections.

Theorem 11.11

(
[
Lee03
]
Theorem 4.12)
.

Suppose
\displaystyle M
and
\displaystyle N
are two smooth manifolds of dimension
\displaystyle m
and
\displaystyle n
, respectively, with
\displaystyle m\geq n
, and
\displaystyle f:M\rightarrow N
a smooth submersion. Then for each
\displaystyle p\in M
there exists smooth charts
\displaystyle(U,\varphi)
for
\displaystyle M
containing
\displaystyle p
and
\displaystyle(V,\psi)
for
\displaystyle N
containing
\displaystyle f(p)
such that
\displaystyle f(U)\subset V
and
\displaystyle\psi\circ f\circ\varphi^{-1}:\varphi(U)\rightarrow\psi(V)
agrees with the projection
\displaystyle\pi:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
onto the first
\displaystyle n
factors.

The following theorem was sketched by Dmitri Pavlov in a MathOverflow answer
[
httc
]
, but, to the best of the author’s knowledge, it has not yet appeared in the literature.

Theorem 11.12
.

There exists a functor

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(-)_{Leb}:\mathrm{Man}_{\mathrm{subm}}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{MblLoc}\\
M&\mapsto&M_{Leb}\end{array}

where
\displaystyle\mathrm{Man}_{\mathrm{subm}}
is the category of large smooth manifolds and smooth submersions as maps between them.

Proof.

The proof of Theorem
11.12
reduces entirely to Theorem
11.4
. The only crucial point is to verify that a given smooth submersion
\displaystyle f:M\rightarrow N
is compatible with the markings given by compact null sets. To do so, we need to verify two statements.

•

The image of a compact null set under
\displaystyle f
remains a compact null set. This only needs smoothness and is provided by Lemma
11.6
.

•

Let
\displaystyle C\subset N
be a compact null set, and
\displaystyle K\subset f^{-1}(C)\subset N
a compact set. We need to argue that
\displaystyle K
is a compact null set. Using compactness of
\displaystyle K
, cover
\displaystyle K
by finitely many charts
\displaystyle(U_{i},\varphi_{i}),i\in I,
together with charts
\displaystyle(V_{i},\psi_{i}),i\in I,
such that
\displaystyle f
maps
\displaystyle U_{i}
into
\displaystyle V_{i}
and such that
\displaystyle\psi_{i}\circ f\circ\varphi_{i}^{-1}
agrees with the standard projection
\displaystyle\pi:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
onto the first
\displaystyle n
factors. Choose compact sets
\displaystyle K_{i}\subset U_{i}
such that
\displaystyle K=\bigcup_{i\in I}K_{i}
. Then
\displaystyle f(K_{i})\subset V_{i}
is a compact null set, since it is contained in the null set
\displaystyle C
, and hence
\displaystyle\psi_{i}(f(K_{i}))\subset\psi_{i}(V_{i})
is a Lebesgue null set. We can now use Lemma
10.1
to conclude that
\displaystyle\varphi_{i}(K_{i})\subset\pi^{-1}(\psi_{i}(f(K_{i})))
is a Lebesgue null set, in other words
\displaystyle K_{i}
are null sets for all
\displaystyle i\in I
and so is
\displaystyle K
.

∎

Remark 11.13
.

The usual condition of second countability that is typically required in standard textbooks on differential geometry is not necessary for Theorem
11.12
