. This means any large smooth manifold
\displaystyle M
has an associated measurable locale
\displaystyle M_{Leb}
and under Gelfand duality
6.6
a corresponding commutative von Neumann algebra
\displaystyle L^{\infty}(M)
, and with it all the standard theorems one usually requires for integration. The set
\displaystyle C_{b}(M)
of bounded continuous functions naturally embeds into
\displaystyle L^{\infty}(M)
by Lemma
11.10
. This opens the potential for a working integration theory even on non-second countable manifolds. Of course, second countability will appear naturally, e.g. any choice of a finite measure on
\displaystyle M
has second countable support.

Remark 11.14
.

In the interpretation of Boolean locales as generalized discrete spaces, we can think of the natural map
\displaystyle M_{Leb}\rightarrow M
as equipping
\displaystyle M
with a
different underlying space
. This underlying space necessarily has no points for
\displaystyle n
-dimensional manifolds with
\displaystyle n>0
, but may be more fundamental for physical considerations than the ordinary set of points of
\displaystyle M
. This idea was already present in the writing of von Neumann
[
NH98
, 254]
, who suggests that
\displaystyle M_{Leb}
“may be considered as the basic system for the logical treatment of classical mechanics”.

Remark 11.15
.

The statements made in this section could be made more generally for
\displaystyle C^{1}
-manifolds and continuously differentiable maps.

12.
Comparison with classical measure spaces

In this section, we aim to connect our notion of valuation sites with the classical notion of a measure space. The central example is the following: If

\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{L},\mu)
is a measure space, define

\mathcal{L}^{fin}=\{E\in\mathcal{L}~|~\mu(E)<\infty\}.

It is clear that
\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}^{fin},\mu)
defines a valuation site. We claim that under suitable conditions, this site determines the quotient algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}/\mathcal{N}
where
\displaystyle\mathcal{N}
is the ideal of null sets. This condition is
localizability
, as introduced by Segal
[
Seg51
]
.

12.1.
Localizable measure spaces

Definition 12.1
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{L},\mu)
be a measure space. Let
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{L}
be a collection of measurable sets. A measurable set
\displaystyle E
is called
essential supremum
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
if

•

For all
\displaystyle E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}
, we have
\displaystyle\mu(E^{\prime}\setminus E)=0
.

•

Whenever
\displaystyle F
is a measurable set such that
\displaystyle\mu(E^{\prime}\setminus F)=0
for all
\displaystyle E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}
, then also
\displaystyle\mu(E\setminus F)=0
.

Remark 12.2
.

We see more or less by definition that a set
\displaystyle E
is an essential supremum of
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
iff the class
\displaystyle[E]
is the supremum of the set of classes
\displaystyle[E^{\prime}],E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}
in the quotient algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}/\mathcal{N}
, where
\displaystyle\mathcal{N}
is the ideal of null sets. In particular, essential suprema are unique up to null sets.

Lemma 12.3

(See also
[
Pav22
]
, Proposition 4.41.)
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{L},\mu)
be a measure space and suppose
\displaystyle E
is the essential supremum of a directed set
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
. Then

\mu(E)=\sup_{E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}\mu(E^{\prime}).

Proof.

We can assume w.l.o.g. that
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
is closed under finite unions. First note that we have for
\displaystyle E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}
by definition
\displaystyle\mu(E^{\prime}\setminus E)=0
, therefore
\displaystyle\mu(E)\geq\mu(E^{\prime})
, hence the lemma is trivially true if
\displaystyle\sup_{E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}\mu(E^{\prime})=\infty.
Therefore assume
\displaystyle\sup_{E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}\mu(E^{\prime})<\infty
for the rest of the proof.

Inductively find a sequence of elements
\displaystyle E_{n}\in\mathcal{E}
such that
\displaystyle E_{n}\subset E_{n+1}
and

\mu(E_{n})+\frac{1}{n}\geq\sup_{E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}\mu(E^{\prime}).

Take the countable (!) union
\displaystyle F=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}E_{n}
. It is clear that
\displaystyle\mu(F)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mu(E_{n})=\sup_{E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}\mu(E^{\prime})<\infty
.

Now take
\displaystyle G\in\mathcal{E}
. We know that
\displaystyle\mu(G\cup E_{n})\leq\sup_{E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}\mu(E^{\prime})
for all
\displaystyle n\in\mathbb{N}
, since
\displaystyle G
and
\displaystyle E_{n}
are in
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
and we assumed
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
to be closed under finite unions. Therefore

\mu(G\cup E_{n})=\mu(G\setminus E_{n})+\mu(E_{n})\xrightarrow{n\rightarrow\infty}\mu(G\setminus F)+\sup_{E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}\mu(E^{\prime})\leq\sup_{E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}\mu(E^{\prime}).

Hence we obtain that
\displaystyle\mu(G\setminus F)=0
. Since
\displaystyle E
is an essential supremum of
\displaystyle\mathcal{E}
, we conclude that
\displaystyle\mu(E\setminus F)=0
. Furthermore, we have

\mu(F\setminus E)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mu(E_{n}\setminus E)=0.

But this means

\mu(E)=\mu(E\cap F)+\mu(E\setminus F)=\mu(E\cap F)=\mu(E\cap F)+\mu(F\setminus E)=\mu(F).

This concludes the proof.
∎

Definition 12.4

(
[
Fre00a
]
, Definition 211G)
.

A measure space
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{L},\mu)
is called
semi-finite
if for every
\displaystyle E\in\mathcal{L}
such that
\displaystyle\mu(E)=\infty
, there exists
\displaystyle F\subset E
such that
\displaystyle F\in\mathcal{L}
and
\displaystyle 0<\mu(F)<\infty
. A semi-finite measure space
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{L},\mu)
is called
localizable
if arbitrary essential suprema exist.

Remark 12.5
.

In particular, for a localizable measure space
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{L},\mu)
, the Boolean algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}/\mathcal{N}
is complete; in other words, a Boolean frame.

Lemma 12.6
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{L},\mu)
be a localizable measure space. Then every
\displaystyle E
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}
is the essential supremum of the set
\displaystyle\{E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}~|~E^{\prime}\subset E,~\mu(E^{\prime})<\infty\}
.

Proof.

Fix
\displaystyle E\in\mathcal{L}
and suppose
\displaystyle F\in\mathcal{L}
is such that
\displaystyle\mu(E^{\prime}\setminus F)=0
for all measurable
\displaystyle E^{\prime}\subset E
such that
\displaystyle\mu(E^{\prime})<\infty
. We consider two cases:

•

If
\displaystyle\mu(E\setminus F)<\infty
, then
\displaystyle E^{\prime}=E\setminus F\subset E
satisfies
\displaystyle\mu(E^{\prime})<\infty
, but by assumption
\displaystyle F
is such that

0=\mu(E^{\prime}\setminus F)=\mu(E^{\prime})=\mu(E\setminus F).

•

If
\displaystyle\mu(E\setminus F)=\infty
, by semi-finiteness, we can choose
\displaystyle E^{\prime}\subset E\setminus F\subset E
such that
\displaystyle 0<\mu(E^{\prime})<\infty
. But then by assumption on
\displaystyle F
we have that

0=\mu(E^{\prime}\setminus F)=\mu(E^{\prime}),

a contradiction.

Therefore we conclude
\displaystyle\mu(E\setminus F)=0
as the only possibility.
∎

Theorem 12.7
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{L},\mu)
be a localizable measure space. Then we have a measure-preserving frame isomorphism

(\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{L}^{fin},\mu;\mathbf{2}),\mu_{*})\cong(\mathcal{L}/\mathcal{N},\mu)

induced by the natural homomorphism
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{fin}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}/\mathcal{N}
.

Proof.

It is clear from Lemma
12.6
that the image of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{fin}
in
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}/\mathcal{N}
gives a basis, hence by the Basis Theorem
3.12
, we know that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}/\mathcal{N}
is obtained from the induced Grothendieck topology on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{fin}/\mathcal{N}
, which furthermore induces a Grothendieck topology on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{fin}
. We only need to check that this Grothendieck topology agrees with the
\displaystyle\mu
-inner topology on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{fin}
. This is equivalent to saying that for
\displaystyle E\in\mathcal{L}
with
\displaystyle\mu(E)<\infty
, we have that a set
\displaystyle\{E_{i}\subset E~|~i\in I\}
is a
\displaystyle\mu
-cover iff
\displaystyle E
is the essential supremum of
\displaystyle\{E_{i}~|~i\in I\}
.

First assume that
\displaystyle\{E_{i}\subset E~|~i\in I\}
is a
\displaystyle\mu
-cover. This means, for any
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
there exists
\displaystyle E_{i_{1}},\ldots E_{i_{n}}
such that

\mu(E)-\mu(E_{i_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup E_{i_{n}})<\epsilon.

By definition
\displaystyle\mu(E_{i}\setminus E)=\mu(\emptyset)=0
for all
\displaystyle i\in I
. Now assume
\displaystyle F
is any measurable set such that
\displaystyle\mu(E_{i}\setminus F)=0
for all
\displaystyle i\in I
. Take
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
and choose
\displaystyle E_{i_{1}},\ldots E_{i_{n}}
as above. Then

\mu(E\setminus F)=\mu(E\setminus F)-\mu((E_{i_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup E_{i_{n}})\setminus F)\leq\mu(E)-\mu(E_{i_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup E_{i_{n}})<\epsilon.

Since this holds for any
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
, we conclude that
\displaystyle\mu(E\setminus F)=0
.

Conversely, assume that
\displaystyle E
is the essential supremum of
\displaystyle\{E_{i}~|~i\in I\}
. Since essential suprema of a finite collection of measurable sets is always realized by finite union, we are left to argue the case where the set
\displaystyle\{E_{i}~|~i\in I\}
is directed. In this case, Lemma
12.3
shows that
\displaystyle\{E_{i}\subset E~|~i\in I\}
is in fact a
\displaystyle\mu
-approximation. That same lemma also shows that the inner valuation and
\displaystyle\mu
agree.
∎

12.2.
Radon measures

While the notion of a localizable measure space connects classical measure theory with the site-theoretic approach, the previous section is still mostly interesting from a theoretical point of view. In this section we deal with a class of measures that covers most cases of applications of classical measure theory in analysis and other fields, namely so-called
Radon measures
. In the case of a Radon measure
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
, the underlying set
\displaystyle X
is equipped with a Hausdorff topology, and the measure is asked to interact favourably with the topology. The standard reference for Radon measures is Schwartz
[
Sch73
]
. The basic point of this section is that in the case of a Radon measure, for the construction of the measure algebra the information of the entire
\displaystyle\sigma
-algebra is somewhat redundant. We will show that the measure algebra can be constructed purely from the datum of the lower bounded lattice of compact sets
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
, together with its valuation
\displaystyle\mu
.

Definition 12.8

(Compare
[
Fre00b
]
411H Definition, and
[
BGL22
]
Definition 6.4.)
.

Let
\displaystyle X
be a Hausdorff topological space. A
Radon measure
on
\displaystyle X
is a
\displaystyle\sigma
-algebra
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}
and a measure
\displaystyle\mu
on
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}
such that

•

\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
is a complete measure space.

•

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}
contains all open sets.

•

If
\displaystyle E\subset X
and
\displaystyle E\cap F\in\mathcal{B}
for all
\displaystyle F\in\mathcal{B}
with
\displaystyle\mu(F)<\infty
, then
\displaystyle E\in\mathcal{B}
.

•

For every
\displaystyle E\in\mathcal{B}
we have

\mu(E)=\sup\{\mu(C)~|~C\subset E,C\text{ compact}\}.

•

For every
\displaystyle x\in X
there is an open neighborhood
\displaystyle U
of
\displaystyle x
with
\displaystyle\mu(U)<\infty
.

Note that the last condition implies that
\displaystyle\mu(C)<\infty
for any compact subset
\displaystyle C
, as
\displaystyle C
can be covered by finitely many opens with finite measure. Observe that the restriction of
\displaystyle\mu
to the set of compact subsets
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
gives a regular content, and the measure on every element
\displaystyle E\in\mathcal{B}
is determined by this regular content. In this sense, for the purposes of measure theory, the information contained in a regular content and that of a Radon measure can be considered equivalent.

Radon measures are abundant throughout measure and probability theory. The basic examples are the ones already given in Example
8.16
: Euclidean space
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}
equipped with its Lebesgue measure, more generally Haar measures on locally compact Hausdorff groups, and Riemannian manifolds together with their volume measures, to name a few.

Theorem 12.9

(
[
Fre00b
]
Theorem 415A)
.

A measure space
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
with
\displaystyle\mu
a Radon measure is localizable.

Lemma 12.10
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
be a Radon measure on a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
. Let
\displaystyle E\in\mathcal{B}
. Then
\displaystyle E
is the essential supremum of the set

\{C~|~C\subset E,C\text{ compact}\}.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle E\in\mathcal{B}
. It is clear that
\displaystyle\mu(C\setminus E)=\mu(\emptyset)=0
for all compact
\displaystyle C\subset E
. Now assume that
\displaystyle F\in\mathcal{B}
is such that
\displaystyle\mu(C\setminus F)=0
for all compact
\displaystyle C\subset E
. Assume
\displaystyle\mu(E\setminus F)>0
. Since
\displaystyle\mu
is a Radon measure, there must exist a compact
\displaystyle K\subset E\setminus F\subset E
with
\displaystyle\mu(K)>0
. But then

0<\mu(K)=\mu(K\setminus F)=0,

a contradiction. Therefore, we must have
\displaystyle\mu(E\setminus F)=0
and thus
\displaystyle E
satisfies the properties of the essential supremum of
\displaystyle\{C~|~C\subset E,C\text{ compact}\}
.
∎

Theorem 12.11
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
be a Radon measure on a Hausdorff space
\displaystyle X
. Then we have a measure-preserving frame isomorphism

(\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{K}(X),\mu;\mathbf{2}),\mu_{*})\cong(\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{N},\mu)

induced by the natural homomorphism
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{N}
, where
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
is the lower bounded distributive lattice of compact subsets of
\displaystyle X
, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\subset\mathcal{B}
is the ideal of
\displaystyle\mu
-null sets.

Proof.

Theorem
12.9
guarantees that
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{N}
is a frame. Lemma
12.10
furthermore shows that the image of
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
is a basis. We are left to identify the induced Grothendieck topology on
\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(X)
with the
\displaystyle\mu
-inner topology.

It is clear that coverings by finite unions agree with finite essential suprema. Now assume
\displaystyle\{K_{i}\subset K~|~i\in I\}
is a directed
\displaystyle\mu
-approximation of compact sets. We want to show that
\displaystyle K
is the essential supremum of the sets
\displaystyle K_{i}
. It is clear that
\displaystyle\mu(K_{i}\setminus K)=\mu(\emptyset)=0.
Now assume that
\displaystyle F\in\mathcal{B}
is such that
\displaystyle\mu(K_{i}\setminus F)=0
for all
\displaystyle i\in I
. Let
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
and pick
\displaystyle i_{0}\in I
such that

\mu(K\setminus K_{i_{0}})=\mu(K)-\mu(K_{i_{0}})<\epsilon.

Then

\mu(K\setminus F)=\mu(K_{i}\setminus F)+\mu((K\setminus K_{i})\setminus F)\leq\mu(K\setminus K_{i})<\epsilon.

Since this is true for any
\displaystyle\epsilon>0
, we conclude that
\displaystyle\mu(K\setminus F)=0
. In other words, we checked that
\displaystyle K
satisfies the condition to be an essential supremum.

Conversely, if
\displaystyle K
is an essential supremum of a set of compact sets
\displaystyle\{K_{i}~|~i\in I\}
, then
\displaystyle\{K_{i}\subset K~|~i\in I\}
is a
\displaystyle\mu
-approximation by Lemma
12.3
.

Finally, we also see that the inner measure on
\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{K}(X),\mu;\mathbf{2})
agrees with
\displaystyle\mu
by definition of a Radon measure.
∎
